# Dissect — Unified Notes App for Deep Reading

A notes-centric web app for completely dissecting complex documents (research papers, financial reports, due diligence, consulting reports) with an AI assistant. Notes are the substrate; documents are inputs that attach to notebooks. Every AI feature is one operation: **Anchor → Derivation → Destination**.

---

## 1. Product Principles

1. **Notes outlive documents.** A note can cite many documents; a document can feed many notebooks. The unit of long-term value is the note, not the annotation.
2. **One primitive, many features.** Simplify, salience highlighting, analysis, and extraction-to-notes are all the same pipeline (anchor → LLM derivation → destination) with different prompt templates and destinations. Never build them as separate subsystems. Explain is one template of this pipeline. Questions about a selection go to the assistant (§7), which gathers the passages across the document that match the selection and answers from them; there is no separate Match-it tool.
3. **User approves everything.** All AI output that writes into notes lands as `pending` and requires one-keystroke accept/reject. Nothing enters notes silently.
4. **Provenance is non-negotiable.** Every note line must click back to its source anchor in the original document.
5. **The retrieval test.** A feature only writes to notes if its output is something the user will read again. Comprehension aids (laymanization, explanation) render in the reader and persist as annotations — not as notes in sections.
6. **Context conditions everything.** The reader's background, purpose, and intended application (the background in Settings; stored as `ReaderProfile`) are injected into every prompt, not scoped to one feature. Context is optional and never blocks reading or upload.

---

## 2. Tech Stack

- **Framework:** Next.js 14+ (App Router, TypeScript, server components where possible)
- **DB:** PostgreSQL + Prisma
- **AI:** The assistant — its answers (Ask, the selection chat, the tools' conversations) and its edit commands (`act`) — runs on Gemini 3.8 Flash (Google, `gemini-3.8-flash`; `GEMINI_3_8_FLASH` in `lib/derive/config.ts`; the `@ai-sdk/google` provider, `lib/gemini.ts` is the client, the key is `GEMINI_API_KEY` or the gateway's Gemini pass-through): Fast Thinking is thinking level low, Deep Thinking high. It is called as written, not as the gemini role's default, so the bimonthly model update never moves it. Gemini reads pictures, so a picture in the assistant stays on it. A deploy with no Gemini key falls back to GLM 5.3 (`FEATURE_FALLBACKS`, `lib/feature-models.ts`), so the assistant never turns off. GLM 5.3 (Z.ai, `glm-5.3`) behind the reader's tools, the assistant with Web on, Stitch's answer pass, Merge with AI, and the contents, and GLM 5.3 Flash (`glm-5.3-flash`) behind every reading but the parse and Extract — SALIENCE, EXTRACT, FIND, the glossary, the skeleton, the gist, the recommended-links scan, Stitch's route and select passes, the grammar check, and Define at low effort (a definition is one sentence, two at most, like the glossary's, and the reader waits for it with the toolbar open) — and Kimi K3 (`kimi-k3`) behind Extract (DISTILL: a question against the whole document, and the quotes copied exactly), all via the Vercel AI SDK's OpenAI-compatible client (`lib/kimi.ts`, the `@ai-sdk/moonshotai` provider), streaming responses, and all through the AI gateway (below): GLM has no direct client, and without the gateway a GLM id resolves to Kimi K3 (`resolveModelId`, `lib/models.ts`). Kimi K3 (Moonshot AI, `kimi-k3`) keeps what GLM 5.3 cannot do: it reads images — a call that carries one (Circle & ask on a figure, a page image, a picture in the assistant, a video frame) goes to `VISION_MODEL` when the feature's model is GLM (`pictureFeature`, `lib/feature-models.ts`). The assistant with Web on runs on GLM 5.3 too (`WEB_SEARCH_MODEL`), searching with Z.ai's Web Search API; the search follows the model that answers, so without the gateway, where a GLM id resolves to Kimi K3, it is Moonshot's web-search formula (§7). The model stays a per-derivation-type config constant, with a reasoning effort beside it (`DERIVATION_EFFORT` in `lib/derive/config.ts`: `high` for the reader's tools, `max` for ANALYZE; Kimi K3 and GLM 5.3 take the same three levels). The parse passes — the URL core, structure, and layout passes — run on Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic, `claude-opus-5-5`) at max effort (`PARSE_MODEL` and `PARSE_EFFORT` in `lib/derive/config.ts`): what the parse gets wrong, every later tool inherits, so the parse gets the strongest model at its most thorough, not the cheapest; "max" is the slowest effort, and the layout pass reads the page's whole HTML against the request's time budget, so a pass that outruns it is skipped and the mechanical parse stands. The passes answer with ops by block index, a reading, and the figure rules are the code's, so the parse keeps its figures under any model. The passes call whichever client their model id belongs to (`lib/parse/model.ts`: a `claude-` id through `lib/claude.ts`, any other through `lib/kimi.ts`) with the same prompts, so the model is one constant; `scripts/parse-compare.ts` parses the same pages under two models and prints what differs. The handwritten passes — Import PDF's judgment and conversion, which read page images — run on Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic, `claude-opus-5-5`, `@ai-sdk/anthropic` provider; `lib/claude.ts` is the client) at high effort (`HANDWRITTEN_MODEL`, `HANDWRITTEN_EFFORT`): what the parse gets wrong, every later tool inherits. An SVG chart — a figure whose media is inline SVG — is read by Claude Opus 5.5 wherever a model reads one, Analyze on it and the assistant acting on it (`SVG_CHART_MODEL`, `SVG_CHART_EFFORT` high, `lib/derive/svg-chart.ts`): the whole source, up to 200,000 characters, goes to the model, where GLM 5.3 read the first 12,000 as XML. Visualize (§20, Unitos Ultra) runs on Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic, `claude-opus-5-5`; `VISUALIZE_MODEL`, `VISUALIZE_EFFORT` `max`): a visualization is vector graphics written as code, which Opus 5.5 leads, and the picture has to be faithful or refused. The assistant's suggestions (§29) run on Claude Sonnet 5 (`SUGGEST_MODEL`, `SUGGEST_EFFORT`: Fast Thinking low, Deep Thinking high): it copies the words it replaces exactly and follows many rules at once at a fifth of Opus 5.5's price; the document is the cached prefix, so every window and every command on the same document reads it from the cache. The voice command (§6) runs on Claude Sonnet 5 (`claude-sonnet-5`; `VOICE_MODEL`, `VOICE_EFFORT`): it follows a multi-part spoken instruction and copies quotes exactly at a fifth of Opus 5.5's price, and the document prefix is cached between commands. The keys are `MOONSHOT_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, and `GEMINI_API_KEY`; `MOONSHOT_BASE_URL` and `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` override the endpoints. **The bimonthly model update:** the seven constants (`GLM_5_3`, `GLM_5_3_FLASH`, `KIMI_K3`, `CLAUDE_FABLE_5_1`, `CLAUDE_OPUS_5_5`, `CLAUDE_SONNET_5`, `GEMINI_FLASH` in `lib/derive/config.ts`) are the defaults of seven roles (`lib/models.ts`: glm, glmFlash, kimi, claude, opus, sonnet, gemini — the three Anthropic roles follow their own families, so the import follows Fable, Visualize follows Opus, and the voice command follows Sonnet; Z.ai publishes no model list, so the two GLM roles move only when the constant does, and the run says so on the row). On the 1st of every second month `/api/cron/models` (vercel.json, `CRON_SECRET`; the admin page's Check now runs it any time) reads each provider's published model list (`GET /v1/models` at Moonshot and Anthropic, the Gemini models list at Google), finds the newest version of the role's family at the same shape as its current id (`claude-<name>-<version>`, `kimi-k<version>`, `gemini-<version>-flash`; a -thinking, -lite, -preview, or dated variant is another product and never taken), makes one probe call on it, and writes the id to the role's `ModelChoice` row; the clients (`lib/kimi.ts`, `lib/claude.ts`, `lib/video/gemini.ts`) resolve a default id to its role's row on every call, so nothing else changes. Every outcome — no newer version, a probe that failed, a key not set — lands on the row for the admin page (`lib/model-update.ts`). **The model per feature:** every model call names its feature (`lib/feature-models.ts`: define, explain, simplify, salience, extract, distill, summarize, compare, analyze, visualize, voice, suggest, find, ask, formalize, assistant, act, web, vision, svg-chart, stitch, stitch-select, merge, gist, grammar, log, glossary, contents, skeleton, connect, parse, classify, convert — the usage record's names), and the feature's model is its constant in `lib/derive/config.ts` unless the admin set another in the gateway page's Model per function section (`/admin/gateway`, shown with or without the gateway; `FeatureModel`, one row per feature that leaves its default; `/api/admin/feature-models`). A pick is one of the chat roles' default ids — which follows the role through the model update — or any other id, called as written; the id is probed with one call before the row is written, and a pick whose key is not set, or that does not answer, is refused and the feature keeps its model. The web feature takes a GLM or Kimi id alone: the search is the model's provider's. A claude- id runs through `lib/claude.ts`, any other through `lib/kimi.ts` (`modelCall`, `lib/model-call.ts`), with the feature's effort translated to the client's levels, so a feature's model is one id whichever provider it belongs to; a call that carries an image still goes to the vision feature's model, and an SVG chart to the svg-chart feature's. The rows are read on every call and again after five minutes, like the roles' rows.
- **The AI gateway:** LiteLLM, one host in front of every AI provider (`litellm/`: `config.yaml` is the whole configuration, `Dockerfile` the image, `README.md` the deploy steps; `lib/gateway.ts` is the app's side). With `LITELLM_BASE_URL` and `LITELLM_API_KEY` set, every AI call goes to the gateway with the app key and the app reads no provider key: GLM's and Kimi's chat calls to the OpenAI-compatible route as `zai/<id>` and `moonshot/<id>` (the Z.ai entries route through LiteLLM's openai provider at Z.ai's base URL, the one route that forwards `reasoning_effort` as written, with Z.ai's prices set on the entry); Claude's to the Anthropic pass-through (`/anthropic/v1`), the raw forward, so every Anthropic field survives — the effort and the server-side refusal fallback, which the gateway's own Anthropic-format route drops; Gemini's to the Gemini pass-through (`/gemini`), the file store included (the resumable upload's second request goes to the URL Google answers, so the gateway never carries the media); Groq and OpenAI Whisper to the transcription route as `groq/<id>` and `openai/<id>`; OpenAI TTS to the speech route; DeepL, Moonshot's API root (the web-search formula, the model list), and Z.ai's API root (the Web Search API) to custom pass-throughs (`/deepl/v2/translate`, `/moonshot`, `/zai`), each taking the app key and adding the provider's own. Deepgram is the one direct call under the gateway (`DEEPGRAM_API_KEY` stays on the app's host): its request body is the media bytes, which a custom pass-through re-encodes as JSON, and the gateway's own Deepgram route answers text and word times without the speakers. The other provider keys live in the gateway host's environment. The gateway holds the model list as the two GLM entries, wildcards per provider (`moonshot/*`, `anthropic/*`, `gemini/*`), and the audio models, so the bimonthly model update needs no gateway change: it lists through the pass-throughs and probes a candidate as `moonshot/<id>`. The gateway applies the app key's limits — requests and tokens per minute, a budget and its reset period, set from the admin gateway page and held in the gateway's database — runs the router's fallbacks (`router_settings.fallbacks`: a GLM call that fails runs again on Kimi K3, a Kimi call on Claude Opus 5.5 through the gateway's translation, with `drop_params` so one provider's fields never fail the next), retries, and records spend per call. The Anthropic pass-through strips the request's `fallbacks` field — the name is one of the gateway's own router parameters — so under the gateway Anthropic's server-side refusal fallback never reaches Anthropic; `callForJson` (`lib/derive/json-call.ts`) does the same work on the app's side: a Claude answer that stops on a refusal runs once more on `CLAUDE_REFUSAL_FALLBACK` (`lib/claude.ts`), and the usage record names the model that answered. Every call carries `x-litellm-tags` (`feature:<name>`, `user:<id>`) and `x-litellm-end-user-id` (`gatewayHeaders`), so the gateway's spend logs split the way the app's usage telemetry does. `LITELLM_ADMIN_KEY` is the master key, read by the admin console alone (`lib/gateway-admin.ts`): the gateway page (`/admin/gateway`) shows readiness and version, the models with their limits and the gateway's prices, the fallbacks, the app key's spend and limits (editable), spend over 30 days by day, model, provider, function, and account (the gateway's daily activity and tag activity endpoints), and Check models, one probe per model; with no app key set it issues one (`/key/generate`, shown once). Without the gateway variables, every client calls its provider directly with its own key, as before; the two modes are one code path with the base URL, key, and model id resolved per call (`gatewayUrl`, `keyFor`, `gatewayModelId`). The usage telemetry (§7) keeps recording on the app's side in both modes: the app's page prices at list, the gateway's page at the gateway's list, and the two are checked against each other.
- **Prompt caching:** The full parsed document is the prompt prefix (Moonshot's automatic context caching: a byte-identical prefix past 256 tokens is served from cache at a tenth of the input price, with no cache markers in the request). Every selection-level derivation must reuse the cached prefix.
- **Parsing:** PDF → blocks server-side with `unpdf` (pdf.js), in reading order, no model pass; how the parse reads a page is §31. The PDF parse also keeps each block's page (`ParsedBlock.page`), where each later page begins inside a block joined across a page break (`ParsedBlock.pageStarts`), the first page's size in points, and the PDF's page labels when it names its pages otherwise than 1 to n (§30). Its style spans are bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, code, small caps (words set in a small-caps font, or lowercase letters drawn as smaller capitals), sub and sup (a run set lower or higher than its line, and smaller, outside math: H₂O, 10³, a footnote mark), a text color and a highlight color (`color:#rrggbb`, `highlight:#rrggbb`), and a face or a size where a run is set otherwise than its block (`font:`, `size:`); the block reader draws each but the face and the size, small caps in the font's small capitals. Each text block keeps the look most of its letters take (`ParsedBlock.font`: face, size, bold, italic, color) and the space the page leaves under it (`ParsedBlock.spaceAfter`), and the parse keeps the body's look and the title's look and alignment (`bodyFont`, `titleFont`, `titleAlign`): the import turns them into named styles and marks (§30). A block's inline formulas travel as `ParsedBlock.math`: the text keeps the readable characters and the span holds the TeX, so a block document shows the characters and an import the equation (§30). A footnote — the words a page sets at a column's foot, smaller than its body, under a short rule or opening with a label the body raises (`lib/parse/pdf/footnotes.ts`) — leaves the page's lines before they are segmented, so the paragraph it cuts joins across the page break, and comes back as its own block (`ParsedBlock.footnote`, its label as printed: "1", "*", "¶¶"; `<p class="footnote">`; its text opens with the label) right after the block that cites it; that block keeps the label, raised, and `ParsedBlock.footnoteRefs` points at the footnote. A table's notes (the small lines right under a table, from the first that opens with a label) and a footnote cited in a table's cell follow the table, and the table's `footnoteRefs` point at a footnote its cell cites; a footnote whose reference is not found stays after the last block that ends on its page, a heading aside, else where its page's words end. Notes set after a document's last words (endnotes: a web page printed) link only to a label raised in one place of the document. Markdown file (`.md`, `.markdown`, `.txt`) → blocks through the URL walk (`lib/parse/markdown-document.ts`): the file becomes one HTML page (remark with GFM: headings with GitHub-style ids, paragraphs, lists and task lists, tables, code fences, blockquotes, images as figures with the title or alt as caption, footnotes, raw HTML kept, front matter dropped and its title kept) and the walk reads it like a web page, no model pass; math is set aside before the markdown parse so its underscores and stars are not emphasis — `$$…$$` and `\[…\]` become EQUATION blocks, `$…$` and `\(…\)` stay as written in the text; an image with an absolute http(s) URL keeps it, an image with a relative path is its caption, a relative link is its text; the bytes are kept (`Document.fileData`), every add makes its own document as a PDF's does (§15), and a re-parse reads them again (a stored file that does not start with `%PDF-` is a Word file when its zip holds `word/document.xml`, else a Markdown file). Word file (`.docx`) → blocks from Word's own structure, no model pass (`lib/parse/docx.ts`, §30). URL ingestion: full-DOM structural parse via `jsdom` — equations keep their TeX (KaTeX/MathJax annotations, rendered with KaTeX in the reader), charts keep their inline SVG, figures keep their images and videos, lists/tables/separators keep their shape — and figures keep the page's look: the page's stylesheets load at parse (`lib/parse/figure-style.ts`), a chart svg carries the page's colors, fonts, and backdrop as inline style on its elements, and an image carries the backdrop the page drew behind it, so a dark site's white-line diagram renders on its own black instead of the reader's paper; and figures keep the page's layout: the same bake reads the page as a 1280×900 desktop browser lays it out and writes what it finds into the DOM before the walk — what that browser hides (a page's mobile tree beside its desktop tree) is pruned, centered captions stay centered, bold, italic, and monospace text keeps its style, headings keep their size, and every media element and every column of a figure row carries its width as a share of the text column, so two charts set side by side stay side by side at their size instead of stacking at full width (the layout walk: a px width sets, a percentage multiplies, padding subtracts, a grid divides by its tracks, a flex row shares the rest among its growing children; the text column is the most common width among the page's prose). The text column's width travels with the document (`Document.columnWidth`, 400–1100 px) and is the reader's article column for it, so the text runs as wide as it did on the page — the pane still caps the column, so it contracts when the notes tray opens; a figure the page sets wider than its text column (a chart at 844 px beside 680 px prose) carries the width past 100% on its `<figure>` and the reader draws it centered on the column and past its edges, as wide as the pane allows. A figure whose media is a video keeps the column: drawn past it, a video runs past its own pixels on a high-density screen and goes soft, where a chart or a photo holds up. A figure row — a flex or grid parent whose columns each hold media and a caption — is one FIGURE with one nested figure per column, its text the captions joined line by line; a single captioned figure is one figure with its caption. After the walk, a "Figure N" caption left with no figure beside it is rebuilt from the media nearest it in the page's DOM, and a caption beside a figure that has none folds into it (`lib/parse/figures.ts`). An image, video, or chart set inside a paragraph of text is a figure between the text's parts, never dropped with the paragraph's tags (an image between two runs of a `<p>`); an inline icon — narrower than a tenth of the text column — stays out. Then the media check (`checkMedia` in `lib/parse/figures.ts`) reads every image, video, iframe, and chart of the page's content against the blocks: a media element no block carries is built into a figure where the page set it — after the block holding its container's text (a list item's, a table cell's), else after the block holding the text nearest before it — and what cannot be built (a video from a host the sanitizer refuses) is named in the check; the counts travel with the document (`ParsedDocument.mediaCheck`). After the model passes, a figure the passes dropped between two blocks that survived is restored (`restoreFigures`): the passes reference blocks by index and may drop a figure by mistake; a figure dropped with the chrome around it stays out. A page whose figures its scripts draw (an svg with a viewBox and nothing inside, a canvas, a figure with no media and no words but its caption — a lazy-loading image's placeholder) first renders in a real browser where one is configured (`lib/parse/render-page.ts`; `BROWSER_WS_ENDPOINT` or `CHROMIUM_PATH`, the browser §11 uses for transcripts, shared through `lib/browser.ts`): 1280×900, media, images, and fonts blocked, scrolled through one viewport at a time so scroll-revealed figures draw, 150 s at most, and the rendered DOM parses in the static page's place. A chart the page's scripts animate settles before the parse (`lib/parse/capture-animation.ts`): the page's clock is paused and stepped — the page samples its own chart's shape every step of its clock, so a batch of steps costs two round trips to the browser, not two per step — a chart that changes nothing in 6 s is still (longer than a loop's pause, so sampling that starts inside one does not mistake the loop for stillness), a one-shot animation is drawn to its end, and a chart that loops is recorded frame by frame over one loop, encoded as a looping GIF (`lib/parse/gif.ts`, 10 frames a second, 64 colors, only the pixels that change per frame), stored as an `ImageAsset` of the document (`ImageAsset.documentId`; a re-parse or a delete removes it), and takes the svg's place as an `<img>` of the reader's own `/api/images/<id>` path Without a browser, or when the render fails, the static page stands and the figure audit (§15) reports the caption without its figure — followed by two AI passes that reference blocks by index and never write text: the core pass returns the block ranges that are the article (site navigation, footer link lists, newsletter, social, and legal chrome fall outside the ranges and are dropped; a figure with media — an image, a video, an embed, an SVG, `hasMedia` in figure-audit.ts — between the first and the last kept block is kept whatever the ranges say, and no pass's `drop` ever removes one: the figure rules are the code's, not the model's, so the parse keeps its figures under any model), then the layout pass (`lib/parse/layout.ts`) reads the page's own HTML beside what survives — as a browser lays it out: class names, inline styles, and the layout facts the bake wrote in — and says what each block is on the page: the kicker over the title, the metadata line, the contents list and its label, a heading and its level, a pull quote, a quote, a caption, the figures that share one row, the chrome to drop, a wrong block type, a fragment split mid-sentence, and the page's font; a join concatenates label-and-value blocks with one separator, a merge joins a fragment to the block above it with a space, a figure row wraps figure blocks in one figure, and nothing else changes a block's words. The layout pass does the structure pass's work (drop, retype, merge) on the page's HTML; the structure pass alone runs for a PDF with upload instructions. The layout pass's decisions land as the same layout tokens the walk writes, so the reader renders both alike. Each pass runs against the request's time budget (`modelPassDeadline` in `lib/parse/ingest.ts`: the route's limit less a margin; `/api/documents` and `/api/documents/[documentId]/reparse` allow 300 s): a pass that cannot finish in time aborts or is skipped and the mechanical parse stands, never a failed add. Every ingest stream sends an empty heartbeat line every 5 s while a pass reasons in silence (`lib/ndjson.ts`), so the connection outlives the call. `@mozilla/readability` is the fallback for pages the structural walk cannot read. Ingest streams stage progress (fetch → extract → select → structure → layout → save) to the client. The browser render is the longest stretch of the fetch stage, so it reports each phase it enters as the stage's detail line — rendering the page, opening it, scrolling through it, settling chart n of m — and the progress card keeps moving instead of standing on one line.. Every document is stamped with the parser version that produced it; a document stamped with an older version is marked in the document list and re-parses when the reader asks (Re-parse in its actions) — adding its URL again makes a separate document (§15), and a re-parse never replaces an import's edits without the reader's yes (§30). **The vision check** (`lib/parse/vision-check.ts`) is the last pass of a URL import, after the layout pass: where a browser is configured, the vision model has its key, and the blocks hold a figure, the page is pictured in the browser as a reader scrolls it — one picture per screen, `VISION_CHECK_TILES` at most — and the blocks are drawn the way the reader draws them (`proofHtml`: the reader's column, figure, figure-row, and role rules) and pictured the same way; the vision model (`VISION_CHECK_MODEL`, the model that reads images) reads both sets side by side with the block listing and answers with ops by block index, never writing text: `figure_row` for figures side by side on the page that the reader stacks, `unrow` for a row the page stacks, `drop` for a figure that appears in no picture of the page (under a ceiling of a third of the figures), `caption` for a text block that is a figure's caption or label on the page. Ops that break the rules are skipped one by one; on any failure, or past its budget (`CHECK_BUDGET_MS`, or what the import has left), the blocks stand. The progress card shows the stage (`check`), the save stage reports it (`visionCheck`), and `VISION_CHECK=off` turns it off.
- **Anchoring:** W3C Web Annotation selectors via `apache-annotator` (`@apache-annotator/dom`, `@apache-annotator/selector`).
- **Digest (Phase 6):** the assistant's stored context — one `NotebookDigest` row per corpus per user, rebuilt on read when a content fingerprint moves (§7). No embeddings: the assistant reads the corpus whole.
- **Styling:** Tailwind. Split-pane layout via CSS grid, not a heavy library.
- **Auth:** dual mode (Scalae pattern). With `SESSION_SECRET` plus Google (`GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID`, `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`), Apple (`APPLE_CLIENT_ID`, `APPLE_TEAM_ID`, `APPLE_KEY_ID`, `APPLE_PRIVATE_KEY`), or email (`RESEND_API_KEY`, `EMAIL_FROM`) credentials set, sign-in (hand-rolled OIDC code flows, database sessions in an httpOnly cookie, 30 days; accounts key on the email, so every provider lands in one account; Apple's callback is a cross-site form_post with a SameSite=None state cookie and a self-signed ES256 client secret; email sign-in stores a hashed single-use token per `EmailConfirmation` row (`purpose` "signup" | "reset"), 30-minute expiry, and creates the account only when the link is clicked; the link lands on the dashboard, with no password step and no card: the card comes after 30 minutes of active time, the billing ask (§24); a password is optional (scrypt, `User.passwordHash` "s1$salt$hash", "" = none): returning users sign in with the same email link, or with email + password at `/signin?mode=in` once Forgot password has set one — it emails a reset link to `/reset`, which sets the new password and signs every other session out) gates the app at `/signin`; corpora, profiles, and digests belong to accounts, and the first account to sign in adopts the local reader's data. The block list (`BlockedEmail`, one row per email; `lib/block.ts`): a blocked email is refused at every sign-in door (Google, Apple, email confirmation, password login, the email sign-in form, Forgot password, the test door), and blocking deletes the account's sessions and pending email links, so it is signed out at once; the row keys on the email, so an email with no account yet can be blocked, and a reset account stays blocked. The operator blocks and unblocks from the admin accounts page. Unset, the app runs as the single local reader (`user-1`), nothing gated. `/admin` keeps its own `ADMIN_PASSWORD` gate, decoupled from reader sign-in; the admin never has access to an account — no session, no impersonation, no edits — and sends notifications into accounts (§18) and nothing else. Corpus routes verify membership (owner or collaborator, §12); object routes resolve their object to its corpus or document and check the same roles. `/api/auth/test-login` is a QA door, sealed unless `TEST_LOGIN_TOKEN` is set. With sign-in on, `/signin` opens a beta notice once per tab (sessionStorage `unitos-beta-notice`): Unitos is in beta, thanks to every beta user, and every beta account gets Unitos free and unlimited for now — the notebook and the Kimi, Claude, Gemini, and Groq tokens it uses — signed by the Unitos team; Continue, Escape, or a click outside closes it. Above the notice's card a figure in the mark's outlined style — the upper half of a person, standing behind the card — bows and rises on a loop (`signin/beta-notice.tsx`, `bow-*` in globals.css; still under reduced motion). The page's hero reads "Got a ___? Put it in Unitos Notebook." — the blank rolls through a video, an audio file, an article, a research paper, a legal document, a PDF assignment, one every 2 seconds, up like a slot-machine reel (`signin/hero-reel.tsx`); set in capitals in the page's own display face, a condensed high-contrast serif (Noto Serif Display, `.font-hero`), the first line as large as the column allows and the second smaller — over one line on what Unitos is: an AI-assisted notebook you can share, every note anchored, every passage explained at your depth. Under the hero, the sign-in card under a clay glow (a halo behind it and a ring on it, breathing on one loop, `si-*` in globals.css): sign-up is the email alone and Start now — it's free, then Google and Apple; sign in (`?mode=in`) is email and password; Forgot password is the email. Beside the card, the sentence with a dashed arrow nudging at it: No billing information — Enter your email. Start your work now. — Two months of Unitos Premium, free. Beside them, the reader deck (`signin/reader-deck.tsx`): five screens of the app — Reader, Notes full page, Notes in the reader, Graph and Stitch, Collaboration — each a drawn mock of the real UI with a looping CSS demo (`signin/deck/*.tsx`, the keyframes beside each in its `.css`; a frame's cursor travels in a layer the frame's size), in a strip that snaps one screen at a time, tabs above, the screen's caption under it, and prev/next; a tab or a button eases scrollLeft itself (smooth scrollTo is unreliable on a snap container), a swipe updates the tab from the scroll position; under reduced motion every frame holds its late state. Under the fold, while the billing switch is on (or as the admin's preview), the plans story (§24, `app/plans/plans-story.tsx`) follows down a gradient from the dark ground to its cream, without the plans page's nav.
- **Language:** English and Chinese, whole-surface. Typed dictionaries in `/lib/i18n/dict` (one namespace per surface; en and zh keys enforced identical by type), `dissect-lang` cookie with Accept-Language first-visit fallback, switcher in Settings and on `/signin`. Every UI surface and API error message translates; prompts, the digest, and stored data stay English (model context and data, not UI).

---

## 3. Data Model (Prisma)

```prisma
model User {
  id         String    @id @default(cuid())
  email      String    @unique
  name       String
  picture    String    @default("")
  createdAt  DateTime  @default(now())
  lastSeenAt DateTime  @default(now())
  sessions   Session[]
}

model Session {
  token     String   @id
  userId    String
  user      User     @relation(fields: [userId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  createdAt DateTime @default(now())
  expiresAt DateTime
}

model Notebook {
  id        String    @id @default(cuid())
  userId    String    @default("user-1") // owner account; "user-1" = the local reader
  title     String
  profile   Json?     // ReaderProfile override for this notebook
  sections  Section[]
  documents NotebookDocument[]
  digest    NotebookDigest?
  createdAt DateTime  @default(now())
  updatedAt DateTime  @updatedAt
}

model NotebookDigest {
  id          String   @id @default(cuid())
  notebookId  String   @unique
  notebook    Notebook @relation(fields: [notebookId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  userId      String   // per-user store; constant in v1
  fingerprint String   // cheap aggregates over the content tables; mismatch = stale
  parts       Json     // DigestParts: documents with text and layers, notes, sections
  counts      Json     // DigestCounts: documents, notes, annotations, distillations, …
  chars       Int      // rendered size before budget cuts
  builtAt     DateTime @default(now())
}

model Section {
  id         String   @id @default(cuid())
  notebookId String
  notebook   Notebook @relation(fields: [notebookId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  title      String
  order      Int
  parentId   String?  // nesting, one level deep is enough for v1
  parent     Section? @relation("SectionNesting", fields: [parentId], references: [id])
  children   Section[] @relation("SectionNesting")
  notes      Note[]
}

model Note {
  id             String     @id @default(cuid())
  sectionId      String
  section        Section    @relation(fields: [sectionId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  content        String     // markdown
  gist           String?    // the phrase a collapsed row shows, written by AI to fit the row (§6); null until written, cleared when content changes
  status         NoteStatus @default(ACCEPTED) // manual notes are ACCEPTED on create
  derivationType DerivationType? // null = manually written
  documentId     String?    // the document the note was written in (§6): the tray's scope; null = the project's
  order          Int
  sources        Source[]
  createdAt      DateTime   @default(now())
  updatedAt      DateTime   @updatedAt
}

enum NoteStatus {
  PENDING   // AI-proposed, awaiting user decision
  ACCEPTED
  REJECTED  // keep for 7 days for undo, then hard-delete via cron
}

enum DerivationType {
  EXPLAIN
  SIMPLIFY
  SALIENCE
  EXTRACT    // stored matches of the old Match-it tool, kept on the attachment; nothing makes new ones (the assistant does that work, §7)
  SUMMARIZE  // document-level summary, one per depth
  SYNTHESIS  // notebook-scope assistant output
  DISTILL    // the reader's Extract: question → the quotes that answer it, stored on the attachment
}

model Source {
  id         String   @id @default(cuid())
  noteId     String
  note       Note     @relation(fields: [noteId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  documentId String?  // null once the document is deleted: the quote stays, orphaned (§5)
  document   Document? @relation(fields: [documentId], references: [id], onDelete: SetNull)
  // Anchor: dual strategy (see §5)
  blockId     String
  startOffset Int
  endOffset   Int
  quotedText  String
  prefix      String  // 32 chars before selection
  suffix      String  // 32 chars after selection
  orphaned    Boolean @default(false)
}

model Document {
  id        String   @id @default(cuid())
  title     String
  sourceUrl String?
  fileHash  String?  // indexed, not unique: every add is its own document; a repeat add asks first (§15)
  importRev Int?     // an import's revision as imported or last re-parsed (§30); null = not an import; richTextRev > importRev = edited
  pageLabels Json?   // a PDF import's page labels when the PDF names its pages otherwise than 1 to n (§30)
  blocks    Block[]
  notebooks NotebookDocument[]
  sources   Source[]
  glossary  Json?    // Phase 7: [{term, definition, blockIds[], lang, definitions: {en?, zh?}}]
  createdAt DateTime @default(now())
}

model NotebookDocument {
  notebookId    String
  documentId    String
  notebook      Notebook @relation(fields: [notebookId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  document      Document @relation(fields: [documentId], references: [id])
  salience      Json?    // SALIENCE layer: [{blockId, start, end}], per notebook per document
  summaries     Json?    // SUMMARIZE output: {layman?, intermediate?, professional?}
  distillations Json?    // DISTILL output: [{id, question, createdAt, quotes}], newest first
  extractions   Json?    // EXTRACT output: [{id, createdAt, origin, spans}], oldest first — the index gives the label
  folderId      String?  // the folder the document sits in within this project (§6); null = the project itself
  folder        DocumentFolder? @relation(fields: [folderId], references: [id], onDelete: SetNull)
  position      Int?     // its place in its list under Custom order (§6); null = never placed by a drag
  @@id([notebookId, documentId])
}

model DocumentFolder {
  id         String   @id @default(cuid())
  notebookId String
  notebook   Notebook @relation(fields: [notebookId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  title      String
  parentId   String?  // the folder this one sits in; null = the project itself
  parent     DocumentFolder?  @relation("FolderNesting", fields: [parentId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  children   DocumentFolder[] @relation("FolderNesting")
  documents  NotebookDocument[]
  position   Int?     // its place in its parent's list under Custom order (§6); null = never placed by a drag
  createdAt  DateTime @default(now())
}

model ReadingPosition {   // where one account left off in one document (§6)
  userId     String   // soft reference like Notebook.userId
  documentId String
  document   Document @relation(fields: [documentId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  blockId    String   // the block at the reading line
  offset     Float    // px from the reading line to the block's top; negative = the line cut into the block
  height     Float    // the block's height when read: another screen scales the offset
  at         DateTime // when the reader was there, by the reading browser's clock; only a newer save replaces the row
  @@id([userId, documentId])
}

model Block {
  id         String    @id @default(cuid())
  documentId String
  document   Document  @relation(fields: [documentId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  order      Int
  type       BlockType
  text       String    // plain text content
  html       String?   // rendered content for figures/tables
  page       Int?      // 1-based PDF page: FIGURE blocks from a PDF, PAGE blocks, converted text, every block of a PDF (the page its first words stand on)
  region     Json?     // FIGURE blocks from a PDF: the figure's region on its page (the §11 percent-coordinate shape); null = the whole page
  mediaId    String?   // an import's FIGURE row: its FigureMedia row (§30)
  cell       Json?     // a table cell's paragraph in the page editor: {table, row, column}, 1-based (§30)
}

model FigureMedia {       // an import's figure, once per document (§30); the rich text's figure object names it
  id         String   @id
  documentId String
  document   Document @relation(fields: [documentId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  html       String?  // a web figure's html; null = a PDF figure, drawn as the crop of its page and region
  caption    String
  page       Int?
  region     Json?
  createdAt  DateTime @default(now())
}

enum BlockType {
  PARAGRAPH
  HEADING
  FIGURE
  TABLE
  EQUATION
  LIST
  CODE
}

model ReaderProfile {
  id          String  @id @default(cuid())
  userId      String  @unique // constant in v1
  background  String  // "Stanford student, stochastic calc + stats + quantum"
  purpose     String  // "due diligence" | "exam prep" | "replicate results" | free text
  application String  // "investment decision for Bough Capital" etc.
}
```

Key invariant: **Note ↔ Source is one-to-many, Note ↔ Document is many-to-many through Source.** One note can cite anchors in three different documents. This is the core structural advantage over document-centric readers.

---

## 4. The Derivation Pipeline (the one primitive)

Single server route: `POST /api/derive`

```typescript
type DeriveRequest = {
  type: 'EXPLAIN' | 'SIMPLIFY' | 'SALIENCE' | 'EXTRACT' | 'DISTILL' | 'SUMMARIZE' | 'FORMALIZE' | 'ASK' | 'COMPARE' | 'ANALYZE' | 'VISUALIZE' | 'DEFINE';
  documentId?: string;         // absent for corpus DISTILL and for COMPARE
  documentIds?: [string, string]; // COMPARE only: the two documents, both attached
  notebookId: string;
  anchor?: AnchorInput;        // required for EXPLAIN/SIMPLIFY/EXTRACT/ANALYZE/VISUALIZE/DEFINE; optional focus for DISTILL
  question?: string;           // DISTILL: the question the quotes must answer; ASK: the question about the range
  video?: { startTime, endTime, region?, frame? }; // EXPLAIN on a moment (§11); ASK: the range
  depth?: 'layman' | 'intermediate' | 'professional'; // SUMMARIZE only; default layman
  format?: 'article' | 'notes'; // FORMALIZE only: the destination shape
  sectionId?: string;          // FORMALIZE notes, COMPARE: where the notes land
};
```

Flow:
1. Load document blocks (cached prompt prefix) + ReaderProfile + notebook section skeleton.
2. Select prompt template by `type` (templates in `/lib/prompts/`, one file per type).
3. Stream response. A streamed text derivation (EXPLAIN, SIMPLIFY, ANALYZE, SUMMARIZE, ASK, DEFINE, and the assistant's answer) goes through `lib/derive/text-stream.ts`: a heartbeat space every 5 s until the first text delta (Kimi K3 reasons before it writes, in silence, and an idle connection dies at proxies), then the text; a failure reports in-band, the stream ending with `STREAM_ERROR_TOKEN` and the reason — the API's error, the output budget spent before the answer, or the model declining. The client drops the heartbeat spaces and shows the reason, never an empty answer for a failed call.
4. Route output by destination:
   - `EXPLAIN` → annotation bubble in the reader rail (persisted as a Note in a hidden "Annotations" section, so it's searchable, but rendered in the rail)
   - `SIMPLIFY` → bubble beside the article, level with the selection (ephemeral, not persisted; close to dismiss)
   - `SALIENCE` → highlight layer (persisted as document-level Json, per notebook)
   - `EXTRACT` → no longer runs. Stored extractions on `NotebookDocument.extractions` still paint as a labeled highlight layer and open the match card (the origin phrase and every passage, each row jumping to its text, Delete); the assistant does the matching now (§7)
   - `DISTILL` (the reader's Extract) → distillation on `NotebookDocument.distillations`, rendered as the extract page; a quote reaches notes only through the page's "Add to notes", which lands a `Note` with `status: PENDING`
   - `SUMMARIZE` → Summary tab in the side panel (persisted on `NotebookDocument.summaries`, one summary per depth; Regenerate overwrites)
   - `FORMALIZE` → the transcript rewritten (§11). format `article`: `{title, markdown}` on `NotebookDocument.formalized`, shown under the player as the view bar's article view; Regenerate overwrites. format `notes`: one `PENDING` note per topic, each with a time source resolved from the topic's transcript blocks
   - `ASK` → the answer to a question about a time range of a video or audio document (§11), streamed as text into the pane's Ask card; persists nothing. "Add to notes" lands it as a `PENDING` note with a time source for the range (`/api/notes`, origin `ask`)
   - `COMPARE` → two documents read whole: one `PENDING` note of agreements, disagreements, and what only one covers, with a source per cited span on both documents. No menu entry: `/api/derive` with type `COMPARE` and two document ids runs it
   - `ANALYZE` → a FIGURE or TABLE block read by the vision model — an SVG chart by Claude Opus 5.5, which reads the source whole (`SVG_CHART_MODEL`, `lib/derive/svg-chart.ts`) — streamed into the card beside the block — the same card as EXPLAIN, titled Analysis — and persisted in the hidden Annotations section like EXPLAIN (the figure toolbar's Analyze figure; a table is text, §6, and has no Analyze). Never a note.
   - `VISUALIZE` (§20, Unitos Ultra) → the selection as a picture, into the same card as EXPLAIN, titled Visualization, and persisted in the hidden Annotations section like EXPLAIN: an SVG `ImageAsset` and one annotation whose markdown is the image and its caption. When the model is not certain a picture carries the passage's core idea, the run declines with the reason and persists nothing.
   - `DEFINE` → the meaning of one word in its sentence (§6), shown in the toolbar under the Define row: the word and Defining… with Stop while it is written, then the definition whole, in one frame, so the rows under it move once. Persists nothing, so a viewer may run it, like FIND and ASK. The route refuses a selection that is not one word (`definable`, `lib/define.ts`). The prompt (`lib/prompts/define.ts`) reads the sentence the word sits in: the document's own definition when it has one, an acronym spelled out, a symbol by what it stands for, a name by its role in the document; one sentence in plain words, and a second only when the everyday meaning differs. It carries the glossary's rule (one sentence, two at most) in place of `STYLE_RULE`. A key term of one word shows the glossary's definition (Phase 7) with no call; a longer key term has no Define, since its hover shows the definition. A definition read once stays for the session.
   - `suggest` (not a DerivationType; `lib/derive/suggest.ts` `runSuggest`, `lib/prompts/suggest.ts`) → the assistant's suggestions in a rich text (§29): ops keyed to the paragraph index, validated, resolved, and landed by the page editor as suggestions authored by the assistant. The model returns `{summary, ops}`, at most 80 ops (`SUGGEST_MAX_OPS`), each with `why`: replace_words {blockId, find, text, format?} (format: the new words' bold, italic, underline, or strikethrough; they then replace the words whole), rewrite_block {blockId, text}, replace_blocks {blockIds, markdown}, insert_blocks {afterBlockId|null, markdown}, remove_blocks {blockIds}, set_style {blockId, style}, set_alignment {blockId, alignment}, set_spacing {blockId, line?, before?, after?} (line spacing as a multiple, the space before and after in points), set_indent {blockId, left?, right?, firstLine?} (in points; 0 takes one off; a list line nests by replace_blocks instead), format_words {blockId, find, format, value?} (bold, italic, underline, strikethrough; link with the address, "" takes the link off; color and highlight_color #rrggbb; font; size in points), insert_row {blockId, where: above|below, cells}, remove_row {blockId}, move_row {blockId, toBlockId, where}, insert_column {blockId, where: left|right, cells}, remove_column {blockId}, move_column {blockId, toBlockId, where} (each names a table cell's row or column by the cell's blockId; cells are the new row's or column's words in order), insert_footnote {blockId, find, text} (a footnote whose number goes right after `find`). An equation's words are its TeX and a figure's its caption: rewrite_block changes them, and replace_words changes an inline equation written as $TeX$ in its block's words; new TeX must render in KaTeX (`texError`, `lib/katex.ts`), else the op is skipped as "tex". An image has no caption of its own: rewrite_block on one becomes new blocks after it, the caption in italics on a line of its own. Markdown takes an image as `![what it shows](address)` on a line of its own when the address is a web address (http or https), the way Insert image By URL inserts one; any other image is its words. The answer is read op by op: an op that does not read is skipped with its why ("Skipped: the change could not be read."), a why past 240 characters and a summary past 400 are cut, and ops past 80 are left with the note that the command covers too much for one run. A window holds at most 8,000 characters and 60 rows (`SUGGEST_WINDOW_ROWS`): short lines and empty paragraphs take an op each. `resolveOps` (`lib/docs/suggest-ops.ts`) checks each op against the paragraph index and the stored rich text; an op that breaks a rule is skipped with its reason, the rest stand, and an op that changes nothing is dropped: the block exists and is in the scope; text blocks only, but for a figure's caption and an equation's TeX (a figure or an equation is never removed or replaced); in a table cell or a footnote only the words change, and a table's rows and columns change by the row and column ops, a row or a column moving only within its table; code and a footnote take no new footnote; `find` resolves by the quote ladder and occurs once, and the stored words become `find` with their offsets; on selected words, a word op falls inside the selection widened to whole words, and a block op needs the whole block selected; `rewrite_block` is one paragraph; replace_blocks and remove_blocks take consecutive blocks of one container; new blocks at the document's start belong to the window that starts it; `set_style` records the style it replaces; a link, a color, a font, or a size the page cannot set is skipped; two ops on the same or touching words, a word op and a block op on one block, two style changes or two alignments of one block, and new blocks beside a block op conflict, and the first stands, while a block's style or alignment and words changed in it stand together; Markdown loses HTML, and images but one on a line of its own from a web address, and keeps 200 lines; a command adds at most 100,000 characters (`SUGGEST_MAX_NEW_CHARS`). A skip is a line in `warnings` ("Skipped: … (why)"), never a write. The revise action (§7) runs the same pipeline on a document without rich text (`target: "plan"`): the prompt lists the ops a document of blocks takes, and its ops become the plan card's block actions (`lib/assistant/revise.ts`).

**Regenerate.** Every AI tool's output can be run again. The reader's cards carry the circular arrow (`RegenerateIcon`), the pages and panels the word Regenerate; the tip says what runs and what it replaces. The run repeats the same derivation on the same input — the card's own selection, the extraction's own question, the answer's own question and range — never on whatever the input box holds now, so an edited box changes nothing. The new output replaces the old, and only once it is stored: the run persists its own result first and the one it replaced goes then, so a failed run, a stopped one, or a Visualize that declines never loses what stands. Simplify, Analyze, Visualize, and the page Ask replace their annotation (`DELETE /api/notes/<id>`, no toast — the new annotation is the notice); Extract replaces its distillation, Summarize its summary at that depth, Formalize its article. Ask and Find persist nothing and simply run again. A tool conversation is not regenerated: a turn is sent, never replaced. Regenerate on an Explain, Simplify, Analyze, or Visualize card keeps the card's conversation: the run sends `conversationOf` (the card's annotation) to `/api/derive`, the new annotation is stored with the old one's turns, and only then does the old one go, kept whole in History, where Restore puts it back (§12). A run that fails, is stopped, or declines leaves the card on the output and the annotation that stand, with the reason under the output, so the next Regenerate still replaces that annotation and never adds a second one beside it.

Prompt templates always receive: reader context, document title, section skeleton, and the anchored text with surrounding context (±2 blocks). Every reader-facing template ends with the one style rule (`STYLE_RULE` in `lib/prompts/types.ts`, carried by every assistant surface — the panel, the selection chat and tool conversations, Ask on a recording, Stitch's reply, Explain, Analyze, Summarize: be straightforward, short, and concise; answer the question directly, and first; short sentences, plain words, one point per sentence; no idioms, no complex phrases, no preamble, no filler, no closing summary; technical language only where the material uses it; an example or a quote only where the answer needs one) and a word cap: Explain 150, Ask 150, Analyze 220, the assistant's answer 250 unless the question needs more, summaries 180 (layman), 400 (professional). Captions, compare points, find explanations, glossary definitions, and Define's definitions are one sentence, two at most.

**DISTILL output contract:** model returns JSON `{quotes: [{blockId, start, end, quote, caption}]}` — the verbatim spans across the whole document that answer the question, each copied out as `quote` and captioned with exactly how it answers the question in the document's context (or which facet of the question it answers, when it answers one facet only). `quote` is what the span resolves on: offsets are what a model gets wrong — counted from the document, from the rendered `[block <id>]` line, or against a mis-copied block id — and the copied words are what it gets right, so a span takes the anchor ladder of §5 (`lib/derive/json.ts`): the offsets when they slice the quote, else the quote inside the named block, else the quote anywhere in the document, else the offsets alone. Only a quote the document does not carry at all drops. The prompt (`lib/prompts/distill.ts`) reads the question first, then scans with it in mind; a span is cut to what answers — a phrase, a sentence, or a whole paragraph — and every quote answers in a different way: a quote that repeats a point another quote already makes is dropped, so the page never over-quotes. Validate strictly; resolve every span against the real block text and drop what does not resolve; on parse failure, retry once with the error appended, then surface failure to user. Never write malformed output to DB. Stored quotes heal at render like salience and orphan visibly (§5). The HTTP response streams heartbeat bytes while the model works and ends with the distillation JSON (or the in-band error token), so the connection survives a minutes-long scan.

**COMPARE output contract:** both documents ride as one cacheable system message under their ids (the corpus DISTILL rendering, 220k characters per document, cut with a marker past that). The model returns JSON `{agreements, disagreements, onlyFirst, onlySecond}`, each a list of `{point, spans: [{blockId, start, end}]}`; an agreement or disagreement cites one span per document. Validate strictly; resolve every span against the real block text and drop what does not resolve; a point with no resolvable span still stands as text. The note (`lib/derive/analysis.ts`) lists the points under Agree, Disagree, Only in <first>, Only in <second>; its sources are the distinct resolved spans, at most 24. No point at all fails the run. Runs behind the heartbeat stream.

**ANALYZE output contract:** the block's visual attaches as it does for EXPLAIN (`lib/derive/figure.ts`: the image, the SVG source, or the PDF page region); a TABLE block attaches its markup, plus its page region when the PDF has one. The model is the one that reads visuals with the least invention — `DERIVATION_MODEL.ANALYZE`, the most capable model, the same one upload and parse trust. It sees the corpus section EXPLAIN sees (related passages from the other documents, the reader's notes and annotations) and streams markdown in exactly three sections, always in this order, each under its bold label (`ANALYSIS_SECTIONS` in `lib/prompts/analyze.ts`, per language): **Insights** — one or two sentences on what the visual is there to show, then 2 to 5 patterns read from the data and interpreted against the document (a trend, a break, an outlier, a gap between groups, a comparison the argument rests on, what the visual shows that the text does not say); **Quantitative** — the numbers behind each pattern, as printed, never rounded, ≈ before a value estimated off an axis or a bar, "(text)" after a number taken from the document instead of the visual; **Linking to context** — where the visual contradicts, weakens, or complicates a claim in the document, cited as `[block <id>]`, and where it connects to the project's other documents and notes; one line when there is neither. Under 220 words. The stream ends with `STREAM_NOTE_TOKEN` + the annotation's id, like EXPLAIN; the annotation is anchored to the whole caption or the table selection, reopens from the block's side label, and lists under Analyses in the Annotations tab.

**VISUALIZE output contract:** see §20. Two calls: the draw, then the check that reads the finished picture back.

**EXTRACT** no longer runs: the assistant's matches replaced it (§7). Stored extractions stay per notebook per document, oldest first — the index gives the label (M1, M2, …); spans heal at render like salience; an unresolvable span stays stored but unpainted; Delete removes one, with no ask and the Undo pill (below). (The v1 EXTRACT selection-to-note flow lives on as DISTILL's "Add to notes".)

---

## 5. Anchoring (make-or-break)

Anchors must survive re-parses and reflows. Dual strategy:

1. **Primary:** `blockId + startOffset + endOffset` against block plain text.
2. **Fallback:** `TextQuoteSelector` — `quotedText` + 32-char `prefix`/`suffix`. On load, if block resolution fails (block deleted or text changed), fuzzy-match the quote across the document via `@apache-annotator`.
3. **Never silently drop.** Unresolvable → set `orphaned: true`, render the note with a broken-link indicator and the quoted text preserved. Deleting a document is never refused for its notes: its annotations (the notes of a project's hidden Annotations section whose every source quotes it) go with it, and every other source that quotes it is orphaned and loses the document (`Source.documentId` null); the note keeps its words and its quotes, and a quote's chip reads "Deleted document" and jumps nowhere. An assistant conversation about one of its passages stays in the history without its anchor.
4. **The quote never changes.** `quotedText` is the words as quoted when the source was made, and a block edit never rewrites it: a note's quote and an annotation's words stay what the reader quoted. An edit inside the anchored words remaps the offsets (`lib/anchors/remap.ts`) and writes the words the anchor covers now to `Source.anchoredText` (null while they are still the quote); the ladder re-finds by `anchoredText ?? quotedText`, so the mark follows the edit while the quote stands.

DOM ranges are never the source of truth. Convert selection → block-relative offsets at capture time using data attributes (`data-block-id`) on rendered blocks.

**In a table** the block text separates cells with a tab and rows with a newline. A table whose html carries the separators as invisible gaps (`.cell-gap`) is read as it is; a table whose html has none (a web page's table) gets them from the reader's walk (`lib/anchors/dom.ts`): a tab between two cells, a blank cell for each extra column of a colspan, a rowspan's words again in each row under it, a newline between two rows. So a selection across two cells captures "Pages\t2", and the table's marks (`table-marks.ts`) paint on the cells' words — on the words alone where a cell keeps blank text the block text does not.

**In the page editor** (§29, §30) the blocks are the paragraph index: a table cell's paragraph is its own block, a figure object is one block anchored whole (its words are its caption), and a page start adds no words. A quote that is a FIGURE block's whole caption re-finds FIGURE blocks first, so a figure's anchor never lands on a paragraph that names the figure. On a document with rich text, an anchor that covered its whole block and lost it waits for the save, which finds it again when a block with exactly its words comes back.

**Passages.** A selection that crosses blocks is one passage (`lib/anchors/passage.ts`): the reader captures one segment per block it touches, in reading order — the first block's span from the selection's start, whole blocks between, the last block's span to the selection's end — and sends the first segment as `anchor` with every segment as `segments`. Equations and pages take no span and are left out (the popover says so). Every tool works on the whole passage: the routes (`/api/derive`, `/api/annotations`, `/api/notes`, `/api/assistant/act`) resolve each segment through the ladder on its own, the anchored text is the segments' quotes one paragraph each, and the note gets one source per segment, so its marks cover the whole selection and each mark resolves, orphans, and jumps on its own. Simplify numbers the passage's sentences on through the blocks — a block boundary is always a sentence boundary (`lib/sentences.ts`) — so a pressed rewritten sentence mirrors its source sentences in whichever blocks hold them.

---

## 6. Layout & UX

**Split view, both panes persistent:**
- Reader views: Normal shows one document; Side by Side and Top and Bottom show two panes, each with the full tool set (`?view=side|stack&doc2=`). The bar between the two panes drags to change how they share the reader — like the notes tray's bar: double-click resets to half, arrow keys nudge, and the split is remembered per browser, one per view kind (`unitos-pane-split:<view>`, 0.2–0.8).
- **Left:** document reader. Blocks rendered from DB, selection popover on highlight. **One toolbar per content kind** (`TOOLBARS` in reader-interactions.tsx): the popover shows the tools of the kind under the selection and nothing else, and a tool missing from a kind's list is never offered there. The first tool after the assistant is the kind's lead tool and reads as recommended. **Define** comes before the assistant on a selection of one whole word in one block (`offersDefine`; `definableSelection` in `lib/define.ts`: the selection's edges are word boundaries in the block text, so never a piece of a word like "re" of "readers"; `definable`: no space, tab, or newline (so never words across two table cells), comma, comma, semicolon, colon, question or exclamation mark, ellipsis, or dash inside — a hyphen, an apostrophe, a slash, and an abbreviation's dots stay inside one word, and quotes and punctuation around the word do not count; 64 characters at most; never Chinese or Japanese text, which puts no space between words, whatever the interface language), in every document the reader draws — an article, a PDF, a transcript, a formalized article, slides, a sheet, a converted handwritten page, a blank document in the page editor (§29), a core in the collapsed view: the first row of the toolbox, right under the highlight colors (on a coarse pointer, where the colors are the toolbox's first row, the row after them). A press opens the definition under the row and widens the toolbox, like the assistant's box: the word in bold, bare of the punctuation around it (`defineWord`: "earned;" is "earned", and both are one word for the glossary and the session's cache), then its meaning in its sentence (DEFINE, §4), with a Stop while it runs and the rating when it lands; a second press folds it away. The call stops when the toolbar closes. It never shows on the hold-and-circle gesture, whose anchor is the whole block. **With Jev configured** (`TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, `lib/jev.ts`) the text and equation toolbars predict their lead instead: when the popover opens on a selection, `POST /api/jev/lead-tool` `{notebookId, kind, blockType, text, tools}` (viewer) asks Jev one choice over the kind's tools — the state is the selection (its text to 600 characters, its word count, its block type) and the reader's own toolbar history (tool uses in the last 90 days, the last eight tools) — and the tool it picks at confidence 0.5 or above reads as recommended: its row takes the lead look (Define, Simplify, Visualize, Comment, Link), or its row draws a clay ring (the highlight colors, Add to notes, Read aloud). The lead look is a tint, with no word on the row; the row's tooltip says Recommended. The rows never move — an answer that lands late would move a button under the pointer — and the assistant stays first after Define. The tools sent are the ones the popover shows: Define only on one word. A key term's toolbar and a figure's keep their fixed lead. Every prediction lands as one `ClickEvent` (`lead-predicted:<tool>`, ai-toolbar) beside the click it tried to predict, so the hit rate reads from the click log. With no key, or no answer at 0.5, the kind's first tool after the assistant leads: Explain on text and equations, Analyze on a figure.

  | Kind | Blocks | Toolbar |
  |---|---|---|
  | Text | PARAGRAPH, HEADING, LIST, CODE, TABLE | Define (one word), Assistant, Simplify, Visualize, Comment, Link across texts, highlight colors, Add to notes, Read aloud |
  | Figure | FIGURE | Figure tools: Define (one word of the caption), Assistant, **Analyze figure**, Comment, Link across texts, highlight colors, Add to notes |
  | Equation | EQUATION | Equation tools: Assistant, Visualize, Comment, Link across texts, highlight colors, Add to notes |

  Analyze (§4) exists only on the figure toolbar; Simplify and Read aloud only on text. A table is text: its cells are rendered text, so a selection inside it is a selection like any other — the text toolbar, the marks painted on the passage (`table-marks.ts`, since the table's html is one string and cannot carry React marks) — and never the figure ring or the hold-and-circle gesture. Explain is on every kind: the selection explained in plain words, tuned to the reader's background, in the card beside the article; on a figure it deciphers the visual. A question about the selection goes to the Assistant, the first tool of every kind after Define (§7). A new kind of content gets a new row here and a new list in `TOOLBARS`, never a condition on a button; Define's one condition reads the selection's length, not its kind.
- **Stop on every long run.** A run the reader starts and waits on can be stopped while it runs, where it runs: the button that started it reads what runs with a Stop pill (`StopPill`, `components/thinking.tsx`), and a press ends the request and the model call (the route passes `req.signal` on). The service worker never fetches a call itself while the browser is online (§17): a call it fetched would not end when the page stops it, and the server would finish the run. Collapse (§28), Generate contents and Generate chapters (§26), Translate (§19), and Regenerate article (§11) store nothing: the article, its contents, and its translation stay as they were. Detect speakers (§11) saves nothing; its Gemini call runs to its end on the server and its answer is dropped. Recommend links (§13) stops at once: the links it proposed for the documents it already scanned stay, and the run still counts this month, since it was recorded before the scan started; closing the graph lets the scan finish. Merge with AI has Stop on the card's Merging line: nothing merges, and the notes come back; the route checks the stop before it would fall back to Join text. The Summary card keeps Stop in its header for the whole stream, and a stopped summary leaves the stored one. Leaving the document stops its runs the same way, with no message. Imports — adding a document, Re-parse, Transcribe again, the handwritten conversion — save their progress on the server as they go and have no Stop yet.
- **Layout tokens.** A text block's html may open with class tokens — `<p class="kicker center">`, `<h2 class="center">`, `<ol class="contents">` — and the reader lays the block out by them (`layoutTokens`, block-view.tsx): `center`, `right`, and `justify` align the text; `kicker` and `meta` are small mono uppercase, muted and letter-spaced; `label` is the same with a hairline rule under it; `contents` is the contents list — no list padding, one entry per 32px line, a hairline under each line drawn by a repeating gradient, so no text node joins the block; `display` is a standalone statement at 28px; `quote` a blockquote paragraph with a left rule; `caption` a caption standing alone at 13px, muted; `footnote` a footnote at 13px under the block that cites it. One token decides a block's look; `center`, `right`, and `justify` only align. The masthead rule: when a centered kicker or meta line opens the document, or its first heading is centered, the title renders centered, large and light (40px, weight 400, `.reader-masthead-title`), the kicker above it and the meta line below. The kicker is the document's first block, pulled out of the flow and still rendered through `BlockView`, so its `data-block-id` and its marks work.
- **Figure rows.** A figure's html is one `<figure>`, and its children lay out as a wrapping row centered in the column (`.reader-figure > figure`, flex): a caption takes the full width, a nested `<figure style="width:33%">` is one captioned column stacking its media over its caption, and media keep an inline width from the page (`style="width:48%"`, a percent of the article column) — two charts share a row at the page's proportions instead of each filling the column. No figure rule is `!important`, so the inline width always wins. Media in a row sit 2% apart, so their widths must sum to 100% less the gaps (two at 48%) or the row wraps; a column carries its gap inside its own width as padding, so three columns at 33% fill one row.
- **Fonts.** `Document.font` is null (Figtree), `serif`, `mono`, or `sans`: the parser stores `sans` for a page set in a sans face, and the font picker offers it. The article carries `data-font`; for `sans` the headings and the title leave the display face for the body's sans (weight 600, `letter-spacing: -0.01em`; the masthead title stays 400). Heading sizes never change with the font.
- **The header, the rail, the bottom bar.** The header: back, the project title (sm and up; below sm the document pill takes its room, and Rename stays on the dashboard card's menu), the document pill, +, then Share, History, Save for offline (an icon; its place stands empty until the browser's copy is known, so History does not move while a document loads), and the guide (md and up). The rail (md and up): fold, Assistant, Graph, Notes, Annotations, Extract (the glyph the article's Extract button carries), Reader view (the views). Below md the rail is the bottom bar: Assistant, Notes, Annotations, Extract, More; More is a menu of the reader views, Graph, History, Save for offline, the guide, and Feedback. A bar button reads pressed only while its tab is open (below md, while the sheet shows it). The Notes button carries the count of pending notes; with notes pending and the tray not on notes, a press opens the tray on notes with the first pending note in view and flashed (the header's pending count did this until 2026-10-07); pressed by a key, it puts the focus on that note's Accept.
- **Right:** docked notes drawer showing the section skeleton of the current notebook. Pending notes render with amber left-border + Accept (`Enter`) / Reject (`Backspace`) / Edit (`e`). Accepting must be exactly one keystroke when a pending note is focused.
- **Reader views** (`reader-panes.tsx`; on md and up the rail's last button, under Extract, its menu opening to the rail's left; below md the first rows of the bottom bar's More menu; so it never stands on the article's lines; it is drawn only once its place is found, so it never jumps there while a document loads): Normal shows one document; Side by Side and Top and Bottom show two panes, each with the full tool set, the view and the second document carried in the URL (`?view=side|stack&doc2=`); a fresh open is Normal. A phone offers Normal and Top and Bottom only: two columns side by side would be too narrow to read, so `?view=side` on a phone opens as Top and Bottom. A split view is its own layout, so two panes never crowd each other:
  - Each pane has a **pane header** — one row at its top, above the scroller, never over the text: the pane's document (a select; choosing changes the URL), the article menu (Contents, §26), Collapse (§28), and Extract. In Normal view the article menu floats at the top left of the pane and stays there as the article scrolls; in a split view it lives in the header, its list dropping below the header.
  - Tool cards stay **collapsed** in a split pane: a stored explanation, simplified rewrite, assistant conversation, or comment shows as its highlight and the tool's symbol at the end of the text, and opens only when the reader clicks the highlight or the symbol; the card then opens under the paragraph at the article's width, as in a narrow reader, and the text under the paragraph moves down to make its room. New output still opens as it streams.
  - With the tray folded the two panes fill the browser exactly. Opening a tab (the rail, a jump to a note or an annotation) does not shrink the panes: the reader keeps its width and the tray becomes a screen past its right edge, in a **strip** that scrolls sideways and snaps to one of two rests — the documents, or the documents' right part with the tray. Opening a tab scrolls to the tray; an edge button at either side (one at a time, on the side with a screen past it) and a sideways scroll move between the two; folding the tray scrolls back to the documents first, then the column closes. In Top and Bottom the pane header and the article column center in the part of the pane the strip leaves visible (`--strip-cut` on the strip, `--reader-cut` per pane), so no line starts under the edge; a Side by Side pane is narrower than the column, so its first pane peeks from under the edge instead. Below md the tray stays a bottom sheet and there is no strip. The sheet stands under the reader, above the bottom bar, so the reader ends where the sheet starts and every line of the document scrolls into view above it. It takes 60% of the room between the header and the bottom bar, at least 400 px, and never so much that less than 150 px of the page shows; its content scrolls inside it, and the assistant's input and Send stay at its foot. A jump to a note or an annotation, a press on a note's mark, and Show all comments open the sheet on their tab, as the bottom bar's buttons do. Add to notes leaves the sheet as it was: closed, the bar's Notes button blooms once, so the reader keeps reading; open on notes, the sheet shows the new note. The workspace grid is one column that can never grow past the browser (`minmax(0, 1fr)`): a pane's widest line stays inside its pane instead of pushing the rail and the header off screen.
- The notes tray holds the open document's notes alone. `Note.documentId` is the document a note was written in: the open document when the note is typed in the tray's composer, spoken in it, or made from a passage, a video range, or an annotation (`POST /api/notes` takes `documentId` and derives it from a source when one is sent); the notes full page's composer and a note citing nothing leave it null, the project's. A note is the open document's when it was written in it or quotes it — a source of the note is in it (`noteInDocument`, `scopeSections` in `use-outline.ts`): the tray's sections are the project's, every one kept so a note can be added to any, with the document's notes under them; its pending queue is the document's, and a line under the queue counts the pending notes of other documents and of the project, which the notes full page holds. The notes full page is the whole project. **Document columns**, the last row of its view menu (the Group by menu; the menu's button carries the New glow until it is pressed), opens the project's notes as a grid over the page (`components/outline/document-columns.tsx`): one column per document that has notes, in attach order, then Project for the notes that belong to no document; one row per section, a child section under its parent; a note under the document it was written in, else the document its first source is in. The header row and the section column stay in view while the grid scrolls; the cards are the page's, so a note edits, pins, and selects there, Compare in the selection bar opens the compare view over the grid, and Esc or Notes closes it.
- **The note's assistant** (`components/outline/note-assistant.tsx`, `POST /api/notes/[noteId]/assistant`, `lib/prompts/note-assistant.ts`): an open note — its editing mode, in the tray, on the notes full page, and in the floating card — has the assistant docked at its bottom, the way Gemini sits at the bottom of a Google Doc: a sparkle, the Web toggle, and ✕ at its head, the conversation above, and one box ("Describe any changes you want to make…") with Send. Enter sends; the words in the box are kept in the browser until the server answers. A message is a question about the note or a change to make to it. One model call (`NOTE_ASSISTANT_MODEL`, Claude Sonnet 5, at the thinking's effort; with the Web toggle on, the web feature's model and its search, §7) reads the note as the editor holds it, its sources, the document it belongs to whole (its own, else its first source's, else the one open in the reader), the reader's other notes, and the turns so far, and answers `{reply, content}`: the reply, and when the message asks for a change the whole note as it should read, every line the message does not touch word for word. The route changes nothing. Two checks run on the change: a `> ` quote whose words neither the note, its sources, nor the document hold is taken out and named ("Taken out: a quote the document does not hold"), and with the web off a number or a quotation that nothing the model read holds is named (`lib/docs/grounding.ts`). The panel shows the change as the note will read, with Accept and Reject, and warns when the change takes out quotes the note had (their sources go with them on save, as with any edit). Accept puts the change into the editor's draft, which saves like any edit; Undo puts the draft back; Cancel on the note restores what it said before the editor opened. ✕ folds the panel to an Assistant chip, remembered in the browser. The panel starts as the chip, so the open note keeps its room; a press opens it (remembered too), and a note with words waiting in the box opens with the panel open. The hint under its head shows while the box is empty and has the caret.
- Notes move by drag in the tray and on the notes full page, and the drag is a hold (`lib/hold-drag.ts`, `HoldSensor` in hold-sensor.tsx): a note has two modes, draggable and editing.
