- Closing layers: Escape closes one layer at a time, the newest first — the toolbar, a pending link, the stacked-annotations chooser, a card, and the page's menus: the document list, the bottom bar's More menu, Contents (one stack for the page, `lib/escape-layers.ts`) — and stops no run; Stop stops a run, and ✕ on a running card stops its run with it. The keyboard: a mark that opens something takes the focus at its first words when the keys reach it (its tip names it: View the annotation, View the note; the chips after the words stay for the pointer and leave the Tab order), and Enter opens what a click opens. Space is never a mark's: it scrolls the page. A press of the pointer on a mark leaves the focus on the page, so the keys after a click read on. A card or chooser opened from the keys takes the focus — its field, else its first control — and a layer the keys opened gives the focus back to what opened it when it closes: the mark or chip, the page editor's words, else a mark in the card's paragraph. A layer the pointer opened gives the focus to the page when it closes — the page editor's words, else nothing — and draws no ring. While the toolbox is open and the focus is on the page, Tab goes to the toolbox's first row, before the marks and chips after the words, in the block reader and in the page editor in Viewing; Shift+Tab on its first row gives the focus back to the words, the selection kept. In the page editor in Editing and Suggesting, Tab is the text's — it indents, nests a list line, goes to the next cell — with or without a selection. Alt+F10 or Shift+F10 go to the toolbox's first row in every mode and every reader while it is open. Ctrl/Cmd+A with the focus in the block reader's article (a mark the keys reached) selects the article and opens the toolbox, as it does after a press there; Escape closes it and clears the selection. A switch of the page editor's mode keeps the toolbox on its selection, with the new mode's rows. The toolbox's rows are in the order they are drawn, so Tab walks them top to bottom. A click on the pane outside every card (not on a mark, a control, or a core) closes the cards with nothing left to do: no run in flight and no typed words in their box. The click that ends a drag (a card moved by its header, an annotation carried from its card) is not such a click. Words typed in a card's box and not sent stay with its annotation for the session: the card reopened from its mark shows them. A width change re-docks the cards and re-places the toolbar on its words.
- Every run has its own card: a second run of Explain, Analyze, Visualize, or Simplify on other words takes the card, and the first run is not stopped: it lands its annotation and its mark, and a toast (Explanation ready, Visualization ready, …) carries Show, which opens its card beside its words.
- Words under more than one note or annotation open a small chooser at the click (a figure's label too): each annotation's kind, in its kind color, and what it holds — a comment's words, a tool's answer, a highlight's hue and its comment — and each note as Note, in clay, with its title or first words; the one picked opens, an annotation's card or the note in the tray. The chooser lists them innermost first, and equal spans in the order they were made (sources resolve by id), the same on every load; an annotation or a note whose words another mark paints is still found by its id (`data-source-ids` on the mark). Where a plain note and a highlight or a comment share words, the mark paints the highlight's or the comment's color, never the note's default clay (`markStack`, table-marks.ts). A new selection's toolbar takes the place of the chooser and of an on-mark card with no typed words, so two boxes never stand over the words — on a touch screen too, where the hold that selects words presses nothing. A drag that starts on a link's words selects them, as a drag inside any mark does.
- A note takes drops (`lib/card-drag.ts`): the floating card over the article, and every accepted note card of the tray, at rest or open in its editor — while editing, what lands goes into the draft, saved with it. What can be dragged onto one: a note dragged out of the notes tray, an annotation dragged by its grip in the Annotations tab, an annotation carried from the reader's own card over the article — the explanation, the simplification, the analysis, the visualization, the assistant's card, a comment, a highlight, each once its annotation is persisted — by a hold on the card's head row, the row with its kind (`HOLD_MS`, as a note's header row in its editing mode: on a card that its header moves, a drag moves the card and a hold lifts the annotation; `dragCard`, `data-hold-head`), or by a hold on that card's blank space, off its controls; the page editor's comment card keeps the grip (`components/outline/annotation-grip.tsx`) (`watchHold` with `pull: false`; `pointsAtText` in `lib/hold-drag.ts`: a press on the card's text, where the pointer shows the I-beam, selects the text and never lifts, hold or pull; a hold on blank space lifts the annotation), and a highlight held in the reader's text (a quote, above). So an annotation goes into a note from the tab it is listed in or from the card being read, and lands in the note that floats or in a note of the tray. A grip shows, and a card's head lifts, only while at least one note can take a drop (`useCardDropOpen` counts the live targets) — with none the gesture goes nowhere. The drop target under the pointer is the one the reader can see and press (`document.elementsFromPoint`), so a card scrolled out of its panel never takes a drop and the floating card wins over what it covers. The dragged card follows the pointer as a ghost carrying its gist; the card under it rings sage and one line says what the drop does, and the release runs it. There is nothing to press and nothing to hold: this drag lands on a card outside its own list, so the drop is the whole gesture — the hold ring is the board drag's, for two notes in one list. A note dropped in is merged: its anchors and replies move and the note is gone. An annotation dropped in lands as an **annotation reference** (`lib/annotation-reference.ts`), three things in this order: the words the annotation is anchored to, as a quote — copies of the annotation's anchors become sources of the note (`copySourcesFrom` on `PATCH /api/notes/[noteId]`), so the quote points back to the reader like every quote; the row, a markdown link on a line of its own to the reader's address of the annotation (`/n/<notebookId>?doc=<documentId>&src=<sourceId>&annotation=<noteId>`), its text the annotation's kind — Comment, Explanation, Simplified, Analysis, Visualization, Assistant, Highlight — drawn in the note as a row like a link card: the annotation glyph, the kind, "Annotation" under it (`components/markdown.tsx`); then what the annotation holds — its text (the comment, the explanation, the analysis, the simplified rewrite without its markers, the note typed on a highlight; a highlight that is only its quote adds none), a visualization's own markdown (the picture and its caption), or, for an annotation that holds a conversation (Explain+, Simplify+, Analyze+, Visualize+, the assistant's card, §21), its log — the same log the mark's hover shows (`POST /api/notes/[noteId]/log`, fetched at the drop, written then if not stored), as a dash list, one line per message opening with You or Assistant, the last 20 at most, so the note keeps the exchange and not only the door to it (`components/outline/reference-drop.ts`). So the note reads: the quote, then the comment or the tool's output on it. The ghost that follows the pointer shows the annotation's first words. A click on the row opens the annotation: in the reader, which arrives on the document, flashes the mark, and opens the annotation on it once the mark is painted (`?annotation=`, the same opening a click on the mark does); on the notes full page, beside the note it was clicked in (`components/outline/annotation-side.tsx`): the page widens to two columns — the notes in one, the annotation in the other, side by side, the annotation sticky — and the board's open note hosts the annotation beside its card; one annotation open at a time, ✕ closes it; the panel reads `GET /api/annotations/[noteId]?doc=` and shows the kind, the anchored words, the text, the conversation, the document, and the jump to the reader. The annotation stays where it is, still painted in the article — an annotation is a comprehension aid, and pulling it into a note must not unpaint the document. Dropping a note that is one of the selected notes brings the whole selection, so several notes land in one drop. A note open in its editor — the target, or a dragged source — saves its draft before the merge runs (`flushNoteDrafts`, `use-note-draft.ts`; every open editor registers itself there, and `mergeNotes` in use-outline.ts flushes the notes it joins), so the merge reads what is on screen, and the merged text takes the target's draft's place (`replaceNoteDraft`), saved and ready to keep editing. So a note in its editor takes every drop and every hold ring the note at rest takes. A note still pending takes no drop at all — a tray card offers itself only once accepted, and the floating card says why rather than taking a drop that changes nothing.
- What covers what over the article: the reader's tools are the top layer — the toolbar a selection opens, and every card it opens (`TOOL_LAYER`, z-40 in reader-interactions.tsx), with the open toolbar one step over the cards (`TOOLBOX_LAYER`, z-41), so a card never hides a row the reader is about to press — then the floating note card (z-30), then the article and its marks. A note card left open over the article covered the toolbar of the next selection, which is the one thing the reader just asked for. In a blank document the tools stand in the page editor's card column (z-30, after the floating note in the page, so still above it), under the page editor's header (z-35); the extract page and the conversation view (z-35, after the header in the pane) cover the header, and the menus that open from the top bar — the document list, Share, History (z-40) — open over all of it. The surfaces that take the whole window (the dialogs, the graph, the distilled page, z-50) stay above all of it.
- A tooltip (`components/tooltip.tsx`) shows for a control the pointer moved onto. A control that comes up under a pointer that stands still — a card that opens where the toolbox was, Run turning into Stop — shows its tooltip once the pointer moves on it, so a tooltip never covers the answer that just opened.
- Tool blocks (Explanation, Simplified, Assistant chat, a comment, a link, and the on-mark card of a highlight) place by proximity to the highlighted text and never cover a word: with nothing beside the text a new block goes right; with a block already close on the right it goes left; with both sides taken it drops below the existing blocks — right before left, top to bottom. A side takes a card only when its margin holds one (260–320px wide, 12px from the words, 8px from the pane's edge: `cardRoom`, reader-interactions.tsx). When neither margin does, the column moves left by what the card needs (`--cards-room` on the pane, globals.css) while a card is open, and every card docks right; when even that leaves no room — the narrow reader — a card opens under the paragraph that ends its passage, at the column's width, and that paragraph's bottom margin grows by the cards' height, so the text after it moves down (`layoutNarrowCards`); a new card below the window scrolls into view, its foot too, above the phone's bottom bar, with the passage's first line kept on screen, and again when its run lands and the card has grown. A width change re-places an open toolbar beside its words, read from the tint when the focus is in a toolbar field, and a card with words typed in its box or messages queued stays open when the reader turns narrow. The column's slide (`--cards-room`) re-places an open toolbar once it is still. The on-mark card closes on a click elsewhere, never inside a drag: the drag's selection opens the toolbar, which takes its place. A highlight's card holds its four colors, a one-line comment field that grows while the reader writes, Delete, and Link to other texts (a link across texts starts from the highlight there); the mic and Save show once the reader writes. A comment's card in the block reader takes the page editor's shape: Resolve (✓) and Delete as icons at its head, the comment under them, and no second copy of the quote, which is lit beside it. A card's Save shows in the header's Saved; a toast says only "Saved · both versions kept". Cards are opaque: no word shows through one. Placement measures the blocks where they actually are, so dragged blocks count, and a card the reader dragged keeps its place. Every tool block grows with its content — a streaming explanation, a growing conversation — up to the pane's visible height (24px short of it), and past that its body scrolls while its header stays. A growing block that reaches a block below it on its side stops above it and scrolls inside, so the block under it keeps its place while the reader reads it (`cardCaps`); only when that leaves it under 160px does it push the block down, the pushed block pushing the next, each sliding to its new place. A cap lifts when a card opens or closes. A ResizeObserver on the blocks settles them (`settleSideCards`, reader-interactions.tsx); a manual resize of the assistant card counts as growth. The connector line follows every frame of a slide, and any scroll box under the text.
- The selection popover follows the same rule: right of the text when that side is clear, else left, else directly below the highlighted text; a side takes it only with the room for it beside the words. A field that widens the toolbox (the comment, the assistant's command box, a definition) grows it away from the words, into the margin, as far as the margin goes; past that the toolbox keeps its width and the field wraps. It never grows over the words it was opened for. The toolbox is one card at every width: the colors and the voice are its first row and Add to notes its second, then the tools, so it covers as few lines as it can. On a coarse pointer two short tools share a row, each one tap: Explain and Simplify, Visualize and Comment. Under words low in the pane the toolbox stands above them when the room under them runs past the pane's foot (on a phone, the bottom bar), and with room on neither side the pane scrolls by the overflow, in the page editor too; on a touch screen the selection the tint puts back over its marks is the same selection, and the toolbox keeps that place.
- Link across texts (two-ended links): the first selection waits as the link's first end — tinted like a selection, a dashed sage line under it (mark kind `pending-link`) — while the reader finds the other end in this document, another attached document, or the other pane of a split view (the pending link lives in sessionStorage across a document switch). Highlighting text then opens the toolbar with Link here as its first row, in Link's place; pressing it closes the link. While the link waits, a banner names its first end: in the block reader it stands in the article's band, between Contents and the Collapse and Extract buttons, where no word sits, and in a band too narrow for it (24rem) it wraps under those buttons, beside the toast, as it does in the page editor; its sentence wraps onto more lines rather than cut, so the steps (select the other end, then press Link here) show at every width; its ✕ or Escape cancels the link (with the toolbar open, the first Escape closes the toolbar, one layer at a time, and the next cancels the link), and the next selection opens the toolbar as before. Both ends paint the moment the link closes, sweeping in, in every pane that shows one (`dissect:link-created`), before the refresh delivers the server's copy. Then a link card opens beside the closing end, docked like the other tool blocks, and no toast: the card says the link is made. It holds both quotes and a box for what the link is about — Save stores it as the link's `reason` (the same field a recommended link's AI reason uses; `PATCH /api/links/:id {reason}`); ✕ or Escape closes the card and keeps the link as it is, and an empty box saves nothing. The description shows in the mark's tip and under the link in the Annotations tab, where Describe / Edit change it.
- Voice: the last button of the toolbox's colors row reads the highlighted text aloud. The Edge voice reads it (`POST /api/speech`, Microsoft Edge's free read-aloud neural voices, no key — Xiaoxiao for Chinese text, Ava multilingual otherwise); when it fails and OPENAI_API_KEY is set, OpenAI TTS reads instead (model gpt-4o-mini-tts, voice alloy); when both are out, the most natural browser voice reads (zh-CN for Chinese text, en-US otherwise). The reading outlives the popover: dismissing the selection leaves a floating "Stop reading" control; that, the bubble, or switching documents stops it.
- Voice: a round bubble under the selection popover (in the compact toolbox, the last button of the colors row) reads the highlighted text aloud. The Edge voice reads it (`POST /api/speech`, Microsoft Edge's free read-aloud neural voices, no key — Xiaoxiao for Chinese text, Ava multilingual otherwise); when it fails and OPENAI_API_KEY is set, OpenAI TTS reads instead (model gpt-4o-mini-tts, voice alloy); when both are out, the most natural browser voice reads (zh-CN for Chinese text, en-US otherwise). The reading outlives the popover: dismissing the selection leaves a floating "Stop reading" control; that, the bubble, or switching documents stops it.
- Voice command: Command, beside "+ note" on every section in the notes tray and on the notes full page, and beside Note under every grouping but Section. Its glyph is a microphone with the assistant's spark (a plain microphone is voice typing); everywhere it is the glyph alone, its name in its tooltip. Press to record (five minutes at most, at a low bitrate so the recording fits one request), press again to stop; the recording goes to `POST /api/notes/voice?sectionId=&documentId=&thinking=` as its bytes and takes the upload transcription ladder (§11: Deepgram, then Groq Whisper, then OpenAI Whisper, then Gemini). The transcript is not the note: it is a command over the open document and the section's notes, read by the voice command's template (`lib/prompts/voice.ts`) on Claude Sonnet 5 (`VOICE_MODEL`, `VOICE_EFFORT` in `lib/derive/config.ts`: `high` on Deep Thinking, `low` on Fast Thinking; the document is the cached system prefix), and the answer is the notes the command asks for. A command that dictates lands as one note in the reader's words, filler removed. A command that asks for the document's words — the evidence for something, the passages about it, what the document says — lands as a note built from verbatim quotes of the document, each on its own `> ` line and resolved against the real block text (exact, then whitespace-tolerant, then typography-tolerant, §5; then any block when the model named the wrong one) into a source, so every quote points back to the reader; a quote that resolves nowhere is dropped. The command sets the shape — a caption, bullets with a quote under each, a checklist, headings, bold on the words it names — and the place: the section it was spoken in, another section by name, or a new one. Every note lands `PENDING` (derivation type `VOICE`) in the pending queue — the reader reads it over and accepts it. On the notes full page no document is open, so a command quotes nothing. The route streams its three stages (transcribe, plan, write) as NDJSON and the tray shows them on the bottom progress bar (`components/progress-bar.tsx`). A refused microphone, a browser that cannot record, an empty recording, a failed transcription, or a command that wrote no note says so under the section header. Offline, Command records nothing and says AI is off (§17). A send that fails keeps the recording: Command becomes Send again, with ✕ to discard it, so a command is never spoken twice.
- Highlight colors: a separate bubble right above the selection popover holds the four color dots (clay, sage, gold, plum); one click highlights the selection in that color. Near the top of the page the bubble drops below the popover instead, beside the voice bubble. Low in the pane, where the popover and its bubbles would end past the pane's bottom edge, the popover is lifted until the stack is inside the pane: beside the words it moves up; under the words it goes above them when the room above holds it, so the words stay where the reader is looking; only with room on neither side does the pane scroll by the overflow. A selection never opens tools the reader has to scroll to.
- A faint dashed connector line runs from the edge of each tool block's highlighted text to the block, so the correspondence stays visible with several blocks open.
- Figures and equations open their toolbar with a hold-and-circle gesture (pointer down + ≥300° of turning), anchored to the whole block; in the block reader a click on a figure's picture opens it too, as a click on an image does in the page editor (a picture under a mark or a link opens what that opens); the toolbar stands beside the block as a selection's stands beside its words — right of it, else left, else under it — never over the figure, the table, or the caption; a table is text, and a selection inside it opens the text toolbar. The assistant on a figure reads the visual — image figures attach the image, SVG charts attach their whole source and go to Claude Opus 5.5 (`SVG_CHART_MODEL`), videos answer from caption. A highlighted figure shows a side label on its right that jumps to the annotation, instead of text marks.
- Extract (`DISTILL`): the reader asks the article one question — from the Extract button at the top right of the reader, the Extract tab in the side tray (every extraction of the open document and of the project, listed for reference), or the article menu. The extract page opens over the article: the question large at the top, under it the quotes that answer it, in document order, each with a caption saying how it answers the question and how it sits in the document's context. Clicking a quote closes the page and jumps to its exact span. Add to notes lands the quote as a PENDING note: the quote as `> ` lines, the caption under it, the quote's source. Highlighting text anywhere on the page offers Add to notes on the selection (`selection-notes.tsx`): the highlighted words land as a PENDING note, anchored to the point or the quote they were highlighted inside; highlighted elsewhere on the page they land without an anchor. The words of a point and of a quote are selectable, and a click that ends a selection belongs to the selection, not to the jump. The corpus extract page offers the same. Cancel stops a running extraction — the request aborts, nothing persists, and the ask view keeps the question for editing; the question box keeps a draft per document (per project on the corpus page; `components/reader/extract-draft.ts`, `unitos-extract-draft:`, written at most every 300 ms and on pagehide), so a reload, a failed question, or a deploy never loses it, and the draft clears when that question's extraction lands; closing the page never cancels, and the Extract button carries a progress bar while an extraction runs. Extractions persist per notebook per document, newest first; the page and the Extract tab list them. The page regenerates one (§4: the question runs again with `replaceId`, the new extraction takes the old one's place in the same write, and carries the count — `regenerations` — of how many times the question ran again to make it; at `DISTILL_REGENERATE_MAX`, two, Regenerate disables and the route refuses, and the reader asks the question as a new extraction instead) and deletes them one by one (the ✕ on a row) or selected together: every row of the list carries a select circle (`extraction-list.tsx`), and the bar over the rows says how many are selected and deletes them in one call (`removeDistillationIds` on `PATCH /api/notebooks/[notebookId]/documents/[documentId]` and on `PATCH /api/notebooks/[notebookId]` for the project's extractions). A delete asks nothing: the rows leave at once and the notes' Undo pill shows (12 s, Ctrl+Z presses Undo); the PATCH waits for the pill to go and is keepalive on page close; a reload while the pill shows hides the rows and sends the PATCH again (`components/reader/attachment-delete.ts`). History has no row for it.
- Matches: the assistant's answer about a selection ends with the passages across the document that match the selection, each with a ¶ chip that jumps to its block (§7). Stored extractions of the old Match-it tool (`EXTRACT`) still paint as a labeled layer — the origin phrase solid-underlined, its passages dash-underlined, every span carrying the match's label chip (M1, M2, …) — and clicking any span opens the match card: the origin quote, every passage quote, Delete (no ask; the Undo pill, as an extraction's delete). Each quote in the card jumps to its text in the article; a quote the article no longer holds does not jump.
- Key terms (the dotted glossary underlines) are pressable: hover for the definition, press for the selection toolbar on the term.
- The article menu floats at the top left of the page and stays there as the article scrolls, like the controls at the top right; both stand on the article's band, a strip of the page's color at the pane's top that the text scrolls under (`data-article-band`), so neither ever sits on a word. On a coarse pointer, where the buttons are 34 px tall, the band reaches 8 px under them and takes those 10 px of room at the top, so no line touches them. It holds one control: Contents (§26), the button that opens the article's parts, each a jump to where it starts. Questions about the whole article go to the assistant tab in the side panel (§7).
- Summary lives in the side panel: one rail button, a depth control with three levels (layman / intermediate / professional), one stored summary per depth.
- The salience toggle gave its top-right spot to the Extract button; the SALIENCE derivation stays in the pipeline without a reader control.
- The background (SPEC.md §1): one field in Settings, optional, editable any time. Saved globally (`ReaderProfile`); a per-project override (`Notebook.profile`, `PATCH /api/notebooks/[id]` with `profile`) still wins when set, and the Context tab that edited it in the reader's header is gone (2026-09-18: Save for offline took its place). Older purpose and application columns clear on save.
- Keyboard-first: `j/k` navigate pending queue, `Enter` accept, `Backspace` reject, `e` edit, `g` jump to source. The keys act only while the reader works the queue: the last press or focus was in the pending queue or on a pending card, and a pending card pressed becomes the one the keys act on. A key pressed after a click in the article, the graph, or anywhere else accepts or rejects nothing.
- **Dashboard:** Projects, with one New project button at the right of the heading (`components/works/works-shelf.tsx`), then the project cards: four to a row on a wide screen, two to a row on a phone, the title under the card's ⋯ with no empty band above it. A card's ⋯ opens its menu under the title, so the title stays in view; Rename turns the card's title into a field in place, as the reader's title does (Enter keeps, Escape drops, a blur keeps the typed words); the card's instruments fan out on hover only, never on focus or while the menu is open. New project is one press: the project is created with the title "Untitled project" (renamed in the reader's title) and opens on the add-document dialog — the upload assistant — through `?add=1`, so the first thing a new project asks for is a document. A project with no document shows "No document yet." and one Add a document button under it (`components/reader/empty-project-add.tsx`), which opens the same dialog as the header's +; a viewer sees the line alone. The dialog of a project with no document yet carries a title field above the drop zone (placeholder: the default title), saved on blur, Enter, and Continue; the dashboard has no title field and no dashed card. A card's ⋯ Delete opens a confirm over the dashboard, in the app's own look: the project, its sections, and all its notes go; the documents only this project holds are named, and they stay in the library with their words (`GET /api/notebooks/[id]` lists them; `lib/documents/orphans.ts`). The delete leaves such a document in no project, never deletes it, and keeps it for the owner, in the delete's transaction (`KeptDocument`, one row per account per document), so the library lists it for them; with sign-in, the library lists the documents of the account's projects and the documents in no project the account kept or wrote in (kept by a project delete, an edit, a saved rich text or version, a link made from it). Reading is no claim: a reading position lists nothing, so a collaborator who only read a document of a deleted project does not get it.
- **Welcome flow (first visit):** on the dashboard of a new account — no project yet, and created within the last 30 days (`NEW_ACCOUNT_DAYS`, `components/works/welcome-flow.tsx`); an admin reset stamps `createdAt` anew, so a reset account is new again, and an account older than that, one that signed up before the flow existed, is never welcomed and never nudged — a screen fades in — "Welcome <first name>" and the tagline over the mark covering the whole background — and fades out into the dashboard (`components/works/welcome-flow.tsx`; localStorage keeps it to one showing per account — the stored value is the account's id and `createdAt`, so an account an admin reset is welcomed again). The nudges' position is stored with the id of the account the splash welcomed, and a nudge shows only while that account is the signed-in one (the account cookie): another account on the same browser, or an account that never had the welcome, sees none. Then the nudges (`components/nudges.tsx`), one at a time, in the **onboarding feature look**: the next thing to try glows — a pulsing clay ring (globals.css `.nudge-glow`) — and one small translucent caption beside it says in a few words what it does; ✕ dismisses it, and pressing the target takes it. The order: New project on the dashboard; after the first document is added, + (the nudge waits for the first document, since a new project opens on the dialog, and hides while any dialog is open); select a passage — the caption itself glows on the article's top edge and ends when the selection toolbar appears; the side panel's rail; Extract at the top right; the first note in the tray, twice — first a ghost card, a translucent copy of the note's shape, lifts, slides onto the note below it, draws back while the hold ring draws around that note, and falls in, on a loop (`.nudge-ghost-merge`, `.nudge-ring`) until a hold ring draws or a merge lands (skipped when the tray holds one note), then the ghost slides out of the tray toward the article on a loop (`.nudge-ghost`) until a note floats; the four arrows in the tray that open the notes full page; and, on the notes full page, the first section's title, which opens its board. A target carries `data-nudge="<id>"`, or several ids with spaces between when two steps share it. A step whose target a project lacks (a transcript has no article and no Extract) is skipped; localStorage (`unitos-nudge-step`) keeps the position across the dashboard and the project. The ? button carries a pulsing dot until the guide is opened once; the guide leads with Extract, then an emphasized Circle & ask section (figures, and handwritten pages where the whole PDF is a figure — lasso highlight included), then one card per selection tool, then the side panel. The /signin backdrop carries the same mark, dimmed, covering the page's top-left quadrant. **With Jev configured** the sequence skips the nudges the reader has already earned: on mount, `GET /api/jev/nudges` reads the account's click log (the last 180 days, grouped by control) and asks Jev one noul per step — has the reader already done what this step teaches — and every step at 0.75 or above is passed over, the position moving past it (`components/nudges.tsx`, once per tab in sessionStorage); no key, or no signed-in reader, and every nudge shows.

---

## 7. Assistant Scopes + the Digest (Phase 6)

One assistant panel with two scopes, both reading the digest. The assistant covers content, never the account: there is no scope across projects.

| Scope | Context sent | Example queries |
|---|---|---|
| This page (wire value `document`; `documentId` required) | the open document from this project's digest, whole — its text, its layers, the project's notes that cite it, then the reader's other notes of the project (`documentSystem` in `lib/digest/render.ts`; past the layers' budget the other notes are cut first, with the cut declared) | "what does the method section claim", "where does this page give the number", "summarize the argument" |
| Project (wire value `notebook`) | this project's digest, whole | "map claims to evidence", "where do my notes contradict", "which sections are thin" |

This page is the default while a document is open and disabled with none open; the tasks (contradictions, gaps, unsourced) run at Project scope. A conversation holds both scopes: the scope is chosen per message. A reader has as many conversations of a project as they start. **Conversations**, in the tray's head row beside the panel's title, lists them newest first — the title (the note's gist, else the first message's words), when it last changed, how many messages, the open one marked; a click opens one in the thread's place, the bin deletes one (its side chats and comments with it) with no ask, the notes' Undo pill offering Undo, Back returns to the thread, and History under the list opens the assistant history page. **New conversation** (+), beside it, starts an empty thread and keeps the one on screen in the list: it deletes nothing (`GET /api/assistant/conversation?list=1` answers the list, `?conversationNoteId=` one conversation). A conversation's delete, from the list's bin or the card's trash, waits for the Undo pill to go without Undo and then runs through `DELETE /api/notes/:id`, which keeps it, its side chats, and the comments on its answers for History's Restore (`components/assistant/conversation-delete.ts`). A failed answer reads as one line under the box that sent it, the app's own words: "No answer: Unitos could not reach the server. Try again." or "No answer: the assistant had a problem. Try again." (`components/assistant/failure.ts`), and the assistant's routes, `api/derive` (Explain, Simplify, Analyze, Extract), and the suggest route word a failed model call the same way, in place of the provider's text (`api/assistant/failure-line.ts`); the panel's other failures — loading the list or a conversation, an attachment, a comment — read "Not loaded. Try again." or "Not saved. Try again."; a reason the server wrote for the reader (a refusal, an assistant with no key, an answer that ran out of room or could not be read) keeps its words, and the technical text goes to the console or the server log. Send, Accept, and Reject have one shape on every assistant surface (`decision-classes.ts`), finger-sized on a touch screen. The scope (This page, Project), the thinking chip, and Web sit on one row right above the composer; the scope's description is its tooltip. Every box that sends a message to the assistant sends with Send, the microphone beside it. In the panel and in the chat card an older answer shows its rating and Save as note on hover or focus (a tap on a touch screen); the newest answer shows them at rest.

**Assistant history** (`/n/[notebookId]/assistant`, from History under the panel's Conversations list): every assistant conversation of the project, whoever had it, newest first, each on its own panel — the contributor who started it as a person badge (shared projects only, §19), the kind (Assistant, or the tool with its plus for a tool's output continued into a conversation, §21), when, the message count, and the turns whole. Side chats are left out: a side chat belongs to the conversation it came from and opens from that chat box. A conversation anchored in a document shows the document's title and the highlighted text it started from, and Open in the reader opens the document at that text (`?doc=&src=`); the sidebar assistant's own conversation shows as Project assistant, and Open in the reader opens the reader. Conversations are the notes of the hidden Annotations section that hold turns (`SYNTHESIS`, or a tool note with `Note.conversation`), each carrying its author.

**The digest** is the assistant's storage: one `NotebookDigest` row per corpus per user holding the serialized corpus — every document in full (text and video transcripts, block-tagged), every note (pending ones marked), every annotation (highlights, comments, explanations, simplified rewrites, assistant conversations), every distillation, extraction, summary, salience span, link, and edit. Never a similarity search: the assistant sees everything, so questions about counts, spread, and absence are answerable.

- **Staleness:** a fingerprint of cheap grouped aggregates over the content tables (note counts and `updatedAt`, block id sets, `BlockEdit` rows, layer Json hashes, titles) is compared on every read; a mismatch rebuilds the row. No mutation hooks to forget. The fingerprint carries the render's version (`DIGEST_VERSION`, `lib/digest/fingerprint.ts`), so a change to how the digest renders (v2: an import's rows name their page) rebuilds every row once.
- **Reading a long project:** past `ASSISTANT_WHOLE_THRESHOLD` (120,000 characters of document text, Stitch's whole threshold) a message at Project scope reads the project the way Stitch does (§22, `lib/assistant/project-reading.ts`): the reading passes (`pickBlocks`, `lib/graph/stitch.ts`) find the blocks the message and the conversation need from the documents' skeletons, and the digest renders each document's gist and those blocks' real text in reading order, the gaps declared, with every note and layer whole (`corpusPickedSystem`). The system message tells the model that text not shown was not read for this message, so a count or an absence across the whole text is answered from what is shown and says so. A message's cost follows what it needs, not the size of the project, and every quote and citation still comes from stored block text. A reading that fails, a task with no message (contradictions, gaps, unsourced), and a project under the threshold read the digest whole as before, so its prefix caches from message to message.
- **The ranked cut:** with Jev configured and the project's documents past the text budget, the documents render in the order the message needs them (`lib/digest/rank.ts`): one yes/no per document — does it hold material the question needs — from its title, its skeleton's gist, and its parts' titles, and the cut falls on the rest. Under the budget nothing is cut, so nothing is ranked and the digest stays byte-identical; over it the system message changed as documents were added anyway, and a message-shaped order is the better trade. Page scope is never ranked: one document has the whole budget.
- **Determinism:** the rendered digest is byte-identical until content changes, so both scopes cache their prompt prefix (§2).
- **Budgets:** past the character budget, document text cuts at block boundaries with a declared marker, never silently. Notes and layers have their own budget. The admin digest page still renders every project of an account together (`corporaSystem`): a document attached to several projects renders its text once; later projects point back to it.
- **Selection questions** stay in the reader: the selection popover's assistant chat (`/api/assistant/act`) already carries the anchor and the cached document prefix.
- **Actions from the sidebar assistant.** At This page scope, Ask can change the open document and the notes, the way the selection chat does: the same action types (`lib/assistant/plan.ts`, one code path for both routes: the schema, the quote-to-anchor resolution, the warnings for actions that do not validate), proposed in the answer, never run by the server. A message that asks for a change ends the model's answer with a fenced block (info string `actions`) holding the actions as JSON; the route holds the fence back from the stream, so the reader sees the answer alone, validates every action against the real document, and sends the plan after the answer as `STREAM_PLAN_TOKEN` + JSON. The fence reads through the slips a model makes when it writes JSON by hand (`readActions`, `looseJson`): one action alone or a list, a field written null, blockIds empty or past 200 (the whole document), a description or an instruction past its length (cut to it), a line break or a quote left raw in a string, a trailing comma, a code fence inside a string, output cut off. Each action is read on its own: one that does not read is a warning that names it ("Skipped: the action could not be read. (…)") and the others stand. Only a fence where nothing reads as JSON is the one warning "The assistant's actions could not be read. Ask again."; on a document with rich text, a suggest action the fence names but that does not read runs with the answer as its instruction. The panel hands the plan to the open document's reader (`dissect:assistant-plan`, by document id, so a split reader's right pane takes its own), and the reader's plan card shows it: every action with a checkbox, its description, and its concrete target; Apply runs the checked ones through the normal API routes; Cancel drops them. Nothing runs without Apply. Under the answer, the panel says how many actions were proposed, with Review in the plan card to reopen the card once closed; the answer saves without the actions. Project scope proposes nothing: a message that asks for a change is answered with where the change runs. On a document with rich text, a change to its words or styles is one `suggest {instruction, blockIds?, description}` action in place of the five block actions (`actionLines`); the reader runs it as the assistant's suggestions (§29), never in the plan card. Several suggest actions in one answer join into one: their instructions in order, their blocks together (one that names none covers the whole document). A request to reorganize, format, restructure, rewrite, fix, shorten, or translate the open document is a change to it, and so is one to "my notes" when the document holds the reader's own notes. The selection chat answers it with no reply; the panel with one sentence on what will change, and the panel sends `caretBlockId`. A suggest action on a document without rich text, or a block action on one with it, is a warning.
- **Edits to a document without rich text** (an article, slides, sheets, a handwritten document, a video's or an audio's transcript): the block actions. edit_block {blockId, newText} (a LIST block's text is its lines, each with its marker, "- " or "1. ", and two spaces per level of nesting: a line nests or unnests by its spaces); insert_paragraph {afterBlockId | null, text, kind?} (null is the document's start; kind paragraph, h1 to h3, list, or numbered, and a list's markers are written for it); remove_block; format_block {blockId, kind} with the same kinds (to a list, the route writes the markers; out of one, it takes them off, as the reader's edit toolbar does); style {blockId, quote, style: bold, italic, or underline}; link {blockId, quote, toDocumentId or href} (href: a web address, http, https, or mailto, kept in Block.links like a PDF's own links, `POST /api/blocks/[blockId]/link`, recorded as LINK_ADD or LINK_REMOVE; the answer names the address the words had, for Undo); move_block {blockId, afterBlockId | null} (`POST /api/blocks/[blockId]/move`: the blocks between shift by one, and the answer names the block it stood after, for Undo; a `BLOCK_MOVE` edit records it, so the Edits tab shows "Paragraph moved" with the words and "Now after …", and History "moved a paragraph"). New blocks after one block land in the plan's order, each after the one before. A Contents entry or a footnote number that points at a block by its order (`Block.links` `targetOrder`) follows its block when blocks are added, restored, or moved (`lib/block-order.ts`). **The rule** (`lib/block-takes.ts`, read by the plan and by every block route, so the plan card offers only what the routes do): refuse only what would corrupt the document. Words change in a text block, in a transcript line, in a slide's replica, whose lines come and go in its text boxes and its speaker notes, and in a sheet's replica, whose rows and columns change too (§27; the plan runs the route's own replica check, `replicaEdit`, first and skips what it refuses with the route's reason, and the prompt lists what each sheet keeps as it is: its frozen rows and columns, the cells formulas compute, its merged cells, and a chart's data, `sheetKeepLines`); a format changes in a paragraph, a heading, a list, or code; a page, a transcript line, or an equation keeps its kind; a text block, a transcript line, a line, a converted table, or a page while another page stays may go, and comes back whole, into its own place when nothing took it since (a removal leaves a gap in the orders; `/api/blocks/restore`); a converted table's words, rows, and columns change (its html is its text drawn, §16); any other table's words change within its cells, and its html stays (`replicaEdit`): a PDF's or a Word file's table, whose DOM text is its text (§5), takes the words in its runs as a slide does (§27), its caption line, its bold words, its note marks, and its merged cells kept, and a web page's or a Markdown file's, whose DOM text is not its text (its text is the grid the page walk reads: a merged cell's words in every row it spans, its other columns empty), takes them cell by cell in the runs that hold them; a table's rows and columns, the break between a cell's paragraphs, a formula, and a cell a merge covers stay; a style or a web link goes on a text block's words and on a transcript line's; a transcript's lines join, split, and change voice through their own route (§11, **The assistant's edits**); nothing changes a video's player or a figure, or a slide's, a sheet's, or a table's kind; a sheets document keeps its sheet names (the HEADING before each SHEET); a slides, sheets, video, or audio document takes no new block, and no block moves in one; in a handwritten document a new block goes after the last page, never between pages or before them, and joins the page whose words it follows; there a page keeps its text ("Page N", which its annotations find it by: `api.pageWords`), and its words are the blocks converted from it, which the prompt lists by page (`pageLines`, `lib/assistant/pages.ts`). An action the rule refuses is a warning; the route answers 400. **revise** {instruction, blockIds?, description} (the sidebar and the selection chat; in the selection chat a revise that names no blocks covers the selected ones): a change to the words of more than five blocks — the spelling or grammar across the document, its register, a section rewritten — is one revise action, never a run of edit_block actions, which a plan caps at 20 (`ACTIONS_MAX`). The route reads the document the way the assistant's suggestions do (§4 `suggest`, the one code path; a slide reads as its lines, a sheet and a table as their rows, and a transcript line as its words, words changed within lines and cells and a transcript line's times kept, and a change the replica does not take is skipped with the reason while the block's other changes stand): windows of 8,000 characters and 60 blocks, at most 12, four at once, one Claude Sonnet 5 call each, windows not started by 270 s reported; a heading named grows to its section. The ops become block actions (`lib/assistant/revise.ts`): a block's word changes one edit_block, with the styles and links on its new words after it (bold, italic, underline, a text color, a highlight, a web address), then its format; replaced blocks pair with the new ones in order around the blocks that stay, the extra old ones go and the extra new ones follow; each action checked against the rule. They take the revise action's place in the plan, however many; in the sidebar, heartbeat spaces keep the stream open meanwhile (the route's `maxDuration` is 300 s); the selection chat starts no window past 150 s. What the plan cannot carry (alignment, spacing, an indent, a font, a size, strikethrough, a table's rows and columns, footnotes, a figure's caption, an image) is a warning. Not yet: a transcript line given a voice the recording does not have yet.
- **The order pass** (`lib/assistant/reorder.ts`, `lib/assistant/reorder-run.ts`, `lib/prompts/reorder.ts`): a change that moves blocks across the document — group by theme, organize, put in order — sets `reorder: true` on its revise or suggest action (a change of order of more than two blocks is never a run of move_block actions). The windows each see a part, so they never move a block: their prompt says the order pass does, and they change only the words the message asks to change ("organize, and shorten only the descriptions" is one action: the order and the words). The order pass runs beside the windows: one Claude Sonnet 5 call over the whole document under the same cached prefix, answering `{summary, why, order, removeHeadings}` — every block id of the scope once in the new order, with `{heading, level}` entries for new headings (one per group, when the message asks for groups), and the ids of headings the new order makes wrong. It orders units: a block in a document without rich text; a top-level node in a rich text, so a list or a table moves whole. A unit that holds a page, a figure, an image, a footnote, a bookmark, or a page break keeps its place. The answer is read leniently: an id it repeats, an unknown id, and an id outside the scope are passed over; a block it leaves out stays right after the block it followed (a warning counts them); only a heading goes, never other words. The new order becomes the fewest moves: the longest run of units already in order stays, and the rest move in runs, each run right after the unit before it, which stays. A block is moved, never written anew: its words, its id, and its anchors go with it. Without rich text, the plan card carries the moves (move_block), then the new headings (insert_paragraph), then the headings that go (remove_block), before the windows' edits; a move the document does not take drops the whole order with a warning, since half an order is no order. With rich text, the moves are one more window, landed after the windows' words: each run is one suggestion that strikes its nodes where they stood and adds them where they go, carrying the asker's earlier suggestions on them accepted (the words the same command changed) and taking those back where the nodes stood, so Reject gives the document back as it was. A copy names the block it moves (`movedFrom`); once the move is accepted the copy takes that block's id (`adoptMovedIds`, `ext/suggest.ts`), so every anchor stays on its words. A node that holds another author's suggestion is skipped as "overlap". Checked by `scripts/qa/reorder-check.ts` (random orders run through the plan card's way of running actions) and `scripts/qa/reorder-page.mts` (the page editor's moves, Accept all, Reject all, and the ids).
- **The one pass** (`lib/assistant/one-pass.ts`, `lib/prompts/one-pass.ts`): a revise or suggest command whose scope holds 40,000 characters or fewer (`ONE_PASS_MAX_CHARS`), in a document of no fixed shape (no slides, sheets, recording, or handwritten pages), is done the way a chat with the whole document does it: one Claude Sonnet 5 call over the whole document (the cached prefix) answers with the document as it should read, by reference — `{summary, why, document, remove, formats}`: every block id of the scope in the new order, a block that stays as its id alone, a block whose words change as `{id, text}`, new blocks as `{new: markdown}` where they go, the ids of the blocks the command takes away, and the format ops the windows take (styles, alignment, spacing, indents, word formats, a table's rows and columns, footnotes). A block named by its id alone is never retyped, so nothing the command did not touch can drift. A block goes only when the answer both removes it and leaves it out; a block the answer leaves out and does not remove stays after the block it followed; an unknown id is passed over. New words become rewrite_block ops, removals remove_blocks ops, each resolved and grounded as the windows' are, and new blocks are grounded too; then the check reads them all. The order and the new blocks become the order pass's moves (the plan card's move_block and insert_paragraph, or the page editor's move suggestions, which carry the new blocks among the moved ones). A longer scope goes by the windows, with the order pass when the command moves blocks. Checked with recorded answers by `scripts/qa/reorder-check.ts` (an article) and `scripts/qa/reorder-page.mts` (the page editor).
- **The target pass** (`lib/assistant/target.ts`, `lib/prompts/target.ts`): a command whose scope runs past the windows one command takes (`SUGGEST_MAX_WINDOWS`), and past the one pass, first names the blocks it changes: one Claude Sonnet 5 call at low effort over the whole document, under the windows' cached prefix, answers `{blocks}` — ids and ranges `<first>..<last>` of the scope, in document order: the blocks whose words change, the blocks it removes, the blocks after which it adds words, and none it leaves as it is. The windows then run over those blocks alone, so "erase the commentary next to the quotes" in a long document reaches every commentary block, not the first windows' worth. An id outside the scope is passed over; a pass that fails or names nothing leaves the scope as it was. The page editor's command, the plan card's revise action, and the selection chat's first window all take it.
- **Accuracy of the assistant's edits** (every command's windows, the page editor's and the plan card's alike, and the plan's own edit_block and insert_paragraph): two guards stand between the model's ops and the reader. **Grounding** (`lib/docs/grounding.ts`): a number or a quotation (three words or more in double quotes) in new words must stand in what the model read — the document, the title, the reader's message, the assistant's instruction and answer, the conversation, an attached file — compared with curly quotes and dashes made plain and thousands commas dropped; a numbered line's marker is no number. An op that names one that does not is skipped as "unsupported", naming it. With the web on, the plan's own actions go unchecked (the pages it read are not kept); the windows read the answer, web sources and all. **The check** (`lib/prompts/suggest-check.ts`): after a window's ops resolve, a second Claude Sonnet 5 call at low effort, under the same cached document, reads each op's words before and after against the command and names the ops that change or remove what the command said to keep (quotes, names, a part it names), change what it did not ask to change, state what the document does not, or remove a block it does not call for removing; those are skipped with its why ("Skipped by the check: …"). Formats and styles are not checked. A check that fails keeps every op: the reader still accepts or rejects each one. Checked by `scripts/qa/grounding-check.ts`.
- **Undo.** Every applied action records the request that takes it back, and the toast after Apply ("2 actions accepted") carries Undo for twelve seconds, the notes' Undo time: a text edit patches the block's text back, a format change its kind and its text (a list's markers change with its kind; a code block is code again: the PATCH route takes `kind: "code"` for it, and the edit toolbar's undo does the same), a removed block restores through `/api/blocks/restore` with its own id and whole (its kind, a page's number, a transcript line's times and voice, its styles and links), an added paragraph, highlight, comment, note, section, or link to a document deletes, a style toggles again, a moved block moves back after the block it stood after, and a web link puts back the address the words had (none takes it off). Undo runs them newest first and says how many came back. The selection chat's plan takes the same path.
- **Matches.** The selection chat does the work the Match-it tool did, and answers questions Explain does not. With a selection, the plan carries `matches` beside `reply` and `actions`: the passages across the document that deal with what the selection focuses on — 3 to 8, from anywhere in the document, never the selection itself, the most revealing first, each a verbatim quote of one block with one sentence on what it says about the topic. The prompt has the model gather them first and answer from them, every claim in the reply citing its passage as `[block <id>]`. The matches are the model's working: the reply lists none of them, and the card shows the reply alone — a claim's `[block <id>]` citation renders as the ¶ chip that jumps to the block. The reply is what the card shows and what the conversation note stores. The first message of a conversation always gathers matches; a later message gathers them only when it asks where else the document deals with something. Without a selection the list is empty.
- **Words from a figure.** The assistant reads a selected figure (its image, an SVG chart's source, or the PDF page it sits on) and can put the words it reads under the figure. Two ways in: the figure's tools (Assistant, the selection chat), and, in the page editor, the **Assistant** button that leads the image toolbar (`docs-img-assistant`, in the assistant's kind color, its spark breathing twice as the toolbar opens, then still): it moves the caret past the image and opens the assistant's bar on the image (`dissect:figure-assistant`), with two chips — The text and The key points — each sent as the reader's message (Put the text under the image, Put the key points under the image), so each puts its words under the image as a suggestion; a question typed in the bar is answered in the chat. The toolbox's Assistant box on a figure (the figure's tools) has the same placeholder and the same two chips. When the words land under a figure of a document without rich text, the pane scrolls them into view. An image has no words to anchor to, so the request names it by `figureBlockId` in place of an anchor, and its conversation is not stored as an annotation. The act prompt's rule (`figureWordsRules`, lib/prompts/act.ts): a command that asks to read, extract, or summarize what the figure shows is answered in reply, with no action; only a command that asks to put the words under the figure returns insert_paragraph actions with `afterBlockId` the figure, one per paragraph, heading, or list. The shape is the one the command names, and only that one: the text when it says text, extract, transcribe, or copy, or names none; the key points when it says key points, summary, or bullet points; both only when it asks for both. Text the figure shows is copied as it shows it, its lines joined, nothing corrected, `[illegible]` for what cannot be read; key points are a list with the figure's own numbers. The words come from the picture, so the grounding check does not hold them to the document's text (`PlanContext.figureBlockId`); the same words after any other block still are. On a document with rich text the route turns them into one insert_blocks op after the figure (`figureWordsSuggestion`, lib/derive/suggest.ts), landed as the assistant's suggestion with its ✓ and ✕. On a document without rich text the reader takes them out of the plan card and draws them under the figure as the assistant's suggestion (`components/reader/figure-suggestion.tsx`): ✓ runs them through the plan's own runner, with its Undo, and ✕ drops them; nothing is written before ✓, and a failed ✓ keeps the suggestion. Checked by `scripts/qa/figure-words-check.ts` (no model), `scripts/qa/ui-figure-words.mjs` (the page editor), and `scripts/qa/ui-figure-words-article.mjs` (an article), the last two against the mock model.
- **Assistant history origins.** Every panel of the history page says where its conversation comes from, as a badge after the kind: Sidebar assistant (started in the assistant tab of the side panel, anchored nowhere — the panel says it stays in that tab and carries no link), Selection chat (started from a highlighted text; the document's title, the text, and Open in the reader), or Tool conversation (a tool card's output continued, §21; the same document line and link).
- **The queue:** a message sent while an answer runs waits under the thread, faded, with ✕ to remove it; the composer's button reads Queue once a message is composed, and the box says so. Queued messages go out in order, one per finished answer — after a Stop too. New conversation, or a conversation opened from the list, puts the queued messages back into the box. Every assistant chat queues the same way (`components/assistant/queued-list.tsx`): the panel, the reader's card and its side chats (a message queued in a side chat goes out into that side chat), tool conversations (Explain+, Simplify+), and the media pane's assistant.
- **Attachments:** the paperclip takes images (shown to the model), PDFs and text files (their text), and video and audio files (transcribed through the media document's own ladder, §11, one line per stretch of speech stamped with its time; the file stages in chunks through `POST /api/uploads` like a media upload and `POST /api/assistant/attach-media` transcribes it, 200 MB at most). Add from Google Drive (shown when Drive is configured, §14) opens the picker with the assistant's own filter (`DRIVE_ASSISTANT_MIME_TYPES`): a Doc, Sheet, Slide, or Drawing exports to PDF and rides as text, a PDF or text file as its text, video or audio as its transcript, an image as a stored image; `POST /api/assistant/attach-drive` spends the picked token once (`lib/drive/request-token.ts`, shared with the Drive import) and stores nothing but an image. A file's text rides in the message; a video or audio file's transcript is that text.
- **Stop:** Ask streams into the card; the Ask button becomes Stop while it runs and keeps whatever has streamed in so far — the read simply stops, the request aborts server-side too.
- **Highlighting an answer:** a selection inside an assistant answer opens three things (`components/assistant/answer-tools.tsx`, on every assistant surface: the panel, the reader's chat card, and the conversation read whole). **Start side chat** branches a conversation of its own off those words. **Ask about this** carries them into the next message of this conversation: the quote sits above the box as a chip, and the message goes out as the quote written as a markdown quote, then the reader's words, so the transcript, the digest, and the model read the same text. **Comment** writes a comment on those words — a `Reply` row on the conversation note (`POST /api/replies`, `GET /api/replies?noteId=`), listed under the thread with its author's badge and deletable by the author. Its box holds the quote, whose ✕ cancels (Escape too), the words, the microphone, and Comment; the thinking chip and Web hide while it is open, since a comment goes to people, not to the assistant; the Annotations tab shows the same rows under that conversation, and refreshes when one is posted. The sidebar assistant's conversation is anchored nowhere, so it lists in the Assistant group of every document's Annotations tab — badged Sidebar assistant, with no anchor to jump to — and its comments with it; a side chat lists nowhere but its chat box. A side chat and a comment need the conversation to be saved first — both hang off its note — and say so until it is. **The highlighted words stay marked** while the reader works on them: the browser's own selection goes the moment the box that opens takes focus, and a tint in the selection's own color (`.answer-tint`, bands painted over the words from the selection's range, clipped to the box that scrolls them) stays until the quote is dropped or sent, the comment posted or cancelled, or a new selection replaces it — the reader's selection tint in the article (§6), for text the reader never edits.
- **Side chats** (`Note.sideChatOfId`, `Note.sideChatQuote`): a side chat is a conversation note of its own, with no sources, that knows the conversation it came from and the quote it started on. It persists like any conversation — the panel's through `POST /api/assistant/conversation` with `sideChatOf`, the reader card's through `/api/assistant/act` with the same field — and reads back through `GET /api/assistant/conversation` (`?noteId=` for one conversation's side chats). It opens from its own chat box alone: the conversation lists its side chats as chips by their quotes, and an open side chat carries a head with its quote and the way back. Each thread — the conversation and each side chat — keeps its own box and its own queued messages (`AssistantChat.inputs`): Back shows the conversation's box, so a side chat's words never go to the conversation, and the side chat reopens with its words. **Assistant history never lists a side chat.** Deleting a conversation deletes its side chats with it (the row cascades). The side chat stays in its card and folds nothing: the article does not move. An open side chat's head carries its quote; the box shows no second quote chip for it.
- **The core.** Every answer that explains — the panel's, the selection chat's reply, Explain, Analyze, Ask on a range, the note's assistant — carries `CORE_RULE` and `ANSWER_LENGTH` (`lib/prompts/types.ts`): find what the words really say, read in the light of the whole document, and open with it in one or two sentences; then only what the reader needs to hold it (the step it rests on, the term they would miss, the link that changes how it reads, the limit). Where the document, another document, and the reader's notes make the same point, it is one point with each cited. A lookup takes one to three sentences; a question of meaning usually 80 to 200 words, longer only when the reader asks for detail. `STYLE_RULE` keeps the words plain and drops the filler. The selection chat and the tools read the reader's notes whole (`readerNotesText`: every note up to 3,000 characters, 40,000 in all, a cut declared), not their first lines.
- **Connection to previous work.** Every answer that reads the reader's notes and annotations — the panel's, the selection chat's reply, Explain with the project context — carries `CONNECTION_RULE`: when a note or an annotation says the same thing, disagrees, extends it, or asks the question the answer answers, the answer ends with a short paragraph opened by **Connection to your work:** that names each, at most three, with its `[note <id>]` chip. None bears on it: no paragraph. Analyze puts the same connections in its Linking to context section. A chip on a note opens it in the notes tray; a chip on an annotation opens its card on its mark, or the reader on its document.
- **Citations read as chips.** `[block <id>]` renders as the ¶ chip; `[note <id>]` renders as the ✎ chip, which opens the note in the notes tray (`dissect:show-note`). The prompts say never to write an id in words. The actions block is found whatever info string the model writes on it (`lib/assistant/fence.ts`, client-safe): any fence, or bare JSON on a line of its own, whose JSON is actions. The route holds it back at both scopes — Project scope drops it — and the panel strips one an older saved answer still carries, so the reader never sees the JSON.
- **Save as note** (`components/assistant/save-as-note.tsx`, `POST /api/notes/organize`, `lib/prompts/organize.ts`): under every answer of the panel, the selection chat, the media pane's assistant, and under Explain, Analyze, and Simplify. One model call (`ORGANIZE_MODEL`, Claude Sonnet 5; the `organize` feature) reads the answer, the question or the selection, the open document whole, and the passages the answer cites from the project's other documents, and writes one note: a title line, the key points each with its reasoning, and under each the document's words as `> ` quotes. It keeps every point, caveat, and link, adds no fact, and writes no id. Every quote resolves against the real block text and becomes a source (`lib/notes/write-planned.ts`, the voice command's writer); the note lands PENDING (`derivationType` SYNTHESIS) in the section the reader last wrote a note in, else the project's first, else a new one; the button then reads where it went, with Show. The answer is not touched.
- **Thinking:** one chip that names the choice, Fast Thinking or Deep Thinking; a click switches to the other (`thinking` on `/api/assistant` and `/api/assistant/act`, `lib/assistant/thinking.ts`). Fast Thinking answers at Kimi's lowest reasoning effort; Deep Thinking at the effort every assistant answer ran at before the choice existed (`DERIVATION_EFFORT.SYNTHESIS`). Deep is the default, so an answer is as considered as it always was until the reader asks for speed. One choice for the whole app, remembered in the browser like the Web toggle and shown on every assistant surface: the panel, the reader's selection box and chat card, the page editor's bar, and the media pane's chat. The tasks (contradictions, gaps, unsourced) run at the reader's choice too.
- **Web access:** the Web toggle at the end of the answer row, after the thinking options (on by default, remembered in the browser, one choice for the whole app, shown on every assistant surface — the panel, the reader's selection box and tool conversations, the media pane's assistant — `components/assistant/web-chip.tsx`) lets Ask, and the selection chat (`web: true` on `/api/assistant/act`, the same tool, the same rules, `WEB_LINES` in `lib/prompts/types.ts`; a turn that carries a video frame or an SVG chart goes to the model that reads it, without the web), search the web through a standard `web_search` function tool the model calls, run by `lib/kimi.ts` (at most five searches per answer; `web: true` on `/api/assistant`). The search follows the model that answers (`webSearchTool(modelId)`): under GLM 5.3, `WEB_SEARCH_MODEL`, it is Z.ai's Web Search API (`POST /web_search`, engine `search-prime`, ten results, through the gateway's `/zai` pass-through), whose results reach the model as plain text — title, site, date, link, summary; under Kimi K3, what a GLM id resolves to without the gateway, it is Moonshot's official web-search tool (`moonshot/web-search:latest` on the Formula API). The material stays the first source: the prompt has the model answer from the project, then verify the factual claims against outside sources and add what the project lacks, cite every web source as a markdown link at the point it supports, say plainly when the web contradicts the project, and end with a Web sources list. The search result reaches the model alone (Moonshot returns it encrypted; Z.ai's goes to the model as the tool's output), so the links the model writes are the sources on screen; an outside link opens in a new tab. Never a web result presented as the project's own. Each search records its provider's price on top of the tokens in usage telemetry: $0.01 at Z.ai, $0.005 at Moonshot (`webSearchUsd`). The tools that explain search the same way, with the same rules: Explain, Analyze, and Ask on a range of a recording (`web` on `/api/derive`, absent read as on; the route uses the web feature's model and adds `WEB_LINES` to the template), and the note's assistant (§6). A call that carries an image or an SVG chart goes to the model that reads it, without the web. The tasks (contradictions, gaps, unsourced) stay off the web.
- **Usage telemetry:** every model call records tokens and estimated cost to `UsageEvent` (list prices at call time; fire-and-forget, never blocking a response). The admin usage page (`/admin/usage`) aggregates per function, model, account, and day. The records are kept for good, and the page reads them as one history: the Cost over time table gives each window — the last 7, 30, 90, and 365 days, and all time since the first call — its total, its cost and calls per day, and its cost per call; the breakdowns by function, model, and account run over all time. Under the gateway (§2) the gateway's own 30-day spend log sits above them, and the table is the app's count. Above everything, the spending estimate: the AI cost of the last 30 days as one month (under the gateway, the gateway's spend plus what it does not price; else the app's count) plus the fixed costs — what the app pays each month whatever its use, the list in `lib/fixed-costs.ts` — per month and per year, a year being twelve months. The gateway names a model by the route it took, so GLM logs as openai/glm-5.3; the page relabels it zai/glm-5.3 with provider zai, reads every provider from its model's name, and counts the calls with no model in the log — the pass-throughs: DeepL, Moonshot's web search, Jev — under pass-through.
- **Admin digest page** (`/admin/digest`, admin-gated like the feedback inbox): the store per user — every corpus → every document → its annotations, notes, and distillations — with counts, built time, forced Rebuild, and the exact text each scope sends (`/api/admin/digest`). Each account is one scroller: its header stays pinned while its corpora scroll under it, and the page scrolls from account to account.
- **Admin accounts page** (`/admin/accounts`, admin-gated): every account with its tier chip, the projects, documents, and notes it holds, Tier (`components/admin/tier-control.tsx`, `POST /api/admin/accounts/tier`: Unitos Ultra, Unitos Premium for good, or Unitos Premium on a trial until a date — TIERS.md), Block and Unblock (`components/admin/block-control.tsx`, `POST /api/admin/accounts/block` with the email and `blocked`; `lib/block.ts`, §2 — Block puts the account's email on the block list and signs it out everywhere; above the accounts, the block list: every blocked email with Unblock, and a form that blocks an email with no account yet), and Reset account (`lib/account-reset.ts`, `POST /api/admin/accounts/reset`, the account's email typed to confirm) — deletes the account's projects with everything under them, the documents only its projects held (one still attached to another account's project, or cited by a note in one, stays in the library), its profile, digests, reading positions, sessions, memberships on shared projects, pending email links, Drive link (revoked), picture, symbol, color, and premium flag; keeps the account row (email, name, password), its usage telemetry, and its work inside other accounts' projects; stamps `createdAt` anew, so the account starts at onboarding like a new account. Sign-in off: the local reader is the one account, and the same data reset applies.
- **Click telemetry:** every click on a reader control records one `ClickEvent` row: the surface the control lives on (top bar, sidebar, AI toolbar, article menu, reader, notes tray — `lib/clicks.ts`) and the control's id. Controls carry `data-track`; regions carry `data-track-surface`; a control outside a marked region records nothing. An id carries the control's type or source after a colon where it has one: `highlight:<color>`, `annotation-recolor:<color>`, `page-highlight:<color>`, `assistant-ask:<scope>`, `assistant-task:<task>`, `assistant-recommended:<depth>`, `ask:<question>`, `note-format:<kind>`, `assistant-command:<name>`, `assistant-suggestions:review|accept-all|reject-all`. The client (`components/click-tracker.tsx`, mounted by the workspace) batches clicks and posts them to `POST /api/clicks` fire-and-forget, so telemetry never blocks the reader. The admin clicks page (`/admin/clicks`) reports the reader's functions in three groups — the AI tools, the notes functions, and the annotations made; `lib/clicks.ts` names the controls of each — as uses per day by group, every function per group, a table of every function, and uses per account by group. General controls (navigation, dialogs, video playback, the article edit toolbar, closes and cancels) record but stay off the page. The daily cron deletes rows older than 180 days.
- **The onboarding funnel:** how far a new visitor gets on the way from the sign-in page to a subscription, in eight steps (`lib/funnel.ts`): sign-in page, account, dashboard, reader, plans, order, checkout, subscribed. A visitor is one browser: the middleware sets a random visitor cookie (`dissect-visitor`, httpOnly, one year) on the first page a browser opens. The page steps record from the browser (`components/funnel-step.tsx`, mounted by the sign-in page, the dashboard, the workspace, the plans page and the plan page, and the order page) as one `POST /api/funnel {step}` per page open; the route refuses the other three. Account records in `upsertUser` when the email is new, checkout in `POST /api/billing/checkout` when the Stripe session opens, subscribed in `applySubscription` when a subscription lands on an account that did not hold it (the confirmation page and the webhook both apply the session; the second finds it held). Each row is one `FunnelEvent`: the visitor, the account when signed in, the step. The admin funnel page (`/admin/funnel`, `?days=7|30|90`) joins a visitor to the account it makes — a visitor whose row ever carries an account is that account, on every device — and counts each visitor once per step: the visitors first seen in the window, the accounts made, the subscribed, the conversion; the waterfall of visitors that reached each step with the share of new visitors and of the step before; the furthest step reached, which is where each visitor stopped; new visitors by day stacked by furthest step; and the latest visitors with the steps they reached. The daily cron deletes rows older than 365 days.

Corpus-scope contradiction/gap detection is the differentiating feature. Implementation: the digest carries all notes (with IDs) in one prompt; output JSON list of `{noteIds[], issue, explanation}`; render as clickable cards.

---

## 8. Build Phases (vertical slices, in order)

Each phase must be fully working end-to-end before starting the next.

### Phase 1 — Notebook outliner (no AI, no documents)
- CRUD: notebooks, sections (drag-reorder, one nesting level), manual notes.
- Notes full-page view. Markdown rendering.
- **Done when:** can create a notebook, build a section skeleton, write/reorder notes, reload with everything intact.

### Phase 2 — Documents: ingest, render, attach
- Upload PDF / paste URL → parse to blocks → persist → render in reader pane.
- PDFs up to 50 MB. Vercel caps a request body at about 4.5 MB, so the client splits bigger files into chunks (`/api/uploads`) and `/api/uploads/complete` assembles them into the same ingest path.
- Attach documents to notebooks. Split view shell (reader left, notes drawer right).
- URL replica fidelity (`lib/parse/url.ts`, parser version 16): the walk reads the page's baked layout (`data-unitos-hidden`, `data-align`, `data-style`, `data-font-size`, `data-font`, `data-box`, from `lib/parse/figure-style.ts`) and works without it.
