The place's own waits — the spinner after 1.2s, not-loading after 15s — start when the video starts loading, never before.

Import PDF still judges every PDF (§16), and an image always imports as a handwritten page — both decided automatically, server-side, on the parsed content alone; nothing in the box steers them.

**Imports.** While the switch is on (§30), a PDF judged an article, a web page, a Markdown or text file, and a Word file become an import: rich text that opens in the page editor. An add past the size guard (the chosen pages' rows alone) stays a block document; the box says "Too long for the page editor, so it opens in the reader instead." (a PDF's line adds "Add it again and choose its pages.") and stays open until Close.

**The range step.** A video or audio file has one step the box waits on: once its bytes are up and before the add completes, the box plays the file from the browser's own copy (`components/reader/media-range.tsx`) and shows two handles over its length; the reader moves them to keep part of the recording, hearing the preview seek to the handle, and presses Import {start}–{end}, or Import the whole recording. The part goes to `POST /api/uploads/complete` as `clipStart` and `clipEnd` (seconds) and is stored on `VideoAsset.clipStart`/`clipEnd`; null is the whole. The file is stored whole and plays whole. The transcription keeps the lines inside the part alone (`clipSegments`, `lib/video/segments.ts`, applied before the lines are cut, so Transcribe again and a pasted transcript keep to it too), so the assistant, Find, Ask, Formalize, and every derivation read that part and nothing else. A media link, a YouTube link, and a Drive pick have no step: the box has no copy to play. A re-upload the reader confirms with Add again is a new document with the part picked this time.

---

## 16. Handwritten documents

Import PDF judges each PDF (AI judgment, not a file-type rule): a computer-text article parses to text blocks as before; rough handwritten notes and drawings become a **handwritten document** — the pages themselves render in the reader, with two tools on them: conversion to text and Circle & ask. This amends §9's "no scanned-PDF OCR": image-only PDFs now land usefully as handwritten documents instead of empty articles.

### Classification (in `ingestPdf`, every PDF path — upload, chunked, Drive)

1. Parse the text layer as always. Article-scale yield (≥250 chars/page) that reads like language = article, no model call. A junk text layer — handwriting apps embed garbled recognition output ("rightrightfracleftleft…"; detected as ≥15% of characters in unbroken 25+ letter-digit runs, URLs excluded) — never counts as article yield.
2. Below that, or on junk, render sample pages (first, middle, last) and ask a vision model (`CLASSIFY_MODEL`, prompt in `/lib/prompts/classify.ts`): typeset computer text whose text layer captured it → article; printed or typed pages scanned or photographed whose content the text layer missed or garbled → scan; handwritten notes, drawings, sketches → handwritten.
3. No key or a failed call: junk = handwritten; else yield decides alone (<40 chars/page = handwritten).
4. **A scan** reads off its page images inside the add (`transcribePages` with kind scan, `lib/handwritten/convert.ts`, prompt in `/lib/prompts/scan.ts`, `SCAN_EFFORT` low): one open of the PDF draws the pages in order, four to a batch, and each batch goes to `CONVERT_MODEL` as soon as its pages are drawn — every batch at once, against the add's deadline (`modelPassDeadline`; the Drive add's limit is 300 s like the others). The box's Parsing step reads "Reading the scanned pages: 12 of 46". The words go on as an article: an import in the page editor while the switch is on (§30), its page starts the PDF's pages, else a block document. A read that fails — no model, the time up, a batch that did not convert, a page that did not render — leaves the PDF as handwritten pages with `conversionStatus` FAILED and the reason, and the box says so (§15).
5. The upload box (§15) sends no directives of its own — every PDF add goes through the judgment above. `pages` and `convert` still exist as fields on every PDF add route (`/api/documents` multipart, `/api/uploads/complete`, `/api/drive/import`), defaulting to `pages: false, convert: true` — the as-is import format (`pages: true, convert: false`) stays reachable by an API caller that sets them explicitly, just not from the reader-facing box.

Every document's menu carries Re-parse document: a text document parses its file or URL again with the current parser (`POST /api/documents/[documentId]/reparse`; an import re-parses into rich text and asks first when it was edited, §30; the progress card shows Parsing and Saving for a stored file, which uploads nothing, and a web page's steps from Fetching for a URL), a handwritten document re-makes its pages and converts again, and a video or audio document transcribes again (`POST /api/documents/[documentId]/transcribe`; the lines are replaced). A document with no file and no URL — a blank document, pasted text, a generated document — has no Re-parse row. A document's menu lists only what can run on it: Re-parse document, Move to folder (with folders), Print document (on the open document; the page editor's toolbar prints it too), and Delete document; the menu draws at the press and scrolls into view as it opens. Remove from this project is in Delete document's confirm, beside Delete document, while another project the reader can open holds the document. Remove from this project takes out this project's attachment only (`DELETE /api/documents/[documentId]?scope=project&notebookId=…`): the document stays in its other projects, every annotation with it, and Add back (History, the notice below) puts it back; the project's own work on the document — its extractions, matches, summaries, salience, formalized article, and folder, all on the attachment row — is kept in the removal's history event (`meta.kept`, `lib/documents/detach.ts`), and adding the document back to the project puts it back (`attachDocument`); every detach keeps it the same way; a link into the project to a document it no longer holds (a note's quote, an old address) opens the project with a notice at the foot of the panes — “{title}” is no longer in this project; the first document opened in its place — and Add back for editors, the same attach; a document no other project the reader can open holds has no such row, and the route refuses it (409), since removed it would be out of the reader's reach — a project of another account the reader cannot open does not count (the footprint's `openProjects`); Delete document is the way then, its confirm naming what goes. Delete document opens a confirm under the row, in the app's own look, never the browser's: where the document is — "It is also in “QA Student” and “QA Skimmer”. Delete takes it out of those projects too.", from `GET /api/documents/[documentId]/footprint`, which names the reader's projects that hold it and counts other accounts' — how many annotations go and how many notes stay, then Delete document, Cancel, and Remove from this project when another project holds it. In this project alone, the question is "Delete this document?" and names nothing more. Delete document leaves the library and every project (`DELETE /api/documents/[documentId]`; its annotations go with it, and notes that quote it keep their quotes, §5). Delete from the library, in the ⋯ of a library row in the add dialog, opens the same confirm. The judgment can be wrong, so Re-parse on a PDF asks which shape first: the row folds open to two choices — Handwritten pages (`{as: "handwritten"}`: the PDF stays as its pages, drawn as they are, and the handwriting converts again) and Computer text (`{as: "article"}`: the text reads into blocks with the current parser; a text layer with next to no text — under 40 chars/page, or junk — reads off the page images as a scan does, and a read that fails stops the re-parse with its reason, the document as it was) — with the document's current shape marked; the page reads which stored files are PDFs from their first bytes (`AttachedDocument.pdf`), so a Markdown file's row runs at once. The route takes the choice as `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/reparse {as}`. Anchors on replaced blocks re-resolve by quote or orphan visibly (§5).

### Data model

- `Document.handwritten Boolean` plus `conversionStatus ConversionStatus` (NONE → PENDING → READY | FAILED, the transcript pattern; OFF = the reader said not to convert — nothing auto-starts, the strip offers Convert to text), `conversionError`, `conversionStartedAt`. The PDF bytes stay in `Document.fileData`.
- `BlockType` gains `PAGE`: one block per PDF page at orders 0…n−1, `Block.page` the 1-based page, `Block.text` "Page N" (so chips, search, and the digest read well). `GET /api/documents/[documentId]/page/[blockId]` serves the page image.
- **Page image.** Each PAGE block's page renders once and is kept: `PageImage` (one row per PAGE block: `width`, `height`, `data` — JPEG at 1400px wide). The sizes land with the document (`storePageSizes`, in `ingestPdf` and the handwritten re-parse); the renders fill in after the response (`renderPageImages`, in `after()` on every PDF add route and the re-parse route); the page image route renders any page still without one on its first request and keeps it. The reader's `<img>` carries the stored width and height, so every page keeps its shape before its bytes arrive and the browser loads pages lazily, near the viewport only. A document from before page images gets its sizes on the reader's next open (`pageSizesFor`) and its renders on request. A re-parse recreates the blocks, and the cascade drops the rows with them.
- **The renderer.** Every page render — the page image, the classifier's samples, conversion, a scan's read, figure crops, Circle & ask, export — goes through `lib/handwritten/pages.ts`, which opens the PDF with pdfjs-dist (the pdf.js version unpdf bundles, 6.1.200) and its wasm decoders: a scan stores its pages as JBIG2, CCITT fax, or JPEG 2000 images, which unpdf's pdf.js cannot decode and drew white. The render runs on pdfjs-dist's own worker; `globalThis.pdfjsWorker` stays unpdf's, so the parse is unchanged. `PageImage.renderRev` names the renderer that drew a row (null = before pdfjs-dist): the page image route draws a PAGE block's image again when its row is older than `PAGE_RENDER_REV` (`lib/handwritten/page-url.ts`) and keeps the old bytes until the new render is in; a failed render answers the old image uncached. The URL carries the revision (`?r=2`, `pageImageUrl`), since the route's answer is cached as immutable; an offline copy saved before the revision still answers by the URL without it.
- A **page anchor** is a `Source` with `region` set (the §11 percent-coordinate shape) on a PAGE block, offsets 0/0, `quotedText` "Page N". It skips the text ladder — pages never change — and orphans only when the PAGE block is gone (shape switch).
- A **PDF figure** is a FIGURE block with `page` set and, when the parse found the figure's place on the page, `region` (the same shape): the page's graphics — a picture, a vector chart or diagram, with the labels drawn inside it (§31) — a display equation whose TeX the parse could not check (§31), or the area a "Figure N" caption names: the space above a caption set under its figure, or the drawing under a caption set over it, down to its note or source line. A display equation whose TeX passes the check is an EQUATION block of its TeX, not a figure; it keeps `page` and `region` (the box of its glyphs). `GET /api/documents/[documentId]/figure/[blockId]` renders the page and crops it to the region; without a region it serves the whole page, as before. The caption is the block's text and its only DOM text (§5): the panels' captions ("(a) …"), the figure's own, and its note or source line ("Note: …", "（出典）…"), in page order. A caption opens with its label (Figure, Fig., Table, Photo, Visualization, Image, Map, Chart, Plate, Box), its number, and a stop ("Figure 2:", "Fig 1.", "TABLE II.", "Photo 11."), or with a label and a full stop alone ("Photo. …"; a colon there names a role, "Visualization: …", and opens no caption), with a label set bold or in small caps and no stop, or with a Chinese or Japanese label and a space, the caption holding no "。"; a figure's own link printed under its caption (a DOI) ends the caption, and a table drawn as a picture takes the "TABLE N." caption beside it. Explain attaches the same crop.

### Conversion (pages → text blocks)

Conversion starts on its own when a handwritten document is added with `conversionStatus: NONE` — the text is the point; OFF (the reader's "do not convert") starts nothing — and `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/convert` runs the same job for Retry, Convert again, and the OFF strip's Convert to text (`lib/handwritten/convert.ts`, prompt in `/lib/prompts/convert.ts`, model `CONVERT_MODEL`). Pages render to images and transcribe in batches that run together, so the wall clock is about one batch. The model transcribes the author's wording verbatim and imitates the notes' formatting: headings by prominence, lists with the reader's own markers, tables with the invisible cell separators (§5 holds inside converted tables), standalone math as EQUATION TeX, drawings as one-sentence bracketed descriptions, illegible words as "[illegible]" — never a guess. Converted blocks land after the last PAGE block's order, each stamped with the page it came from (a block added after a page's words later joins that page); the reader shows pages first, then the converted text as a normal article — anchors, the selection popover, and every derivation work on it. Convert again deletes only the non-PAGE blocks, so page anchors never move, and writes the text after the last page's order, so a page removed (its order a gap) never puts text among the pages. A converted table's html is its text drawn (`tableHtml`, `lib/replica.ts`): the first row the header row when there are more, the cell separators of §5; an edit of its text draws it anew, so its words, rows, and columns change through the text PATCH and Undo gives the html back byte for byte. One failed batch fails the run with its reason — a partial text never lands silently; past 60 pages the cut is declared in a final paragraph.

The strip under the pages shows the status: Converting…, the failure reason with Retry, or the Converted text header with Convert again. A PENDING older than 10 minutes is a dead run and may start again.

### Images

An image adds like a PDF — dropped in the upload box, picked from its drop zone, or queued offline — and imports as one handwritten page. Before ingest the server wraps it into a one-page PDF (`lib/handwritten/image-pdf.ts`; png, jpg, gif, webp, bmp, told apart by their bytes in `lib/handwritten/image.ts`, the one definition the drop filter, the upload box's file input, and the documents route share) and runs `ingestPdf` with `pages` forced true server-side — no classification call, no directive to send — one PAGE block, the page image, Circle & ask, and conversion offered after, exactly as a scanned page — a new source, never a new parser. The stored bytes are the PDF, so the repeat-file ask (§15), the page image route, re-parse, and the shape switch read them as they read any PDF. A JPEG embeds as it is (DCTDecode; baseline or progressive, 8-bit, gray or RGB), and its EXIF orientation becomes the page's `/Rotate`, so a phone photo shows upright; any other format, and a JPEG outside that set, decodes through `@napi-rs/canvas` onto a white ground and stores pixel for pixel (Flate) up to 6 MP, as JPEG above that, drawn no larger than 24 MP. The page fits inside Letter and never upscales — the size sets the aspect only; every render scales to its own width. Google Drive still declines images (§14).

### Images dropped into a note or into the reader

An image dropped on a note goes into the note; an image dropped on a block of the article's text, in reading or in edit mode, goes into the article as a figure right after that block, and the drop line under the block says so while the drag is over it; an image pasted while a block is edited goes in after that block. An image dragged from another page counts as a dropped image (`lib/image-drop.ts`): its bytes are stored when the browser may read them, else the figure keeps its address. An image dragged from the page editor onto a note — a note card or the floating card, in any mode, Viewing too — goes into the note as a copy: the drag carries the image's address and alt text under its own type (`PAGE_IMAGE_DRAG_TYPE`, `lib/image-drop.ts`, written by `components/docs/insert/image.ts`), a stored image keeps its address, any other is stored first under the tier's rules, and one the browser may not read is refused with a plain message. The page keeps its image, and a drag inside the page still moves it. A transcript, slides, sheets, and a handwritten document's pages take no figure; a drop off the text, and a file that is not an image, is the project's (§15). A link dropped on a note — from a page, a bookmark, or the address bar — goes into the note too (`lib/note-links.ts`, `useNoteDrop` in use-note-drop.ts): on its own line as `[title](url)`, the anchor's text or the address as its title, and the rendered note draws a link on a line of its own as a link card (`rehypeLinkCards`, markdown.tsx) — the link's text, the site under it, the row a target. A photo fits the note (`.note-image`): the note's width at most, a capped height, never stretched past its own size, centered, on display and in the editor alike; the reader's own width from the corner handle still wins. A note card and the floating card take both drops, into the draft while editing and saved onto the note otherwise; the tray's editor takes an image by paste as before. Both paths store the bytes once: `POST /api/images` takes the file as the whole body, reads the format from the bytes (never the file name), and answers with the id; `GET /api/images/[imageId]` serves them, cached for a year — the id is a cuid and the bytes never change. A note points at that URL in its markdown (`![name](/api/images/<id>)`, the file's name as the alt so a note read without the image still says what was there); a figure block points at it in its html. `components/use-note-drop.ts` is the one drop handler both surfaces use: it tells a drag carrying files from any other, refuses what the tier does not allow before anything leaves the browser, and stops the drop there — anything that is not an image keeps travelling to the window, where a dropped file is added as a document (§15).

The note editor shows a dropped image as the image, not as its markdown: `![alt](url)` is an atom in the note grammar, like the `[block id]` chip — the whole tag shows as one thing the caret steps over, and reading the document back writes the same markdown (`lib/note-markup.ts`, `lib/note-doc.ts`). An image reaches a note three ways: dropped on the note, pasted into the editor, or chosen with the bar's image button on the notes full page (png, jpg, gif, webp, bmp; a GIF plays). It sizes itself to the column — never wider, its height following — unless the reader sets a width: the handle at the image's corner in the editor drags it (60px to the column's width; the column's width means "size itself" again), and the width rides in the image's url (`?w=320`, `imageWidth` and `withImageWidth` in lib/note-markup.ts), so the markdown stays standard and the rendered note reads the same width back. A collapsed note's one-line summary reads an image as its alt text: a preview line has no room for an image and none for a URL.

The note editor draws the page editor's squiggles (§29, Spelling suggestions and Grammar suggestions): a red one under a misspelled English word, a blue one under a grammar or wording problem of an English line, with the same card, the same switches (Show spelling suggestions, Show grammar suggestions), and the same personal dictionary; Ignore all keeps the word with the words ignored in notes. The note editor paints its text again after each edit, so the squiggles are ranges the browser draws over the text (the CSS Custom Highlight API, `components/proofing/note-proofing.tsx`), worked out again 200 ms after each paint; the browser's own check stays on only on a line that does not read as English. Accept selects the wrong words and types the replacement, so it is one edit with the note editor's own undo. A browser without the API keeps its own spelling check and draws no squiggle.

**Tiers (TIERS.md).** An account whose Premium trial ended drops images up to 5 MB; above that the account needs Unitos Premium. No tier stores an image past 25 MB. The client checks the size before the upload so the refusal is instant, and the route checks it again — the client's check is a courtesy, not the gate.

### Circle & ask

On a page, holding the mouse and dragging draws a freehand loop (the §11 draw, on a page instead of a frame). Releasing opens the Circle & ask card under the loop: a question box, two actions — **Ask** (the typed question), **Comment** — and the four highlight color dots (§6's hues, shared via `components/reader/hues.ts`). A color dot saves a **lasso highlight**: the whole PDF is a figure, and the loop persists as a highlight painted in that color (a `Note` with `color` and a page anchor; typed text rides on it as the note). The guide's Circle & ask section, right after Extract, covers handwritten pages too. Ask runs through the one pipeline (§4): `POST /api/derive` `type: EXPLAIN` with `page: {blockId, region, question}` — the one entry point EXPLAIN still serves; the server renders the page and the circled part from the stored bytes, attaches both (the page carries context, the crop carries the spot, enlarged), and the prompt's page variant answers — transcribe what is there, never guess at illegible handwriting. The answer streams into the card and persists as an annotation with a page anchor; Comment posts to `/api/annotations` `{page}`. Marks paint on the page as SVG loops carrying `data-source-id`, so source chips jump to them and flash them like text marks, and clicking a mark opens its annotation. Viewers see marks, draw nothing.

---

## 17. Unitos Premium: offline work

Offline, the open tab keeps working — reading what is loaded, and for an account with Unitos Premium active (a running trial, a granted Premium, or Ultra; `premiumActive` in `lib/tiers.ts`), the non-AI writes. Writes made offline queue in IndexedDB (`lib/offline/queue.ts`) and sync in order when the browser is back online. AI features (derivations, the assistant, the upload review, conversion) need the server and stay unavailable offline for everyone: the routes that need a model are one list (`lib/offline/ai-routes.ts`; the service worker keeps the same pattern), and a call to one of them offline answers with the plain message — "AI is off while offline. Notes, highlights, comments, and edits save on this device and sync when you are back online." — from `api()` before the request leaves, from the reader's own streams before the request leaves (`fetchWithModel`, reader-interactions.tsx: Define, Explain, Analyze, Simplify, Visualize, the assistant), and from the service worker as a 503 for a stream the client fetches itself, so every AI surface shows the same words in its own error place. While the browser is offline, the selection toolbar's rows that need a model (Define, the assistant, Explain, Analyze, Simplify, Visualize) are dimmed and their tooltip is the same plain message. The service worker answers only while the browser is offline; online, the call goes to the network untouched, so Stop reaches the server (§6). Merge with AI answers the same; Join text queues. The offline pill in the header says AI is off beside what saves.

- **The flag:** `User.tier` (PREMIUM | ULTRA) and `User.trialEndsAt` (TIERS.md). A new account is PREMIUM with the trial's end two months out; past that date on PREMIUM the account is expired and this gate closes. The operator grants for good from the admin accounts page (Tier: Ultra, Premium for good, or Premium on a trial until a date; `POST /api/admin/accounts/tier`). No billing yet. The single local reader (sign-in off) is ULTRA: there is no account to gate. Every page reads the tier once through `accountTier` (`lib/tiers.ts`) and every route reads it off the session's user row, so no surface can disagree with another. The tier shows as the tier mark beside the person's badge — the white crystal for Unitos Premium, the black diamond for Unitos Ultra (`components/tier-mark.tsx`) — the tier chip in the dashboard header, and the plan card under Plan in Settings. The reader shows no tier.
- **What queues:** note edits (auto-save included), note create and delete, section renames and reorders, replies, block text edits and deletes, highlights and comments (`/api/annotations`; the optimistic paint stays), and content uploads — a file's bytes queue whole (single-request or chunked replay, same caps as online), a URL queues as the plain ingest request. Importing needs the server to parse and store, so an offline add skips the box and queues instead.
- **Queued notes show:** a note saved offline stays in the notes while it waits. A queued note create carries its id (`lib/api.ts` adds one, `lib/notes/client-id.ts`), so a replay never makes the note twice; the notes draw every queued note write from the queue itself — a new note in its section, a note's new text, a deleted note gone — and mark each note "Waiting to sync" (its tooltip: "Saved on this device · syncs when online"), or "Not saved" once the server answered its write with an error and the queue tries it again, until the queue drains and the refresh brings the server's copy (`lib/offline/queued-notes.ts`). Read from IndexedDB, so a reload while offline still draws them. A queued note edit made from a base text replays with `onConflict: "keep"`: the queue drops a refused write, so the route puts the texts together instead of refusing (§6). A queued note write with a quote's source replays with `onSourceLost: "keep"` (records queued before this rule too): when the passage changed meanwhile, the words land without the source, and the pill says so. An edit to a note deleted meanwhile lands in a new note (§6, `lib/notes/gone.ts`). While records wait and the browser says online, a write that did not reach the server is tried again after 2, 4, 8, 15, then every 30 s: a write that failed with the browser online (a Wi-Fi handover) sends no online event. A 5xx, a 408, and a 429 wait on the same timer (Sync, below). An editor whose save waits in the queue reads Waiting to sync, not Saved; a collapsed row shows that state, and Not saved, as its icon, the words in its tooltip.
- **What does not queue:** any write whose response the caller reads (a created section's id, the style route's healed spans), everything AI, and every write on an account whose trial ended — those fail with the plain offline message.
- **Sync:** at-least-once, oldest first, writes before uploads. A record leaves the queue when the server takes it, and is dropped, with a console warning, only when the server says it is stale (a 4xx refusal: the note, the section, or the right to write is gone). A network failure, a 401, a 5xx, a 408, or a 429 keeps it: the drain runs again after a growing wait (2 s up to 30 s) for as long as it takes, so tries count time, not page loads. Each record names the account that queued it and is sent only while that account is signed in to the browser; the middleware's 409 for another account (`code: "accountChanged"`) keeps it too. Records of another account wait, not counted and not drawn, until that account signs in again. A write the same as the last one waiting for its path is queued once (a note's text write: the same text). Online, a queueable write the server answers with a 5xx, a 408, or a 429 (down or busy) queues the same way, as does the toolbox's highlight and comment (`api(…, {queue: true})`): the optimistic paint stays, the note is drawn marked Not saved, the save indicator reads "Not saved" until the queue lands it, and nothing asks the reader to do it again. A write that fails and does not queue says one line, "Not saved. Try again." ("Not saved. Try again when you are online." offline); a refusal the route words for the reader (a 4xx) shows its words; a status code or a server's raw text never shows, it goes to the console. A note's local draft (§6) stays until the server confirms its words: a queued save is not a confirmation, and the queue clears the draft when its write lands with the same text. The workspace header shows the pill: offline with the queued count, then the sync until the queue drains ("Syncing 1 change…", "Syncing 3 changes…"; a write queued after a server error syncs the same way). A held repeat add stays in the queue but is not counted: it waits for the reader's word in the ask, not for the sync. An add queued offline shows no notice of its own; the pill's count says it.
- **The save indicator** (`components/save-indicator.tsx`, `lib/save-state.ts`): one small dim line in the header of the reader and of the notes full page, left of Share — "Saving…" while a write is in flight or a note's draft waits for its save, "Saved" once every write landed, "Not saved" when the last write failed, and offline with Unitos Premium "Saved on this device · syncs when online". Every `api()` call counts, and the note editors count their drafts from the keystroke; nothing shows until the tab's first write.
- **The boundary:** offline work lives in the open tab, or in a project's offline copy. A reload while offline loads a saved project from its copy; any other page answers with the offline page. Queued records survive the reload and sync on the next online visit.
- **Offline copies (Unitos Ultra):** a project saved for offline opens without a network, like a Google Drive file made available offline. The copy is the project's pages — the reader on each document and the notes full page — and the images those pages show (an import's figure crops too: the page data names their URLs, §30), kept in the browser's cache under the project's own cache name (`unitos-project-<id>`), with one row per project in IndexedDB (`lib/offline/saved.ts`, store `saved`). Save for offline is an icon in the reader's header (where Add context stood; its name in the tooltip; below md a row of the bottom bar's More menu) and an item in the project's ⋯ menu on the dashboard; saved, the icon fills and the card shows the Offline badge, and a press on either removes the copy. A save shows the bottom progress bar (`components/progress-bar.tsx`): pages, then images, counted as each fetch lands. The bar and the one-line toasts after it sit over the phone's bottom bar, never on it (`BOTTOM_STATUS`, the notes pill's place). Offered to every account; a non-Ultra press answers with the plain Ultra message, and the route that lists what to save (`GET /api/notebooks/<id>/offline`) answers 403, so no copy is made. The service worker (`public/sw.js`, registered by `components/offline/service-worker.tsx` in production) caches the app's static files as they load and the offline page (`/offline`) at install; a page load is network first, and while online it refreshes any page or image a copy holds; offline, the copy answers, and a URL no copy holds answers with the offline page, which lists this account's saved projects from IndexedDB and goes to Projects by itself when the browser is back online. The dashboard refreshes copies older than an hour in the background; a copy whose project is gone or no longer readable is removed. Sign out removes the account's copies. Videos and the assistant's pages are not part of a copy: a video document's transcript and notes open, the video itself needs the network. Offline writes in a saved project queue as above (Unitos Premium, which Ultra holds).

---

## 18. Notifications

The admin sends notifications to accounts; the admin never has access to an account. `/admin` is the operator's console — feedback, digest, usage, notifications — behind its own password (§2). It holds no session for any account, opens no account, and changes nothing on one: no impersonation, no profile edits, no premium toggle. The notification is the one thing the admin sends into an account, and it flows one way — the recipient reads and dismisses; nothing comes back. Feedback is the reader's one message to the admin, and the admin's reply to it is a notification too (kind `feedback`).

**Where Feedback is:** a pill at the window's bottom right, above the phone's bottom bar. The reader has no pill: it lay on the tray's last row, the article's last lines, and on a short screen the rail's Extract. There Feedback is a button in the guide's head (?), beside its ✕, on md+, and the last row of the More menu (the last button of the bottom bar) below md; Reader view holds only views. On a phone's dashboard, on a phone's notes and annotations full pages, and on Settings on a phone the pill would lie on a card's ⋯, a row's controls, or the plan's words: there Feedback is a button in the header. Every one is named Feedback and opens the same form; its ✕ at the top right closes it and keeps the typed words.

**Photos and links on feedback:** the feedback form takes photos (Add photo, a paste into the message, at most 6; each uploads through `POST /api/images` as a note's image does, and the route checks every id is one this account uploaded) and links (Add link, absolute http(s) URLs, at most 10). `Feedback.images` holds the image ids, `Feedback.links` the URLs; the inbox shows the photos as thumbnails that open whole and the links as links, and `GET /api/admin/feedback` returns both.

**The feedback pipeline** (`.claude/skills/feedback-pipeline`): a daily Routine reads the inbox (`GET /api/admin/feedback?status=new,seen&take=2000`), clusters the requests that fit this spec, makes each change on a `feedback/<slug>` branch, and opens one pull request per change with the feedback quoted and a review checklist, marking the feedback `seen`. A person reviews and merges; Vercel deploys `main`. The next run finds the merged pull request by its `Feedback-Ids:` line, replies to each sender ("Shipped: …", the admin's reply notification) and marks the feedback `resolved`. The pipeline never pushes to `main`, never merges, never replies before the change is on `main`, and opens at most five pull requests per run. Feedback it cannot place — vague, out of scope, or rejected by a closed pull request — keeps its status for a person.

### Data model additions

```prisma
model Notification {
  id         String   @id @default(cuid())
  kind       String   @default("update") // "update" | "account" | "feedback"
  title      String
  body       String   // markdown
  feedbackId String?  // kind "feedback": the feedback this replies to; SetNull when that feedback goes
  feedback   Feedback? @relation(fields: [feedbackId], references: [id], onDelete: SetNull)
  recipients NotificationRecipient[]
  createdAt  DateTime @default(now())
}

// Feedback gains the account that sent it:
//   userId  String?  // soft reference like Notebook.userId; null = sent signed out, no reply possible
//   replies Notification[]

model NotificationRecipient {
  id             String       @id @default(cuid())
  notificationId String
  notification   Notification @relation(fields: [notificationId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  userId         String       // soft reference like Notebook.userId; "user-1" = the local reader
  dismissedAt    DateTime?    // null = open on the dashboard
  @@unique([notificationId, userId])
}
```

- **Kinds:** `update` — an update to Unitos (a new function, a changed behavior); `account` — a change made to the account (Unitos Premium turned on, a limit changed); `feedback` — a reply to feedback the account sent (made only by Reply in the feedback inbox, never composed on the notifications page). The kind renders as a chip on both sides.
- **Sending** (`/admin/notifications`, `POST /api/admin/notifications`): kind, title, body, recipients — every account, or accounts chosen from the list (name and email, nothing else; `lib/notifications.ts` is the admin's whole view of accounts). One `Notification` row and one `NotificationRecipient` row per recipient. With sign-in off the local reader is the one account. The page lists every send, newest first, with its recipient count and how many dismissed it; Delete (`DELETE /api/admin/notifications`) removes a send for every recipient.
- **Receiving:** the dashboard shows the account's open notifications as one card above Projects, in the page's flow, so it covers no project card (`components/works/notifications.tsx`): the newest open notification: kind, "1 of 3" when more are open, Dismiss all and Dismiss on one row; then the title and the date; then the body (markdown), folded to two lines. A press on a folded body opens it whole, and a press again folds it. Dismiss (`PATCH /api/notifications/[id]`, the recipient only) stamps `dismissedAt` and shows the next; Dismiss all, shown when more than one is open, dismisses every one. The dashboard itself lists no notifications. Dismissed rows stay, so the admin's count holds; only the admin's Delete removes them.
- **Replying to feedback** (`/admin`, `POST /api/admin/feedback`): `POST /api/feedback` records `Feedback.userId`, the account that sent it (signed out: null). Reply writes one `Notification` of kind `feedback` — title the feedback's message on one line, body the reply (markdown), `feedbackId` the feedback — with one `NotificationRecipient`, the account that sent it; new feedback turns seen. The inbox names the account under each feedback and lists its replies with whether the account dismissed each; the card reads "Reply to your feedback", the feedback's message, then the reply. Feedback with no account, or an account the recipient list does not know, cannot be replied to (400). Delete on the notifications page removes a reply like any send. The first account to sign in adopts the local reader's feedback and notifications with the rest of its data (§2).
- **Release notifications** (`lib/releases.ts`, `lib/releases-server.ts`): a **release** is one shipped set of new functions, one entry in `RELEASES` — its date, the dictionary keys of its title and body, and the controls it added. On every dashboard open, `ensureReleaseNotifications` makes sure that for every release whose day ends after the account was made, the notification row for the account's language exists (`Notification.releaseKey` `<release>:<lang>`, kind `update`, dated the day it shipped, written the first time an account in that language opens the dashboard after the deploy) and the account is a recipient of it; a dismissed row is never reset; the local reader is told of every release. So the news of a deploy lands on every dashboard with the deploy, with no admin send. The admin's notifications page lists a release send like any other.
- **The New glow** (`components/new-feature.tsx`, `useNewFeature(<id>)`): every control a release added — Collapse, the annotations full page's four arrows, By document, Conversations, the Define row — glows with a pulsing sage ring and a New pill until the reader presses it, for `NEW_GLOW_DAYS` (45) after the release shipped; this browser remembers what was pressed (`unitos-new-seen`), and the server's render shows no glow. The guide (`components/guide-dialog.tsx`) explains every one of them.
- **The boundary, enforced:** the admin routes touch `Notification`, `NotificationRecipient`, and `Feedback` only; no admin route reads or writes `User`, `Session`, `ReaderProfile`, or a corpus. No email: the notification lives in the app.

---

## 19. Translation

The reader's language (the `dissect-lang` cookie, §2) and a document's language can differ: a Chinese paper opened in the English reader, an English podcast transcript in the Chinese reader. Translation closes the gap through DeepL — the one translation provider, Chinese and English both ways (`DEEPL_API_KEY`; a Free API key ends in `:fx` and takes the free host on its own, any other key the Pro host; `DEEPL_API_URL` points a local run at a stand-in). Nothing here is a model call of the app's own: DeepL translates, the app caches and shows.

- **The offer.** When `DEEPL_API_KEY` is set, a bar above the article (above a media document's transcript, and over the first page in the page editor, §29) says which language the document is in and offers Translate to the reader's language. The document's language is judged from its characters (`lib/translate/detect.ts`: Chinese when a fifth or more of the letters are CJK, English when the letters are Latin, unknown otherwise — no model call); a document already in the reader's language gets no offer, an unknown one is offered anyway, since DeepL detects the source itself.
- **The translation.** `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/translate` `{lang}` (editor) translates every PARAGRAPH, HEADING, LIST, TRANSCRIPT, TABLE (plain text under 5,000 characters), and FIGURE caption block the document has, in batches of at most 50 texts and about 100 KB (DeepL's limits), and stores each as a `BlockTranslation` row keyed by block and language with a hash of the block text it came from. A block edited since reads as stale and translates again on the next Translate; everything else is served from the cache, so a document costs its characters once per language. `GET …/translate?lang=` answers the cache (viewer). Usage telemetry records the characters sent, at $25 per million (provider `deepl`).
- **The reading.** Each translation reads under its block, quieter, marked by a rule, in the reader's language; in the transcript a paragraph's lines read together under it. Anchors, highlights, links, and every tool stay on the original text — the translation is a layer, never a replacement. Hide translation puts the article back; the choice is remembered per document in the browser, and a remembered document shows its cached translation again on open without a DeepL call. Edit mode shows no translations. In the page editor (§29) each paragraph's translation reads under it as a decoration drawn from the paragraph's `data-translation` (`components/docs/layer/reading.ts`, `css/reading.css`), never words of the page: it is not selected, copied, or counted. It moves to the next page with the paragraph's last line and prints as the block reader's does. A paragraph whose words changed since shows its words alone until Translate writes it again. A translation that reads the same as its paragraph (a number, a name) is not drawn. Viewing shows the translations; Editing and Suggesting show none.
- **Not translated.** Notes, annotations, and the assistant's answers: those are written in the reader's language already (§4). Code and equations. A table past the plain-text cap.

---

## 20. Visualize (Unitos Ultra)

Visualize turns a selected passage into one picture that delivers its core idea at a glance: a directed diagram for named things and the relations between them, a simulation for a law of change — the state it governs evolving through time, integrated by the server — a still picture for a mechanism, a physical arrangement, or the shape a formula describes, or a short animation for a process whose order is the point. It is a derivation (§4, type `VISUALIZE`, template `lib/prompts/visualize.ts`), Unitos Ultra only (TIERS.md; the toolbar shows the tool to every account, with the tier mark on its row for an account without Unitos Ultra, and answers a non-Ultra click with the plain Ultra message; the route answers 403), and runs at the highest effort on the model that draws best (`VISUALIZE_MODEL`, `VISUALIZE_EFFORT` in `lib/derive/config.ts`: Claude Opus 5.5, `max`), because the picture has to be faithful or refused.

- **The passage in the article comes first.** The model reads the whole document (the cached prefix, §2) and says what the passage does there: it states a problem; extends a problem stated earlier; solves one; offers an alternative to a structure, a method, or an explanation given earlier; is one step of a process the article builds; is an example of a claim made earlier; or stands on its own. The core idea is the passage's point as it sits in the article — what it adds to what the article set up. When the passage answers, extends, or replaces something stated elsewhere, the picture shows both — the problem and this solution, the earlier structure and this alternative — with the passage's own part standing out, because a reader who sees this passage's part alone does not see what it is for. What the article states elsewhere is there to be drawn: drawing it is not inventing. In a diagram every node carries a `role`, `passage` or `article`; when a diagram holds both, the passage's nodes draw in the accent on the fill and the article's faint — a muted dashed outline on the paper, muted text — and a diagram of the passage alone draws plain. In a picture the passage's part takes the accents and the rest the ink. The caption says the relation.
- **The judgment.** With the passage placed, the model names its core idea in one sentence, works out both ways to draw it, and draws only when all four hold: a picture shows this better than words; the picture needs nothing the passage, its context, or the article elsewhere does not state; a reader who sees the picture without the passage takes away that core idea and not another; and the model is certain this picture is the best way to show it, not merely a possible way. Otherwise it declines. A decline is a valid outcome: the card says there is not enough certainty for a picture that carries the core idea of this passage, shows the model's reason — which names what was tried, so the reader knows the passage was worked on and not skipped — and points at the assistant, Explain, and Simplify; nothing persists.
- **The two ways to draw, and the analogy among them.** The literal picture draws the passage's own structure. The analogy draws a familiar thing that shares the idea's shape. A passage whose own structure carries the idea takes the literal picture — an analogy there is only a second thing to understand. A passage whose idea is abstract, unfamiliar, or has no drawable structure of its own is where the analogy earns its place, and is often the difference between a picture and a refusal. The model names three candidate analogies and takes the one that passes all five: every part maps to a part of the passage and it can say which to which; it adds nothing the passage contradicts; the reader knows the analogue already, without being taught it (the reader context says what they know — an analogy from their own field beats a general one); it breaks outside what the passage claims; and it carries the idea better than the literal picture would. No candidate passes all five, no analogy: the literal picture, or a refusal. In a diagram the node label is the analogue's part and its detail is what that part stands for, so the mapping reads off the picture; the caption names the analogy and says what stands for what.
- **The equation in motion.** When the passage is a law of change — an equation with a time derivative, or a description of how a state evolves under a rule — the picture is the state evolving under that law, the way a textbook animates the heat equation or a wave packet, never a drawing of it: the model could not write a true frame, so it writes the spec and the server integrates (`lib/derive/simulate.ts`). The spec is `{law, equation?, x0, x1, initial, velocity?, potential?, momentum, boundary, coefficient, variables?, duration, xLabel?, uLabel?, tUnit?, levels?}`: a variable may carry a `label` for the legend, `tUnit` is the unit the clock shows, and `levels` are up to three values the passage names (a threshold, the temperature the rod settles at), each a dashed line across the plot with its label, there from the first frame. The laws: `heat` (u_t = α u_xx; Crank–Nicolson on a fixed or insulated domain, explicit on a periodic one), `wave` (u_tt = c² u_xx; leapfrog inside the CFL bound, from the initial state and velocity), `advection` (u_t + c u_x = 0; the exact solution u₀(x − ct), so a pulse travels without a scheme's smear), `schrodinger` (iψ_t = −a ψ_xx + Vψ with ħ = 1; Crank–Nicolson in the complex plane on a box or an insulated domain, on a grid the physics sets — a third of a radian of phase per step at most, eight points across the potential's narrowest feature, sixteen across the packet, 128 to 2,048 points — so a barrier's transmission is the equation's and not the grid's; the state is initial(x)·e^{ikx}; drawn from a smoothed 200-point copy as |ψ|², Re ψ faint under it where a wavelength spans six points or more, V dashed), and `ode` (dv/dt = f(t, v…) for up to four variables; fourth-order Runge–Kutta; drawn as curves against time revealed by a curtain whose edge is the present). Boundaries: `fixed` holds the ends at their t = 0 values, `insulated` passes no flux, `periodic` makes the ends one point. Formulas are a small language of their own (`compileFormula`: numbers, the law's variables, arithmetic, comparisons, pi and e, and a fixed set of functions with `step` and `pulse` for square starts) parsed once and never evaluated as code; a formula that does not parse, a state that is not finite, or a duration the scheme cannot cover fails the run with the reason. The drawing: 36 frames over an 8-second loop as one SMIL `d` animation on a 480×320 plot with axes at round bounds (0 and 100, not −8 and 108; thousands grouped, no exponent under a million), the passage's labels, the law as the passage writes it above — one line at 16 down to 14 when it fits, else two lines, the plot moved down to make room — the state at t = 0 kept faint under the motion, the boundary marked (dots for held ends, bars for insulated, the word for periodic), and a time bar from t = 0 to the duration. Coordinates are whole pixels in x, so a field law is about 75k characters and the wavefunction about 230k — under the SVG ceiling. The check reads a simulation's spec, not its frames.
- **Output contract.** JSON `{judgment: {structure, certain, reason}, visual: null | {kind: 'diagram' | 'simulation' | 'picture' | 'animation', caption, diagram?, simulation?, svg?}}`, validated strictly (`lib/derive/visualize.ts`), retried once on a failed parse like every JSON derivation. `certain: false` or a null visual is a decline. A diagram is a spec — `{direction: 'right' | 'down', nodes: [{id, label, detail?, role?}], edges: [{from, to, label?}]}`, 3 to 12 nodes, labels at most 6 words — that the server lays out itself. A picture or an animation is SVG source the model writes to the prompt's rules (a 480-wide viewBox, the reader's palette, text at 14 to 20, SMIL for motion) and the server reduces before storing: only the listed elements survive (shapes, text, defs, gradients, clip paths, and the SMIL animation elements), no script, foreignObject, image, style element, event attribute, or reference outside the file — a `url()` that does not name a fragment of the file is dropped with its attribute; a link keeps what it wraps; the root keeps its viewBox and loses width and height; every color attribute is normalized to lower-case six-digit hex. An SVG that is not well-formed, has no viewBox, or is empty fails the run with the reason. The SVG ceiling is the same number in the contract and in the reduction (300000 characters), so an SVG past it is caught by validation and retried with the reason rather than reduced and then thrown away.
- **The layout.** Ranks by longest path, a cycle's closing edge marked and arced around the nodes. An edge that skips ranks travels through one waypoint per rank it passes, and the waypoints order and place with the boxes, so a long edge bends through the gaps instead of crossing the boxes between its ends. Ordering is eight sweeps, down and up, each rank sorting by the mean seat of its neighbours in the rank before or after; the arrangement with the fewest crossing edges wins and the model's order breaks every tie. An edge's label is measured with the nodes, and the waypoint that carries it is the size of that label, so the rank keeps room for it and no label lands on a box; the gap between two ranks holds the widest label of the edges that cross it, with an arrowhead's length on either side, twice that where two labeled edges leave or enter one box, and two labels that would meet slide apart along their own curves. An edge from a node to itself draws as a loop beside its box, its box keeping the room for the loop and its label. A back edge's arc lifts from the outermost box of the ranks it spans, never through one. Boxes are sized from measured text (`@napi-rs/canvas`, an estimate without it); details and edge labels are 13, labels 15. A word in a spaced script is never cut; a run of CJK wraps by characters. The layout is chosen for the card, which is 320 px wide: the model's direction with the widest text first, then narrower text, then the other direction — the first no wider than 460 units (a detail line at 9 px or more in the card) wins, else the narrowest that is not a long scroll. The viewBox is the drawing's own bounds — every box, every label, the far side of every back edge's arc — so nothing is cut.
- **The check.** A second call on the same model at the same effort (`VISUALIZE_CHECK`, `lib/prompts/visualize-check.ts`), after the picture is drawn and laid out. It is the one pass that sees the finished picture: the pass that drew it never does — a diagram's layout is the server's, and a picture's SVG has been through the reduction, so what the reader gets is not what the model wrote. It reads the picture back, with the whole document above it — a simulation by its spec, since its frames are the server's — against five questions (the core idea as the passage sits in the article, every part mapping to the passage or the article, nothing missing that the reduction cut, legible at 320 px, the caption) and answers `{keep, reason, visual?}`: keep it, replace it whole, or withdraw it — a withdrawal declines the run with its reason, because a picture that misleads is worse than no picture. A check that fails, or a replacement that does not render, leaves the picture as drawn: the draft is the work and a second call that did not answer never costs it. A picture past `VISUALIZE_CHECK_MAX_SVG` is kept as drawn, unchecked — reading back an SVG that large costs more than the check is worth.
- **Light and dark.** A stored picture shows in an `<img>`, where the page's theme tokens do not reach it, so the SVG carries its own dark palette (`lib/derive/visual-palette.ts`): one style block re-points the exact values the diagram and the simulation draw with and the prompt gives the model, under `prefers-color-scheme: dark` (the image route's CSP allows an inline style and nothing else). The model never writes a style element; the server always does, after the reduction. A color the model writes another way stays as drawn, which is why the prompt asks for the palette's exact values and the reduction normalizes what it can.
- **Destination.** The SVG is stored as an `ImageAsset` (`image/svg+xml`, the account's; `/api/images/[imageId]` serves it with a Content-Security-Policy that runs nothing) and one annotation lands in the hidden Annotations section with `derivationType: VISUALIZE`, one source per segment of the selection, and the markdown `![caption](/api/images/<id>)` followed by the caption in italics. The reader's card is EXPLAIN's card titled Visualization; Delete removes the annotation and its mark.
- **The viewer.** A visualization opens large in the app, never in a new tab: one viewer for the workspace (`components/reader/visualization-viewer.tsx`), the picture at up to 78vh over a dimmed page with its caption under it, closed by Escape, the backdrop, or ✕. Every visualization image on the page opens it — the picture in the card, in the Annotations tab, in a note — because `Markdown` renders an image under `/api/images/` as a button that raises one event (`dissect:open-visualization`, the image and its caption, which is the alt text). The card has no Open button of its own: the picture is it. Its Expand shows once it holds a conversation. An animation plays in the viewer as it does in the card. The mark carries the tool's symbol, reopens the card from the stored markdown, and lists under Visualizations in the Annotations tab. An animation plays wherever the image shows: SMIL runs inside an `<img>`.
- **The run.** Behind the heartbeat stream (§4): spaces while the model reasons, then `{ok, noteId, kind, caption, content}` or `{ok, declined, reason}`, or the error token with the reason. Cancel aborts the model call and persists nothing. Regenerate (§4) draws the passage again; a regenerate that declines — the judgment's, or the check's — keeps the picture that stands, because it stored nothing to replace it with.

## 21. Tool conversations and the log

Every AI tool's card can continue into a conversation, and every conversation anchored in the text — a tool's, or the assistant's from the selection popover — shows its condensed log when the reader hovers its mark. Continuing a tool's output into a conversation is Unitos Ultra (TIERS.md); the log is not gated.

- **Continue.** An Explain, Simplify, Analyze, or Visualize card, once its output is saved, carries one pill on its foot row, beside the rating and Save as note: Continue, a word only, so the row holds it; its tooltip names it whole, Continue in a conversation. Save as note on a card that holds a conversation saves the output and every turn: the reader's messages as the question, the output and each answer as the answer. It is offered to every account — a non-Ultra account sees it with the plain Ultra badge (like Visualize's own entry in the toolbar), and a press answers with the toast that this is Unitos Ultra, opening nothing; a turn sent anyway (`toolNoteId` set) answers 403. For an Ultra account, pressing it turns the foot into a text box and the card into Explain+ (Simplify+, Analyze+, Visualize+): the same card, the tool's symbol with a plus, the output above, the turns under it in the same scroll body, the box at the bottom. Every turn goes deeper on the output: the server (`/api/assistant/act` with `toolNoteId`) reads the selection from the annotation's own sources, puts the output in the prompt as what the reader ran and got, and answers from the output, the selection, and the document, adding to the output and never repeating it. It is the assistant's own route, so a turn can still propose actions, which go through the plan card as ever. Stop mid-turn, closing the card, and Delete work as on the assistant card. A conversation started while Ultra, read after a downgrade, stays readable with no box to send more.
- **Where the turns live.** On the tool's own annotation: `Note.conversation`, `[{role, content}]` oldest first, at most 60 turns. The output stays in `content`, so Simplify's sentence mirroring, the gist, and the Annotations tab read it as before; the Annotations tab shows the turns under the output, labeled Conversation, and the digest renders them after the output in the assistant conversation's transcript format. The assistant's own conversation keeps its transcript in `content` (§4); `lib/conversation.ts` reads both forms into one shape. Deleting the annotation deletes the conversation.
- **The mark.** The tool's symbol at the end of the highlighted text (`.mark-chip`) and on a figure's side label carries the plus once the annotation holds turns (`Highlight.plus`; `ToolSymbol` in block-view.tsx draws every symbol with or without it), and its tip says the card opens with its conversation. The card reopens from the mark with the turns and the box open. An open card's turns count before the refresh delivers the server's copy, and the turns a card sent this session are kept by note id (`toolConversationsRef`, reader-interactions.tsx): the page's copy arrives with the refresh, which takes seconds on a large document, and a card closed and reopened before then shows the session's copy — whichever copy is longer is the newer one. Without this the reopened card showed the output alone, and the conversation looked lost.
- **Expand.** Every conversation card carries Expand at its right — the assistant's card, and a tool's card once it holds turns or output. It opens the conversation whole over the pane: the turns in one wide column that scrolls, the box at the foot, always in reach. The assistant's view draws the card's own turns, wider: each answer with its rating and Save as note, the plan under its answer (shown in the view while it is open), the queue (`assistantTurns`, reader-interactions.tsx). The focus goes with the reader: in the card's box, Expand moves it to the view's box, and Close moves it back. A tool's output is the conversation's first message. The card stays open under the view, out of sight (no card covers the view), so the same box sends from either place, and Close (or Escape) puts the pane back at the scroll position it left. The Annotations tab carries the same Expand on every annotation that holds a conversation; a stored conversation read there has no box.
- **How tall a card gets.** A card grows with its content up to the pane's height, then its body scrolls inside it (§6). A card anchored low in the pane still grew past the pane's bottom edge, and the box at its foot went out of reach — a long conversation kept pushing it further down. So a card that grows past the pane's foot keeps its top and stops there, its body scrolling inside and its box at its foot in view, when at least 160 px (`CAP_MIN`) of the pane is under its top: the answer the reader is reading never climbs as it lands. A card with less room than that, or one that opened tall (reopened from its mark with its turns), is lifted until its foot is back inside the pane; a card taller than the pane sits at the pane's top, under the article's band (Contents, Collapse and Extract stay uncovered), and scrolls inside. The lift stops under the foot of a card above it in the same column: the lifted card runs on below the pane's edge rather than cover that card. Only a card the reader can see moves: one scrolled away stays at its anchor. Expand is the way out when the conversation is longer than a card can hold.
- **The log.** Hovering a mark whose annotation holds a conversation — the tool's or the assistant's — shows the log card where the conversation's card would open (the same slot the card claims, the same connector line; `data-log-card`, never a side card, so it takes no slot and pushes nothing): one line per message, the reader's lines as chat bubbles, the assistant's plain, so the conversation's point is clear at a glance. The pointer has to rest on the mark for 320 ms; the card stays while the pointer is on the mark or the card and leaves 220 ms after it goes; a scroll or a click closes it, and clicking the mark opens the card in its place. An open card shows no log for its own conversation.
- **Writing the log.** `POST /api/notes/[noteId]/log` (viewer) answers the stored log when its `turns` matches the conversation's message count, else writes one (`lib/notes/conversation-log.ts`, `lib/prompts/conversation-log.ts`, the gist's model and effort): one line per message, each one complete statement that finishes its point, at most 25 words and 150 characters (65 in Chinese), in the message's own language, the reader's lines saying what the reader asked, the assistant's the answer's core point; a message the model skips shows its first words. A line over the cap is cut at the last sentence end that fits, else at the last clause break with an ellipsis, so a log line never stops mid-thought. For a tool conversation the output is the first message logged. Stored on `Note.log` as `{turns, v, lines: [{role, text}]}`, where `v` is the line rules the log was written under (`LOG_VERSION`): a log stored under older rules is written again on the next hover, so a change to the rules reaches logs already stored. The reader caches it per conversation length for the session, so a hover asks once. Usage telemetry records the call as `log`.

---

## 22. Stitch

**Stitch** is the assistant over the project's documents, from the graph (§13): one command across two or more documents — gather every passage on a topic into a new page, connect the passages that answer a question, find where the documents contradict each other, write one page that combines them. Every document of the project is its own page; the graph is where they are read together.

- **Where it lives.** The Stitch box sits at the foot of the graph overlay (`components/graph/stitch-box.tsx`), open when the graph opens and folding to a pill; the graph needs two documents for it to show. Beside the canvas, Generated content (`components/graph/generated-list.tsx`) folds out like Recommended links: every page Stitch wrote for the project, newest first, with the command that made it; a row opens it in the reader, and Delete removes it like any document from the library (§5: its annotations go with it; notes that quote it keep their quotes). One folded list shows at a time. The legend sits at the canvas's top left, clear of the box; a picked node stays lit while another node is spotlighted. Escape takes one layer at a time: in the box it leaves the box, the typed command kept; then an open list beside the canvas closes; then the graph. The graph is a step in the browser's history (`components/graph/graph-history.ts`): Back closes it and the document stays open; a document opened from the graph takes the graph's place, so Back from that document returns to the one read before. The typed command and the pick are kept per project for the browser tab (session storage), beside the conversation, so Escape, a closed graph, or a reload never throws them away.
- **Which documents.** Every attached document, or the documents picked in the graph: a ⇧-click (or ⌘/Ctrl-click) on a node picks it, and Pick documents in the box turns every click into a pick until Done; a picked node draws a clay ring, and the box lists the pick as chips (✕ drops one; Clear drops them all) and says what the command reads — "Every document (5)" or "3 documents picked". Send ends picking. A document that leaves the project leaves the pick.
- **The route.** `POST /api/notebooks/[notebookId]/stitch` `{command, history, documentIds?}` (`lib/graph/stitch.ts`, prompts in `/lib/prompts/stitch.ts`, `STITCH_MODEL`, usage under `stitch`; the heartbeat stream, §4). `documentIds` names the pick, distinct and attached to the project; absent, every attached document. Fewer than two attached documents is refused. Editor role. The model reads and writes block aliases, never the stored ids: a document's letter by its place in the read (A, B, … Z, AA) is its `[document <letter>]` tag, and a block's alias is the letter and the block's number in the document, in reading order (`[block B12]`); an alias is a few tokens where a stored id is a dozen, and copied right far more often, and the route resolves every alias (`blockByRef`, aliases and stored ids alike) and rewrites the reply's `[block …]` tags to the stored ids before the reply leaves (`replyWithIds`). A video or audio document reads as its transcript lines (the `TRANSCRIPT` blocks, each tagged with its seconds; the `VIDEO` block is the title, not content) and a handwritten document as its converted text (the `PAGE` blocks are page numbers, not content). A document with nothing to read — its transcript pending, stale, failed, or never run; its conversion pending, failed, or never run; or no text — is declared to the model as `(nothing to read: …)` with the reason, and every prompt names it as not read: the model never cites it and says so in the reply.
- **The skeleton.** The cost of a command must scale with what the command needs, not with the project, so no pass reads the documents' text whole. Every document carries a **skeleton** (`Document.skeleton`, `lib/graph/skeleton.ts`, prompt in `/lib/prompts/skeleton.ts`, `SKELETON_MODEL` at `SKELETON_EFFORT` (low: a reading), usage under `skeleton`): the document collapsed to about a tenth of its length — a gist of one sentence, one summary of one to three sentences per part of the contents (§26; built first when the document has none, the headings when the build fails), and one line per block, at most 40 words, every claim, number, name, and term kept as printed and the wording dropped; a heading is its text, a figure its caption and numbers, a transcript line what was said, a reference entry its first author and year. Built one model call per window of `SKELETON_WINDOW_CHARS` (100,000) of readable text, the windows at once, each window's system message the cached document prefix over its blocks; a line the model skips is the block's own first words, cut at a sentence end. Each line carries the hash of its block's text (`blockHash`), and that is how the skeleton follows the document: a block whose text changed, or a new block, reads as its own first words in place of its line (`currentSkeleton`, no model call), and when more than `SKELETON_STALE_FRACTION` (a tenth) of the document's characters are changed, new, or removed (`skeletonDrift`: a removed block's line counts at ten times its length, since the text is gone) the skeleton is rebuilt whole. The build runs in the background (`refreshSkeleton`, in `after()`) after an add lands a text document, after a re-parse (which also clears the stored skeleton, its ids being gone), when a transcript or a conversion lands its text, and after every block edit or removal — the drift check is all an edit under a tenth costs; a build older than `SKELETON_STALE_MS` is a dead run and may start again. Stitch takes the stored skeleton when under a tenth changed and builds a missing or stale one at once (`ensureSkeleton`); a build that fails reads the document as its first words, so a command still runs. The skeleton stays byte-identical from turn to turn while the document does, so the select pass's prefix caches per set of documents (§2).
- **The passes.** Documents under `STITCH_WHOLE_THRESHOLD` (120,000 characters) together skip the reading passes: the answer pass reads them whole. Past it, up to three passes at `STITCH_MODEL`. (1) The select pass reads every document's skeleton in one call — the gist, each part's summary above its first line, the lines under their aliases (`skeletonSystem`) — at `STITCH_SELECT_EFFORT` (low) and names the blocks the command needs: aliases most relevant first, consecutive blocks as one range (`B10-B15`, `expandPick`), from every document that bears on the command. (2) When the skeletons together run past `STITCH_SKELETON_BUDGET` (200,000 characters), the select pass reads every line in groups of about `STITCH_SKELETON_GROUP` (60,000) characters, in document order, a long document's lines over several groups, the groups six at a time (`skeletonGroups`): no line goes unread, and no call reads more than a few documents' worth. Only when the skeletons run past `STITCH_GROUPED_MAX` (1,200,000 characters, about 300 articles) does a route pass run first at `STITCH_ROUTE_EFFORT` (low): it reads only the gists and the part summaries (`routeSystem`), each part tagged `[part at <alias>]` with its first block's alias, and names the parts (up to 80); the select pass then reads those parts' lines only, the gaps declared, a document the route pass named nothing of keeping every line. (3) When those lines still run past the budget, they are ranked against the command (`lib/graph/rank.ts`: BM25 over the line's text and its part's title, Latin script by word, CJK by character bigram, no index and no model) and cut to it — every document's top lines first, up to a quarter share, then the rest most relevant first (`cutLines`) — so no document goes unread. A route pass that fails ranks every line; a select pass that fails, or names nothing of a document, reads that document's opening instead, cut to the document's share of the budget. The picks merge most relevant first across documents (`interleave`), then cut to 400 blocks and `STITCH_SELECTED_BUDGET` (200,000) characters of real text (`cutSelection`). The answer pass reads those blocks' real text at `STITCH_EFFORT` (high) — every document in order, its header saying how many of its blocks are shown, a gap between shown blocks declared (`selectedSystem`) — then the turns so far (at most 20, a turn with no text left out), then the command. So a quote or a link is never taken from a skeleton line: the answer pass sees only stored block text, and every quote and every link resolves against it as before.
- **The passes on Jev.** With Jev configured (`TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, `lib/jev.ts`, `lib/graph/stitch-jev.ts`), the route pass and the select pass are Jev's: they are judgements, not writing — does this part, does this block, hold material the command needs — and Jev answers one calibrated yes/no per part and per skeleton line, so every line of every document read gets its own decision instead of one model naming 400 blocks from a 200,000-character skeleton. The route pass is one call per document, one noul per part, the command and the document's gist beside the parts; a part reads at 0.4 or above, and every document's top two read whatever their number. The select pass is one call per chunk of 32 skeleton lines of one part (the lines the route cut to, or every line), one noul per line, the command, the gist, and the part's title and summary beside them; a line is picked at 0.5 or above, and the picks stand most likely first within each document, as the GLM pass's did. The state of a call stays one part's lines, since Jev loses accuracy as the state fills with text the question does not need; eight calls run at a time; the thresholds live in code. A pass that fails — more than a quarter of its calls, or Jev unreachable — falls back to the GLM pass, so the command always runs. Usage lands under `stitch` on the `jev-latest` row. The answer pass is unchanged: Jev writes nothing.
- **What was read.** The result carries one `StitchDocument` per document read: `read` (every block of it was under the reading passes — its skeleton's lines, or its text whole) or `empty` with the `reason` and, for a failed transcript or conversion, the stored error as `detail`. The box lists them under every reply — "Read 3 of 5 documents", then one row per document: "312 transcript lines read", or in red why it was not read ("Not read: transcription failed. The video's captions could not be read."; a known transcription error class renders in the UI language through `transcriptErrorKey`, the rest as stored). With fewer than two documents read the command does not run: no model call, nothing stored, an empty reply, and the box says Stitch needs two documents it can read, with the rows saying why. The model answers with three outputs and uses only the ones the command needs: `links` — a block in one document to a block in another document, with a reason, up to 24, each end the whole block or a verbatim quote of it; `document` — a new page built from the documents: `heading` parts, `quote` parts (one whole block, or a verbatim part of it when the part carries a quote), and `text` parts (the model's own writing in markdown, each with the sources it rests on); and `reply` — the answer to the command in plain sentences: a question gets its answer, concise, each claim naming the document it comes from; a command to gather, link, or write gets one to three sentences on what was done. Every block id and quote resolves against the real block text before anything is stored: a quote resolves in its named block (exact, then the whitespace-tolerant match, §5), then exact in any block when the model named the wrong one, and a quote that does not resolve falls back to its named block whole — real text of a document, never the model's copy; an id that names no block drops, a link whose two ends are in the same document drops, a link that already exists skips, and the same passage twice on one page is one passage. So "a page of every paragraph that mentions X across all of these" is quote parts; "connect the sections that answer this question" and "where do these contradict each other" are links, the reason saying how the passages relate or what each side claims; "write one page that combines what they say about X" is text parts with sources; a question gets its answer in the reply. The passes together, a skeleton built on the way included, get `STITCH_DEADLINE_MS` (270 s) of the route's 300: past it the run is cut and the reader is told to narrow the command, never left with a stream that ended empty.
- **Links land recommended.** Every link Stitch proposes is a `DocLink` with `recommended: true` (§13): it draws dashed in the graph and paints nowhere until Accept — the reader approves everything (§1). The reply says how many were proposed, and Recommended links beside the canvas is where they are accepted.
- **A generated document.** The document Stitch writes is a real document of the project: its parts become markdown, the markdown becomes blocks (`lib/parse/markdown.ts`, the FORMALIZE article's path, §11), the document carries `generatedCommand` (a document with one is a generated document of the project it is attached to), and it attaches to the project — every reader tool works on it, and it is a node of the graph. Provenance (§1): each part links back to the block it came from — a quote part to the passage it copied, a text part to each of its sources — as accepted `DocLink` rows from the generated block (the whole block as the anchor) to the source's range, so every paragraph clicks back to its source in the reader. A page with no part that resolved is not stored.
- **The conversation.** The box keeps the turns per project for the browser tab, so closing the graph and opening it again keeps them; New conversation clears them. Send becomes Stop while a turn runs; a stopped turn stores nothing more. Suggestion chips fill the box with the four commands above. A viewer reads the documents and sees no box.


---

## 23. Companions

Under Projects on the dashboard, a list of web apps outside Unitos for the steps around a document that Unitos does not do (`lib/companions.ts`, `components/works/companions.tsx`). Two groups — before the reading, getting the material ready; after the reading, taking the notes onward — each a row: the name, one line on what it is for and why Unitos does not do it, and an arrow. Every one opens in a new tab, so the project the reader came back to stays where it was, and carries `rel="noopener noreferrer"`: the link says nothing about the reader or the project they came from.

- **What earns a place.** The step is real, Unitos has no specialized function for it, and the app is one a reader can trust with their own material. A step Unitos does well never appears — a document translates in the reader (§19), a handwritten PDF converts in the project (§16), a video transcribes on upload (§11) — and neither does a site that would take a reader's private notes to make its money from them. Every link is checked before it ships (`scripts/qa/ui-companions.mjs` checks them again).
