- **The list today.** Before: Mathpix (a photo of handwriting or maths → text and LaTeX; Unitos converts a whole handwritten PDF, this is for one formula), PDF24 Tools (split, merge, rotate, OCR a PDF before it goes into a project; Unitos takes a PDF whole), Connected Papers (the papers around one paper, for deciding what is worth dissecting). After: DeepL (loose text and whole files; Unitos translates an open document's blocks, not a file being sent on), Overleaf (the LaTeX the notes feed), Zotero (the references a project's documents rest on), Anki (the notes as cards that come back).
- **Sponsored entries.** The section is where paid entries will sit: a notemaking app whose work sits beside Unitos' own. `Companion.sponsored` marks one and the row says "Sponsored" beside the name — a paid entry the reader cannot tell from an unpaid one is worth nothing to either of them. Nothing is sponsored today, and a sponsored entry meets the same bar as an unpaid one; the label is never the only thing separating a companion from an advertisement.

## 24. Billing

Unitos Premium and Unitos Ultra (TIERS.md) sold through Stripe as subscriptions. The whole pipeline lives under `/billing`, outside the app's own pages, with its own frame (`components/billing/frame.tsx`: a back link and the language switcher over a ground; `app/billing/layout.tsx` is the gate), so a reader goes from discovery to payment to confirmation on pages that do one job each. Two looks, fixed and not the theme's: a Unitos Premium page is light (a cream ground, pearl cards, the white crystal floating at the corner), a Unitos Ultra page is night (a starry ground, obsidian cards, gold text and buttons, the black diamond). The plan page is light. Headings are gold where the page celebrates: the trial line on the Premium card, the confirmation's title.

0. **The plans page** (`/plans`, `app/plans/page.tsx`). The long scroll that sells the two tiers, from the design file — the plans story (`app/plans/plans-story.tsx`: the acts and the choice, one component; the plans page wraps it in its nav, and the sign-in page (§2) embeds it under the front door while the switch is on): the cream hero (Plans, the intro, Cancel any time, the trial's length), the white crystal with the Unitos Premium rates, what Unitos Premium holds — the tools (Simplify, Extract, Assistant, Stitch, Graph view), the notes, the collaboration, the document kinds — with Buy Premium after each part, the turn (Wait, there's more…), the descent into the night sky, the black diamond with the Unitos Ultra rates, what Unitos Ultra adds (Visualize, Tool conversations, offline work, the runs, the storage) with Buy Ultra after each, then the choice: the same account line and plan cards as the plan page, on the night ground. The acts are the design's markup with one class per element (`app/plans/acts.tsx`, `app/plans/plans.css`, generated from the design file and kept as they are); the words are the plans dictionary's (`lib/i18n/dict/plans.ts`, en and zh); the rates come from Stripe through the page (a yearly price as its twelfth per month); the choreography runs on scroll-driven timelines under `@supports`, so a browser without them shows every act still. Public, gated by the switch like every billing page. The fixed nav carries Back to Unitos and the language switcher. The account's side of the choice — the state line, the trial, what each card offers — is one reading shared with the plan page (`components/billing/plan-choice.tsx`), so the two pages cannot disagree. Every order page's and confirmation page's back link goes here.
1. **The plan page** (`/billing`, `?interval=month|year`). The two tiers side by side in their own material (`components/billing/plan-card.tsx`): the tier mark, the tier's name, the monthly rate at the chosen interval as Stripe reports it (`lib/billing/plans.ts` reads the price `STRIPE_PRICE_<TIER>_MONTHLY` / `STRIPE_PRICE_<TIER>_YEARLY` names, cached five minutes; a yearly price shows as its twelfth per month), under it the other interval's rate and the tier's yearly saving (`savingsPercent`, floored, never overstates), and what the tier holds (TIERS.md). Under the title: the intro and, in bold, Cancel any time. A Monthly/Yearly toggle sits over the cards; Yearly carries the largest saving among the tiers as a badge (Save up to n%). Beside the toggle, the account's state — on a trial, expired, granted, or subscribed — and, with a subscription, Manage subscription and Receipts. The trial (TIERS.md): while the account's trial runs, or before sign-in, the Premium card says the trial's length in gold (2 Months Free Now) and, signed in, when the first payment lands. Public: a signed-out visitor sees the plans and Sign in to choose a tier. A card offers Start Free (Premium, on the trial) or Get the tier for a tier the account can buy, Your plan for the tier it holds, Manage subscription when it holds a subscription to the other tier, and nothing for a tier below the one it holds. The button carries the chosen interval to the order page.
2. **The order page** (`/billing/order/<premium|ultra>?interval=month|year`). The tier chosen at the interval carried from the plan page (monthly if absent) beside the order sheet: the account it goes to, the tier, the price (a yearly price with its monthly rate in brackets), what is charged now, how it renews and when, Cancel any time, a link to switch the interval without leaving the order, then Pay with Stripe (`POST /api/billing/checkout {tier, interval}` → a Stripe Checkout session in subscription mode, `lib/billing/checkout.ts`) and the legal line: the Terms, the Privacy Policy, and that card details go to Stripe, never to Unitos. On the trial (`lib/billing/trial.ts` `checkoutTrialEnd`: the account's trial runs and ends at least two days out, Stripe's floor) the checkout starts free: Charged now is zero, a First charge row names the price and the trial's end, the renewal line reads Free until then, and the session carries `trial_end` = the trial's end, so Stripe takes the card now and charges then. Either tier starts free on the trial. An account that already has a subscription sees Manage subscription instead of Pay: the tier changes in the Stripe billing portal, never through a second checkout. Cancel on Stripe returns here with the plain canceled line, at the same interval. Pay dims the page under the Stripe hand-off overlay (`components/billing/pay-button.tsx`: a spinning ring, Opening Stripe…, and that Stripe brings the reader back) until the browser leaves.
3. **Stripe Checkout.** Stripe's hosted page. The session carries the account and the tier in its metadata and in the subscription's metadata, so every later event names both. The session names no payment method types: Stripe shows the ones the Dashboard turns on. With `STRIPE_TAX` on (`taxEnabled`, `lib/billing/config.ts`), the session calculates tax with Stripe Tax, collects the billing address and a tax id, and saves both on the customer, so every renewal invoice carries the same tax; off, the session charges the price as it is. On needs Stripe Tax active on the account with a head office address, a tax code on each product, and a registration in every jurisdiction that collects: without a registration Stripe collects no tax and reports no error.
4. **The confirmation page** (`/billing/confirmed?session=…`). Stripe returns here. The session must belong to the signed-in account. Paid, or started free on the trial (Stripe reports `no_payment_required` and a trialing subscription), the page records the purchase through the same path as the webhook (`applyCheckoutSession`, `lib/billing/events.ts`) — idempotent, so the two can land in either order or twice — and the tier is on before the webhook arrives. The tier mark floats over the title in gold, You're Powerful!, then Thank you for choosing Unitos, the tier the account has, and one line on the money: nothing charged today and the first payment's amount and date on the trial, or the amount charged today and the next renewal's date. Go to dashboard is the button; See the receipt (when a payment made one) and Plans are small links under it. Not yet paid (a payment method that settles later), the page says the tier turns on when it does. Without a session id — a stray visit, or an ads tool checking the URL — the page says there is no order to confirm and points at the plans page; that state is public, a door in the middleware like the plan page.
5. **Receipts** (`/billing/receipts`, `/billing/receipts/<id>`). One `Purchase` row per Stripe invoice that paid something — the first payment and every renewal; the trial's first invoice pays nothing and makes no receipt: number, date, account, tier, period, amount, status (paid or refunded), with Stripe's PDF and hosted invoice page, and Print.

**What a payment does to the account** (`lib/billing/events.ts`, from `POST /api/stripe/webhook`, signature-checked with `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET`). The gates keep reading `User.tier` and `User.trialEndsAt` only (TIERS.md): the events write them. An event names its account by the Stripe customer first (`User.stripeCustomerId`), then by the metadata's `userId`; it names the tier by the price first, then by the metadata's `tier`. A completed checkout (`checkout.session.completed`, or `checkout.session.async_payment_succeeded` when the payment method settled later) applies the subscription and records the invoice through the same path as the confirmation page; a first payment that never settled (`checkout.session.async_payment_failed`) and a renewal that did not pay (`invoice.payment_failed`) are logged and change nothing: Stripe retries, and the subscription's own event holds or ends the tier. A paid subscription (`invoice.paid`, `customer.subscription.*` active or trialing) writes `tier` = the tier bought and `trialEndsAt` = null, and keeps `subscriptionId`, `subscriptionTier`, and `subscriptionEndsAt` (the paid period's end) on the account for the billing pages. An ended subscription (canceled, unpaid, or expired without a first payment) writes `tier` = PREMIUM and `trialEndsAt` = the paid period's end: the account reads as Unitos Premium until then and expired after, whatever the operator had granted before. Past due or paused holds the tier until Stripe ends the subscription. A refunded charge (`charge.refunded`) marks its purchase refunded and changes no tier: Stripe's cancellation does that. The webhook runs whatever the switch says: a payment that happened is recorded.

**The Stripe account** is configured by `scripts/stripe-setup.mjs`, run once per account (the sandbox, then live) with a key that may write products, prices, webhook endpoints, and the customer portal: one product per tier (metadata `unitos_tier`), a monthly and a yearly price on each (lookup keys `unitos_<tier>_<monthly|yearly>`; a changed amount archives the old price and a new one takes the lookup key), the webhook endpoint `<origin>/api/stripe/webhook` with the nine events, and the customer portal (switch tier between the two products, cancel at the period's end, update the card, see invoices). It prints the four price ids and, at the endpoint's creation, its signing secret. Rerun, it changes only what differs.

**The switch.** Billing is off until the operator turns it on from the admin billing page (`/admin/billing`, `POST /api/admin/billing`, `lib/billing/switch.ts`; the `AppSetting` row `billing`, no row = off). Off, every `/billing` page answers 404 and the app shows no link to billing; the admin, signed in to `/admin`, sees the pages as a preview with a line saying so. On needs sign-in on (there must be accounts to bill), `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, and the four `STRIPE_PRICE_*` variables set, and all four prices readable from Stripe; the refusal names what is missing. The admin page also shows each Stripe value's set state (`STRIPE_TAX` among them), the webhook URL to register, the four prices (two tiers × monthly and yearly) as Stripe reports them, how many accounts hold a subscription, and every receipt.

**The links in the app**, shown only while the switch is on (`billingLinks`). The tier button (`components/billing/tier-button.tsx`): the tier chip in the dashboard header, pressed. A Unitos Ultra account goes to the plans page. A Unitos Premium account — on the trial, granted, or expired — opens the upgrade panel (`components/billing/ultra-upgrade.tsx`): what Unitos Ultra adds to Unitos Premium, in the Ultra material over the dashboard, the plan page's own list (`billing.ultraFeature2`–`6`, so the two cannot disagree), then Get Unitos Ultra (the Ultra order page) and See plans (the plans page). No Plans link sits beside the chip: the chip is the one button. The subscription panel (`components/billing/subscription-panel.tsx`), under Subscription in Settings: the plan as the tier chip; its status — on the trial, the trial's end and that no card is on file, with the line that a card keeps Unitos Premium after it; with a subscription, the subscription as Stripe reports it (`lib/billing/subscription.ts`, one read per Settings load: free until the trial's end, renews on a date, canceled and paid until a date, past due; the stored columns stand when Stripe does not answer) — the billing interval, the card on file (brand and last four digits); then the actions: with a subscription, Manage subscription, Change plan, Update card, and Cancel subscription — each opens the Stripe billing portal at that flow (`POST /api/billing/portal {flow: update | payment | cancel, back: settings}`, `portalUrl`; Stripe returns to Settings) — and Receipts; without one, See plans and Receipts; under the rows, what the tier holds. With the switch off the panel shows the plan and its state alone. The plain Ultra message in the reader (Visualize, Continue) carries a Plans action that opens the plan page in a new tab. The local reader (sign-in off) never sees them: there is no account to bill.

**The billing ask.** Sign-up takes a name and an email (§2), no card; the card comes after 30 minutes of active time. Active time (`lib/active-time.ts`, `User.activeSeconds`) is how long the account has used the app: the tab visible and the reader acting in it. The active time clock (`components/active-time-clock.tsx`, mounted by the dashboard, the reader, the notes full page, and the assistant page beside the account guard) counts it in the browser, one second at a time while the tab is visible and an input — a pointer, a key, a scroll, a touch — landed within the last minute, and posts one tick per minute of it to `POST /api/active-time {seconds}`, the rest on pagehide; a tick past 120 seconds is refused, and a zero tick on every page open asks whether the ask is due. The route adds the tick to the account and answers whether the ask opens (`askDue`): the switch on, outside the beta (every account has Ultra then), the account past 30 minutes, holding no subscription, a checkout today starting free (`checkoutTrialEnd`: the trial runs and ends at least two days out, so the ask's promise holds), and no ask in the last day (`User.billingAskedAt`, stamped when the route says yes, so a tab closed on the ask does not open it again on the next page). The ask (`components/billing/billing-ask.tsx`) is a dialog in the Premium material over the page: keep Unitos Premium free for two months, nothing charged until the trial's end, cancel any time. Add a card opens the Unitos Premium order page, where Stripe takes the card now and charges when the trial ends; Not now closes it, and it opens again after a day more of the clock. A reset account starts its active time and its ask over. Sign-in off: no row to count on, never an ask.

**The beta** (TIERS.md). `BETA=on` gives every account Unitos Ultra in the app while it is set: `accountTier`, `ultraActive`, and `premiumActive` (`lib/tiers.ts`) answer Ultra whatever the record says, so every gate opens and every tier mark is the black diamond. Billing runs beside it, disconnected: the pages read the record through `tierState` and sell the tiers as always, a purchase writes the record as always, and the state line on the plan page and the plans page says first that the tier chosen takes effect when the beta ends. The plan page and the plans page are public doors in the middleware, so a visitor from an ad reaches them signed out. The admin billing page shows whether `BETA` is set. Unset it and redeploy to end the beta.

---

## 25. The tool quality loop

Every AI tool answers to one question: did the reader get something they could not have written without the document, and could not have found faster by reading it? The loop measures that and moves it.

- **What a valuable output is.** One rubric per tool (`scripts/eval/rubrics.ts`): the tool's promises, written as questions a reader would ask of the output — Simplify: understood on first read, every claim and number kept, nothing added, markers correct; Extract (DISTILL): the question read with the intention behind it, every quote on what is asked and nothing tangential, each adds something, each caption states the answer with its number and is true to its quote, nothing stretched, what the document does not answer said plainly, and something a careful reader would miss; Collapse: each core carries its block's key words and its point, rephrased in the light of the whole article, never a meaning the block does not have, an extremely important line quoted word for word, within its ceiling; Summarize: findings first with numbers, wording at the asked depth, no sentence that fits any document; the assistant and the selection chat: the answer first, every claim on a cited block, the passages across the document gathered (the matches), what the document does not answer said plainly; Ask and Find: from inside the range, quoted with times, nothing invented; Define: the meaning the word has in its sentence, the document's own definition when it has one, plain words, never circular, the everyday meaning only when it differs. Every assistant-voice template carries the same three lines — `STYLE_RULE`, `GROUNDING_RULE`, `SPECIFICITY_RULE` (`lib/prompts/types.ts`) — so the rubric and the prompt say the same thing in the same words.
- **The cases.** `scripts/eval/cases.ts`: one case is one tool on one fixture with one reader context — a paper, a memo, news, docs, an essay, a Chinese article, a transcript (`scripts/eval/fixtures/*.md`, original texts, block-tagged the way the routes render a document) — for readers set to nothing, a novice, an expert, a professional with a purpose, in English and Chinese, with questions the document answers, answers in part, or does not answer at all. The set is the contract for "across every context": a context the set lacks gets a case before its tool is judged good.
- **The run.** `npm run eval` (`scripts/eval/run.ts`): each case goes through its tool's real template (`lib/prompts/*`) on the real model with the route's effort and budget; the output is checked mechanically — the word cap, every `[block <id>]` resolving, every span and quote verbatim (`resolveSpan`, `matchInText`), Simplify's markers covering every sentence, the answer's language, a core for every block under its ceiling, its numbers and quotes in its block, no banned opener ("In other words", "This passage") — then judged by Claude Fable 5.1 (or Kimi without an Anthropic key) against the rubric: a score per criterion, an overall, the worst criterion with the exact words that show it, and one concrete change to the template that would raise it. The report (`.eval/runs/<stamp>/report.md`, `results.json` with every prompt and output) is the table per tool and the eight weakest cases; `--baseline latest --gate` compares with the last run and fails on a mean drop of 0.5 or a check that newly fails.
- **The readers' signal.** Every tool's output carries a rating (`components/rating-buttons.tsx`, `POST /api/ratings`, `ToolRating`): 👍 or 👎 on the Simplify, Analyze, and Visualize cards, on a definition under the Define row, on every answer of the selection chat and the sidebar assistant; a 👎 opens one optional line for what was wrong, with the focus in it; under a card's or an answer's foot row it opens on its own row, so the row's buttons stay where they were. A press on the other thumb changes the rating on the same row (`PATCH /api/ratings`; a comment the reader wrote stays on the row), and a second press on the pressed thumb takes the rating back (`DELETE /api/ratings`, the reader's own row only). The row keeps the tool, the input it ran on, the output, the reader's language, and the comment. `npm run eval:ratings` (`scripts/eval/import-ratings.ts`) turns the thumbs down of the last 30 days into fixtures and cases — the document as the tool saw it, the selection or the question, the comment as what a good answer must fix — so the loop works on the answers real readers disliked, in the contexts they read in.
- **The check** (`lib/derive/check.ts`): with Jev configured, a tool's output is judged against its rubric at once. The criteria that are judgements — plain words, the answer first, nothing added, each quote adds something, honest about what is not answered — are asked as yes/no questions of the input and the output; the criteria that count (every number kept, every marker present) stay with the eval's mechanical checks, since Jev does not count. Simplify, Summarize, and the assistant's answer are checked after the reader has them (a streamed output is on screen before a check can answer); a criterion under 0.5 fails, and a failed output is flagged: one `ToolRating` row with rating `flag` and the failed criteria as its comment, beside the readers' thumbs, which `npm run eval:ratings` imports like a thumb down. Extract, whose output is JSON, is checked before it is shown: a run failing two criteria or more runs once again, the run with fewer failures is kept, and a kept run that still fails is flagged.
- **The loop** (`.claude/skills/toolloop/SKILL.md`, `/toolloop`): pull the ratings, run the baseline, read the weakest cases whole, change the one template the weakest tool uses — the rule for the fault class, never the case — run that tool against the baseline, keep the change when its mean rises and no case fell under 3, revert otherwise, one template per commit; stop when no case is under 3 and every check passes, or a change no longer moves the mean. A fixture is never edited to pass; a case is never dropped for scoring low.

---

## 26. Contents

**Contents** is the article's parts, each a jump to where it starts: the one control of the article menu at the top left of the article (§6), in Normal view floating at the top left of the page, where it stays as the article scrolls; in a split view in the pane header, its list dropping below the header. A transcript's contents are its chapters, in the media pane (below). Two clicks make the contents: Contents opens the list, and with nothing stored the list asks whether to generate them — one line saying AI reads the article and writes its parts, the Generate contents button, and the disclaimer that a part's title or start may be off — and Generate contents runs the one model call. Nothing is written by AI before the reader asks for it.

- **Chapters.** A media document's contents are its chapters: one part per topic of the transcript, each pointing at the transcript line it starts on. The media pane's view bar carries Chapters (`components/video/chapters-menu.tsx`), beside the transcript's actions: stored chapters list as jumps, each with its time, and a click seeks the player to the line; none stored, the ask and Generate chapters (an editor), which runs the one Jev pass (`lib/video/chapters.ts`; `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, else a 503) through the same route with `generate: true`. Jev reads the transcript in chunks of 40 lines with the four lines before each for context and answers one yes/no per line — does a new topic start here — and code makes the chapters: the lines at 0.6 or above, at least 45 seconds apart (the surer line wins when two are closer), at most 40 (the surest kept), the first line always the first chapter; a transcript under 12 lines has none. A chapter's title is its first line's opening words, since Jev writes no text. The parts store as `Document.contents` like an article's.
- **The parts.** Built when the reader presses Generate contents, never at ingest — one model call over the whole document, for a list many documents are never asked for — and stored on the document (`Document.contents`: `[{title, blockId, level}]`, in reading order; `lib/contents.ts`, prompt in `/lib/prompts/contents.ts`, `CONTENTS_MODEL` at `CONTENTS_EFFORT` (low: a reading of where the parts begin, like Distill's), usage under `contents`). The model reads the document under the cached prefix and segments the whole of it: where the document has headings, a part starts at a heading and its title is that heading as written, nested headings level 2 under their level 1 part; where it has none, the model segments by what the text does and writes a title of at most 8 words in the document's language. A part names the id of its first block; the route resolves every id against the stored blocks (`resolveParts`): an id that names no block drops, a part that starts before the one before it drops, two parts at one block are one, a part cannot start at the title block, a page marker, or the video block (the title block is the first heading when it repeats the document's title, or the first block when it is a heading above every other heading; a first heading at the level of the others is the first part, so "Part 1" lists with "Part 2" to "Part 10"), and a level 2 part with no level 1 part before it is level 1. At most 80 parts; a document under 6 readable blocks has none. Titles are never translated.
- **The route.** `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/contents` `{generate?}` (viewer): stored parts are answered as they are, no model call, `fallback: false`; none stored, the document's headings (`headingContents`: every `HEADING` block past the title is a part, `h1`–`h2` level 1, deeper level 2 under a level 1) with `fallback: true`, no model call. `generate: true` from an editor runs the one model call and answers the parts stored (`fallback: false`; no parts for a document too short to have any); a failed call is a 422 the list shows with Try again, and no model configured a 503. A viewer's generate answers the headings. A re-parse carries the stored contents onto the new blocks (`carryContents`, `lib/contents.ts`): each part finds the new block whose text is its old block's — the whole text, else its first 60 characters — in reading order past the part before it; a part whose text is gone drops, and when fewer than half of the parts carry, the contents clear, so the next Generate contents writes them anew. The parts a reader asked for are never thrown away for a parse of the same text. The skeleton (§22) builds the contents on its way when the document has none, so a document whose skeleton has built shows its parts on the first click.
- **The list.** `components/reader/contents-menu.tsx`: the Contents button, and the list under it. With stored parts: the disclaimer, then the parts — level 1 bold, level 2 indented; a click scrolls the reader until the part's block stands at the reading line, 80px under the pane's top, and flashes it (`dissect:flash-block`) and closes the list; Escape and a click outside close it. With none and the article has headings: the headings first, each a jump like a part, then under a rule Generate contents as one row (an editor; its tooltip says what AI does and carries the disclaimer; a viewer reads that an editor can generate them). With none and no headings: the ask, Generate contents, the disclaimer. While the call runs the button says so; a failed call says why, and the button stands for Try again; a document too short to have parts says so. The parts are kept per document for the browser tab, so a reopen shows them at once, and read again on every open, so a re-parse's new block ids replace the kept ones.

---

## 27. Slides and sheets

Two more file formats, each with its own parser and its own reader form: **slides** (a .pptx, or a Google Slides file from Drive) and **sheets** (a .xlsx, a .csv, a .tsv, or a Google Sheets file from Drive). Every AI tool, every annotation, and every note works on them exactly as on an article: their words are rendered text, selected and marked like a table's (§6), anchored by block offsets with the quote fallback (§5), and read by the assistant and every derivation through the one document prefix (§4). Google Drawings still export to PDF, and Google Docs to a .docx (§14).

**The one rule.** A SLIDE or SHEET block's html is a replica of the slide or the sheet, and the replica's DOM text — every text node outside `[data-anchor-skip]` — equals the block's text exactly. Text pieces are joined by invisible gaps (`.cell-gap`, a tab or a newline, the table convention of §5); what is not the document's own words — a slide number, the layout's and master's decoration, a sheet's column letters and row numbers, the style element — is skipped. So a selection inside a replica captures block offsets (`lib/anchors/dom.ts`), and the block's marks paint on the passage inside the html (`table-marks.ts`), never a ring around the whole block.

**Data model.** `BlockType` gains `SLIDE` and `SHEET`. `Document.format` is `"slides"` or `"sheets"` (null for everything else): re-parse picks the parser by it, since a .csv has no magic. The stored file is the .pptx, .xlsx, .csv, or .tsv; an add of the same file asks first by `fileHash` like a PDF (§15). Neither block type styles, removes, or changes its kind (`/api/blocks/[blockId]`). A slide's words change through the text PATCH, the one rule kept (`lib/replica.ts`, `replicaWithText`): the text is pieces between the replica's gaps, and an edit changes words within pieces and keeps every gap (a newline between lines, a tab inside a line or between cells), so the slide keeps its lines; each changed stretch (a word-level diff) goes into the run where it starts, so the new words keep that run's font, size, and color, and words added at the edge of a link or of a raised or lowered mark go outside it; the html changes text node by text node and every other byte stays, so Undo gives the replica back byte for byte. An edit that, run backwards, would not give the html back (words that emptied a run) keeps the html it had (`BlockEdit.meta.html`, up to 100 KB), and the edit taken back puts it back. A bullet, the line `Speaker notes:`, and a chart's data under its drawing keep their words. Lines come and go in the text boxes and the speaker notes (`slideWithText`): the slide's lines and its notes' lines align apart, paired by the words they share. A line that goes takes its paragraph and the gap beside it, and a text box whose every line goes stays, empty. A new line comes after the kept line before it (at the start, before the kept line after it), a paragraph built like the one of its box whose bullet it opens with, the nearest first: its style, its bullet, its first run's look; a numbered line takes its own number, so a line joins a numbered list at its end, since the numbers after it stay. Speaker notes taken away whole take their label, and a slide without notes takes them at its end, under the line `Speaker notes:`, a paragraph per line. Refused with the reason (400): a line of a slide's table, a line a break divides, a tab added or removed, notes with no line under their label, a new line whose bullet no paragraph of its box has, and words where the replica has no run. A sheet's cells, rows, and columns change through the same PATCH (`sheetWithText`): the text is its grid, a line per row and a tab between cells, then a chart's data, which stays. The grid reads row by row: a line break in a cell's words before its row's last cell stays in its cell, and rows and columns change around it, never so that the break comes to end its row (one in a row's last cell keeps the sheet's rows and columns); a cell's words gain or lose no line break. Words change cell by cell in place, as a slide's do, and an empty cell takes its words at its end. A new row or column is built like the kept one before it (the one after it, when the one before is frozen): its height or width and its cells' style; the row numbers, the column letters, `data-rows`, `data-cols`, and the grid's width follow. Rows (or columns) pair up by the cells they share, so a row that stays keeps its style and its formulas. What an edit takes out — each removed row's source, or each removed column's col, letter, and cells — is kept on the edit (`BlockEdit.meta.cut`, up to 100 KB), so the edit taken back puts it back as it was. A cell's classes follow its words in place: words that turn into a number line up right unless the cell's style sets the alignment, and words that ran over an empty neighbour are clipped while it has words (`data-over`) and run over again once it is empty; so Undo gives the sheet back byte for byte. With merged cells, a row goes only with every merge it holds whole (none reaches it from above, none it starts reaches down), no new row goes into a merge, and a new row is built like the nearest row of plain cells; a column goes only with plain cells, and a new one comes after a column of plain cells. A drawing moves with the rows and columns under it: each edge keeps its place in its row (its column), an edge in a row that went moves to the next row that stays, and one past the grid keeps its distance from the grid's end. Formulas follow their cells, as a spreadsheet's do (`lib/sheet-formulas.ts`): a formula's references move with their cells (a range grows with a row inside it and shrinks with one taken), and the formulas the sheet computes show their new values when a cell they read changes. The sheet computes a formula it reads (numbers, text, TRUE and FALSE, a cell or a range, + - * / ^ & and comparisons, a percent, and SUM, AVERAGE, MIN, MAX, COUNT, COUNTA, PRODUCT, ROUND, ABS, IF) when that reading gives back its stored value in the cell's number format (`data-fmt`, which the parse writes on a formula's cell) or in one the shown value reads as; any other formula keeps its value. The new text gives a formula's cell its value as it was or as computed, and the text stored is the one the replica reads. Refused with the reason (400): rows and columns changed in one edit, or a row with more or fewer cells than the first; a frozen row or column removed, or a row or column added among them; a row or a column a merge spans; a row or column holding a cell that a formula the sheet computes reads alone; a formula's cell given another value; a chart's data changed. A slide whose own words (before the speaker notes) differ from its words as parsed shows its replica: its picture shows the words as parsed, so the frame's `data-picture` waits as `data-picture-held` until the words are back, and `markSlidePictures` leaves such a frame as it is. The stored file does not change: a re-parse reads it again and brings its words back.

### Slides (`lib/parse/slides.ts`)

One SLIDE block per slide, `Block.page` the 1-based slide number, in the presentation's own order. The parser reads the .pptx part by part (`lib/parse/office.ts`: the zip, the XML, the relationships, the theme):

- **Replica.** The slide as a frame that scales with the column (`container-type: inline-size`; every length inside is a share of the slide's width in `cqw`, so a 24pt title is the same share of the slide at any size): every shape at its place and size in percent of the slide, its fill (solid, gradient, picture) and outline, its geometry (a preset as rounded corners, an ellipse, or a clip path; a custom geometry as an SVG path with its fill and stroke; connectors and lines as SVG), its text in the slide's fonts, sizes, weights, colors, alignment, indents, bullets and numbering, line spacing, autofit scale and vertical anchor; pictures stored as images of the document (`ImageAsset`, claimed on save like a captured chart, replaced on re-parse; an SVG beside a raster wins); tables as tables with their merges; a chart as its title and its data table; groups flattened with their transform. Placeholders inherit place, fonts, and bullets from the layout and the master, as PowerPoint draws them; the theme resolves scheme colors with their modifiers. The master's and layout's own shapes draw under the slide's, their words skipped. Slide-number, footer, and date placeholders are furniture: drawn, skipped.
- **Text.** Reading order: the title first, then the shapes top to bottom, left to right (a 4% band decides "same row"); the z-index keeps the slide's stacking whatever the order. A bulleted paragraph carries its bullet (`• `, `1. `) in the text; empty paragraphs at either end of a body drop. Speaker notes follow the slide's words after the line `Speaker notes:` (stored English, §2) and render as a strip under the slide. The document prefix says what a SLIDE block is (`formatLines` in `lib/derive/context.ts`).
- **Fonts.** Every typeface the deck names rides on the slide frames (`data-fonts`); the reader loads a web font for each (`lib/office-fonts.ts`, Google Fonts): the metric-compatible stand-in for an Office or system font (Carlito for Calibri, Arimo for Arial, Tinos for Times New Roman, Anton for Impact, …), or the typeface itself when Google Fonts hosts it. The declaration names the typeface first, so a machine that has it uses it. Sheets do the same for their cell fonts. The slide and sheet styles live in `lib/office-css.ts`, put in `<head>` once by the reader and used as-is by the server's picture render.
- **Charts.** A chart part draws as an SVG (`lib/parse/chart.ts`): bars and columns (clustered, stacked, percent stacked), lines, areas, pies and doughnuts, scatter plots, with the title, axes, gridlines, legend, and data labels, in the file's colors or the theme's accents. The SVG's words are skipped; the chart's data — the title, the categories down the first column, one column per series — lies under it invisible as the block's words, so the assistant reads the numbers and a selection sweeps through them. A chart kind the drawing does not cover shows its data table. Google Slides charts are pictures already.
- **Pictures.** A Google Slides import asks Drive for the .pptx export and its PDF export (`/api/drive/import`, kinds `slides` and `slides-file`; `fetchExported` in `lib/drive/fetch.ts`). The PDF's pages are the slides as Google draws them: their sizes land with the document (`storeSlidePictureSizes`), the renders after the response (`renderSlidePictures`, JPEG at `PAGE_IMAGE_WIDTH`, one `PageImage` per SLIDE block), and the frame carries `data-picture`. The reader (`MarkedHtml` in block-view.tsx) loads the picture from the page image route over the replica; once it loads the replica's words turn transparent and its shapes hide, the marks still painting on the words — a PDF viewer's text layer. A picture that fails to load, or a slide that has none yet, shows the replica alone. A re-parse carries the stored pictures over by slide number; the PDF itself is not kept. An uploaded .pptx (or one picked from Drive as a file) gets its pictures after the response by the best means the deployment has (`lib/handwritten/slide-pictures.ts`): LibreOffice where it is installed (`SOFFICE_PATH`, or `soffice` on the PATH) converts the deck to a PDF and the pages render like Drive's; else the configured browser (§15's `BROWSER_WS_ENDPOINT` or `CHROMIUM_PATH`) draws each replica with its web fonts, its pictures served from the database, and photographs it. Once stored, the frames are marked `data-picture`. Neither available: the replicas stand alone.
- **Caps.** 500 slides; past that the block list ends with a paragraph saying where it was cut.

### Sheets (`lib/parse/sheets.ts`)

- **The sheet repairs on Jev.** A .csv has no frozen panes and a workbook often none, so the header row scrolls away with the records; a column of numbers typed as text ("1,200 kg", "$4.50") lines up left like words. With Jev configured, one call per sheet (`repairSheet`) asks whether row 1 is a header row and, for every column that mixes text and digits, what the column holds (number, date, identifier, text). A header row at 0.7 freezes (`frozenRows` 1, as a frozen pane would); a number column's number-like text cells take the number kind and line up right. The cells' text never changes. Slides need no repair: speaker notes are separated by PowerPoint's own placeholder type, not by a reading.

One HEADING block (the sheet's name) and one SHEET block per visible sheet, in workbook order; a .csv/.tsv is one sheet named after the file (the delimiter sniffed unless the name says tabs; RFC 4180 quoting).

- **Drawings.** A sheet's drawing part (`xl/drawings/`) lays its pictures, charts, and shapes over the grid at the cells they are anchored to, inside the scroll box, so they scroll with the cells: a picture stored as an image of the document, a chart drawn as an SVG with its data laid under it as the block's words (the same chart drawing as slides; references without cached values resolve against the sheet's own cells), a shape as its fill and words (skipped). A drawing past the grid's last cell lies on the empty space beyond it; the grid's text never grows for it.
- **Replica.** The sheet as a grid in a box that scrolls both ways (`max-height: 78vh`), so frozen rows and columns, the column letters, and the row numbers stay in view while the page's scroll stays the reader's. Every cell at the sheet's column width and row height, showing the value as the sheet formats it (the number format applied through `ssf`, so 0.1 with a percent format reads `10%` and a serial date reads as a date), in the cell's font, size, weight, color, fill, alignment, wrap, and borders — one class per cell style in a scoped `<style>`, so a large sheet stays small; merged cells span; a formula rides on its cell as a tooltip; a hyperlink is a link; numbers align right, booleans and errors center; text longer than its cell runs over empty neighbors and is clipped otherwise. Theme and indexed colors resolve through the workbook's theme.
- **Text.** One line per row, cells separated by tabs, values as formatted. A merged-away cell's tab rides inside the cell that covers it, so the rows stay aligned. Hidden rows and columns are left out of both the grid and the text; trailing empty rows and columns are trimmed, a colored band near the words kept. The document prefix says what a SHEET block is and that the HEADING before it is the sheet's name.
- **Caps.** 10,000 rows, 256 columns, 200,000 cells per sheet; a cut sheet ends with a paragraph saying where.

### Verifying them

`scripts/qa/ui-office.mjs` runs the whole path against a server on :3311: the multipart and chunked uploads of the fixtures under `scripts/qa/fixtures/office/`, the parse, the pictures made after the response (LibreOffice or the browser), a note anchored on a slide's words and one on a sheet's cell, the reader (replicas, pictures over them, marks, the text toolbar, charts, sheet drawings, web fonts), and a re-parse that keeps pictures and anchors. Every run uploads fresh bytes, so no run's add asks first about another run's document.

### Adding them

Every add path takes them: the upload box's drop zone and file input (`SLIDES_ACCEPT`, `SHEETS_ACCEPT` in `lib/office-file.ts`), a drop anywhere on the page, the chunked upload (`/api/uploads/complete`), and Drive (`classifyDriveFile`: Google Slides → `slides`, Google Sheets → `sheets`, a .pptx in Drive → `slides-file`, a .xlsx/.csv/.tsv in Drive → `sheets-file`; the picker lists them). The server never trusts the name for a zip: `sniffOfficeFile` reads the format from the zip's parts; a .csv/.tsv is read by name only when the bytes are not a zip. The assistant's Drive attachments keep Drive's PDF export for Google Slides and Sheets (they ride as text). Translation treats a slide's words like a paragraph's (under the table cap) and a sheet's cells as data.

---

## 28. Collapse

**Collapse** shows the article with every block collapsed to its **core**: what the block really says, in plain words, at a tenth to a third of its length, written by AI in the light of the whole article. The reader reads the cores in place of the blocks, in order, as the article, and opens any block whole with a click. The tool is the Collapse button at the top right of the article, beside Extract (in a split view, in the pane header); pressed, the article collapses and the button reads Collapsed; pressed again, the article shows whole. Either press closes the selection toolbar: its words change view under it. A card about words the new view no longer draws closes too (its mark reopens it), unless a run is in flight or its box holds typed words. The choice is remembered per document in the browser, in a cookie the page reads (`lib/collapse-memory.ts`, the 40 newest choices; the older localStorage memory `unitos-collapse-<documentId>` is still read, and moves to the cookie on the document's next open): a document left collapsed comes with its cores and is drawn collapsed on the first paint. A transcript and the article card in the media pane do not collapse yet.

- **The cores.** One per collapsible block — a paragraph, a list, a figure with a caption, a table, an equation, code, a transcript line, a slide, a sheet (a table's, a slide's, or a sheet's text past 5,000 characters is data and shows as it is); never a heading, which is short already, a separator, a page marker, or the video block. Stored on the document (`Document.collapse`: `{v, cores: [{blockId, hash, text}]}`, `lib/collapse.ts`), each core with the hash of the block text it was written from (`blockHash`): a block whose text changed shows as it is until the next Collapse writes its core again, and a re-parse's new block with the same text keeps its core by the hash. The cores are the document's, written once for every reader, in the document's language.
- **The prompt** (`/lib/prompts/collapse.ts`, `COLLAPSE_MODEL` — Claude Opus 5.5, the best writer of the roster: the cores are read by every reader in place of the article, and a document collapses once — at `COLLAPSE_EFFORT`, high, usage under `collapse`). The model reads the whole document under the cached prefix and, for every block listed, asks three things in order: what the block serves in the whole document — the claim it makes, the evidence it gives, the step it takes, the example it shows, the ground it lays for what comes next; what it is really trying to say, the one thing the reader must take from it, said first, a number, a name, or a term kept only when the point rests on it and written as printed; and how to say it in plain, simple words, the way one explains it to a smart reader outside the field. Length: a tenth of a long block, a third at most, the ceiling given per block (`coreCeiling`: a third of the block's words, between 8 and 60; a block of 24 words or fewer keeps up to seven tenths, so a sentence stays one shorter sentence); a long paragraph becomes two or three sentences at most. A figure collapses to what it shows and why the document shows it; a table to what it says — the pattern, the comparison, the biggest number; an equation to what it states in words; a list to its point; code to what it does; a transcript line to what was said. A core past its ceiling is cut at the last sentence end under it, a word at a time and a CJK character at a time (`fitCore`); a core is never longer than its block. Built one call per window of `COLLAPSE_WINDOW_CHARS` (30,000) of the missing blocks' text, the windows at once; a block the model skips stays without a core and shows as it is.
- **The route.** `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/collapse` (editor) writes the cores the document lacks and answers every current core with `complete`; a failed call is a 422 the reader shows as a toast, no model configured a 503. `GET` (viewer) answers the current cores; a viewer with none stored reads that an editor can collapse the article. While the cores are written the button reads Collapsing… with a Stop pill, and a press on it is Stop: the request aborts, the route passes the abort to the model calls (`buildCollapse`'s `signal`), nothing is saved — the windows are stored together only once all of them answer — and the article stays whole, with no toast. Leaving the document stops the run the same way.
- **The reading** (`components/reader/core-block.tsx`, `reader.tsx` `collapse`). A collapsed block is a paragraph in the block's place, a sage rule at its left, and its kind before the core when it was a figure, a table, an equation, code, a slide, or a sheet; it carries the block's id, so a jump from the contents and a flash still find it. Every block that has a core has its own button at its right (`CoreToggle`): it collapses that block to its core, or reads it whole, whatever the rest of the article shows — in a collapsed article and in a whole one (`flippedBlocks`, cleared when Collapse is pressed). Beside a core the button shows only while the pointer or the keyboard's focus is on the block (a click on the core reads it whole too); beside a block read whole in a collapsed article the button that folds it again stands faint; once Collapse is off, the button that folds a block shows only while the pointer is on the block. A click on a core's words (not a drag, not a mark) reads its block whole, as the button does; the button folds it again. A block whose whole text has annotations shows a clay dot on its core. Edit mode shows no cores. A jump to words in a collapsed block reads it whole first, as its own button does, and the rest of the article stays collapsed: a note's source, an annotation's Jump, a search result, a ¶ chip, an extraction's quote, a match, and a link's other end. A contents part lands on the core. A document opened with Collapse remembered on is not jumped to until its cores are in.
- **Two sets of annotations.** The collapsed view and the whole text keep their own annotations. A selection in a core opens the same tools as a selection in the text — highlight, comment, Explain, Simplify, Visualize, the assistant, Add to notes, a quote dragged to a note — except Link, which joins the texts themselves. Its anchor is in the core layer (`Source.layer` = "core", `lib/anchors/layer.ts`): the offsets and the quote are the core's words, the server resolves it against the cores the reader sees (`layerBlocks`, `coreBlocks`), and a tool reads the core words with the cores around them as context (the cached prefix stays the whole document). The core anchors heal against the current cores on every render (`resolveDocumentSources` with layer "core") and paint on the cores alone (`coreHighlights`); the whole text's healing and a block edit's remap never touch them. A core rewritten after its block changed keeps an anchor whose quote it still holds; else the anchor orphans, quote kept. In the reader a core anchor's block id is its core key (`core:<blockId>`, `lib/anchors/core-key.ts`), so marks, cards, and local marks work as on any block; the key turns back into the block id and the layer on the way to the server. The Annotations tab and the annotations full page list one set at a time under a Whole text | Collapsed switch (`layer-switch.tsx`), shown once a core annotation exists; in the reader the tab follows the article's view. A jump to a core annotation shows its block's core; where no core can be drawn (Editing, edit mode, or no cores in hand), it lands on the block's words and flashes them.
- **In the page editor** (§29, §30; `components/docs/layer/collapse.tsx`). The button stands at the toolbar's right end (in the pane header in a split view). A core stands for a unit of the paragraph index (`collapseUnits`, `lib/collapse-units.ts`): a paragraph, a list (its outermost list, every line), a table (every cell, by `Block.cell.table`), code, or an equation; headings and figures stay as they are, and a block document's unit is its block. The prompt lists a unit of several rows as one block (`(LIST, blocks X to Y, …)`). In Viewing a unit that has a core shows the block reader's core in its place, drawn over the page — the rich text never changes — with its button to read it whole (shown only under the pointer and on keyboard focus: a click on the core does the same) and the chip that folds it again; a click on a core's words (not a drag, not a mark) reads its unit whole, as in the block reader; and beside it the numbers of the PDF pages that begin in the unit ("p. 7 · p. 8"). A core is one piece to the pagination: it moves whole to the next page and never runs across a page edge. A selection in a core, and one that runs across cores, reads as in the block reader. Editing, Suggesting, and Find turn Collapse off; Collapse pressed in Editing or Suggesting switches the page to Viewing, and Collapse off goes back to the mode it left, the caret where it was, unless the reader chose a mode in between. A collapsed unit shows no translation line; read whole, its paragraphs show theirs (§19). A contents part that starts in a collapsed unit jumps to its core. A jump to words in a collapsed unit reads the unit whole first, as in the block reader, and a unit of several rows flashes every row.


---

## 29. Blank documents: the page editor

Imports (§30) open in the page editor too.

A blank document (made with Blank document, §15) is written in Unitos the way a Google Doc is written: a title row, Google Docs' toolbar, and a white page on Unitos's paper, one continuous rich text with nothing to click into. It is not a list of blocks. The Unitos layer sits on top of it: the selection toolbar and every tool on it, the cards, the marks of notes and annotations, and the notes tray work on its text as they work on an article's.

- **The look** (`components/docs/frame.css`, `docs.css`, `css/`): Google Docs' layout, sizes, keys, and behavior in Unitos's colors, surfaces, and faces. Every color is a `--docs-*` token set from a Unitos variable (globals.css), so dark mode needs no second value; only the document's own colors (black words, gray headings, the table grid, links) have dark values. Clay is the accent: `--docs-blue` for fills and rings, `--docs-accent-text` (clay-700) for accent words. Controls are round: clay-100 under the pointer; clay-200 when pressed, on, or open; their symbols clay-800. Menus are 16 px cards with the float shadow, a row under the pointer or the keys clay-100 with clay-800 words, section names in small capitals. Dialogs are 24 px cards over an ink scrim at 30%, with the title in Caprasimo, pill buttons (the primary clay, the rest sand), and filled pill fields (`--docs-field-bg`). The tooltip is Unitos's ink bubble in Docs' place and timing. The chrome is Figtree at Docs' sizes, 600 where Docs uses 500 (Figtree has no 500); titles are Caprasimo. The document keeps its own formatting (Arial 11 pt, the named styles, blue links, the black table grid, the palettes) and prints and exports as it shows. On a page with a color in dark mode, the words, the marks, and the assistant's suggestions keep the light theme's colors (the kind colors too).
- **The document.** `Document.richText` is the rich text — Tiptap (ProseMirror) JSON, the source of truth for its words and formatting — and `Document.richTextRev` its revision; `Document.pageSetup` is its page (`{pageless, width, height, margins, color}` in points; null = Letter, 1 in margins, pages, white). A document with rich text is a blank document; every other document has none. The node and mark names, the request schema, and the sanitizer live in `lib/docs/schema.ts`: a save keeps only known nodes and marks, and every attribute that reaches a style, an href, or a src is checked by name (a color is a hex or an rgb, a face is a plain name in any script, a link is http(s), mailto, tel, or a path).
- **The paragraph index.** The document's Block rows are derived from its rich text on every save (`deriveBlocks`, `lib/docs/blocks.ts`, the same function in the editor and on the server): one row per node a reader can select — a paragraph, a heading, a list item's paragraph, a table cell's paragraph, a code block — plus an image (FIGURE) and a horizontal line (SEPARATOR). A row's id is the node's `blockId`, its text the node's words as if every person's suggestion were accepted and every one of the assistant's rejected (a line break is "\n"): the words a person's suggestion adds count, the words it removes and the zero-width spaces of a suggested break do not, and a block it removes has no row; the words and blocks the assistant's suggestion adds are no rows, the ones it removes stay, and its format changes read as before (`deriveBlocks` reads `withoutSuggestions(doc, isAssistantSuggestion)`; `inlineText` and the editor's anchors count the same way, `outOfIndex`). Its html is the heading level and the layout tokens (`center`, `quote`), its styles and links the node's marks (small caps as `smallCaps`, subscript and superscript as `sub` and `sup`). Every node the index reads carries a `blockId`, drawn as `data-block-id`; a split, a paste, or a duplicate gets a fresh one, and pasted content never keeps the ids it came with. An equation on its own line is an EQUATION row holding its TeX. A smart chip's words are its label; an inline equation's words are its TeX between dollar signs (`$\sigma(\mathcal{A}_\alpha)$`), so every AI tool reads the formula, and a passage, a quote, or an anchor takes it whole: it starts and ends only at the formula's edges (`mathWords`; `layer/anchor.ts`); a footnote's number and a bookmark add no words; the footnotes' paragraphs are indexed, each on the page its number stands on. So an anchor captured in the editor (§5) resolves against the rows, and every AI tool, the digest, Stitch, search, and export read a blank document like any other. Accepting a person's suggestion leaves the rows as they are and rejecting one changes them; the assistant's is the other way round: accepting it changes the rows, the sync moves the anchors, and the history row is the accepting person's; rejecting it leaves them. A save that only adds the assistant's suggestions changes no row, moves no anchor, and writes no history.
- **Saving** (`components/docs/use-docs-save.ts`), the way Google Docs saves: no Save button. A change marks the document unsaved; a pause of 700 ms, or 3 s of steady typing, sends the whole rich text with the revision it started from (`PUT /api/documents/[documentId]/rich-text {richText, rev}`, editor), without the attributes that are null where the schema's default is null (`compactRichText`, `lib/docs/schema.ts`), so the first save of an import grows it by a few percent, not by half. One save runs at a time; steady typing saves once per 3 s, counted from the start of the last save. A save from an older revision answers 409 with the stored copy, and the editor lays its own changes since its last save over it (`mergeRichText`, `lib/docs/merge.ts`) and saves again 700 ms later, also right after a lost connection. When the stored copy is exactly the copy of a save whose answer was lost, that copy is the base: only the changes since it are laid over it. Nodes pair by the first blockId inside them, at every level, compared as key-sorted JSON in the editor's form: two list items or two table cells are two changes, and two changes to one paragraph both stand when they touch different words; else the copy on screen wins that paragraph. Two insertions at one point stand with the stored copy's words first (typed earlier); when one begins with the other's words (a save whose answer was lost, a reload while a save was on its way), the longer one stands once. A paragraph whose only change on screen is the assistant's suggestions (it reads the same once they are taken back) is the stored copy's instead: a person's words win over the assistant's suggestion, and that suggestion goes whole, from every paragraph it touched. A new node the stored copy already holds (a save whose answer was lost) is not added twice. A new node whose id another document holds answers 409 with the ids, and the editor gives those nodes fresh ids. A lost connection retries with a growing wait of up to 10 s, and at once when the browser is back online; typing in the meantime sends no more saves. When the editor goes away (another document opens), it saves what waits once more and tries no more; a header's or footer's save does the same, and its warning on leaving goes with the editor. Leaving with unsaved changes tries one last save, which fails quietly offline, and asks the browser to warn. A save whose text is the stored copy again (typed and taken back) sends nothing. The title row shows the state: Saving…, then Saved to Unitos for 3 s, the cloud with a check, Trying to connect…; a change of the state draws the title row again, never the toolbar or the pages. One save runs at a time: every caller waits out the save that runs. A newer stored copy that arrives with the page (another person's save, a server-side edit; the sync refreshes the page while the caret is in the page editor, but not for the page's own save when it was the only change and lost or found no mark: the save's answer carries the project's new revision) replaces the one on screen when nothing waits to be saved; otherwise the next save merges. While another person has the same document open (live sync's presence), the page polls every 2 s instead of every 8 s, so their words land within seconds. A stored or merged copy goes on screen as one change outside the undo history that touches only what differs (`applyStored`): nodes pair by their type and the first blockId inside them, or in place by type when neither is found in the other copy; a paragraph takes one replace of the words that differ; a node without a partner is removed or added whole. No table or cell is made up, and the caret and the marks outside the changed words keep their place. The change carries `STORED_COPY`: someone else's words never close the text toolbar or a comment being written. Ctrl+Z takes back only this person's own steps — with one limit: words another person typed inside a stretch this person added go with it when that addition is taken back. `GET` reads the rich text; `PATCH {pageSetup}` sets the page.
- **Version history** (`components/docs/versions/`, `lib/docs/versions.ts`), Google Docs': a version is the rich text as one sitting of edits left it (`DocumentVersion`: the revision, the rich text, the account that saved it last, the save time, and a name). Every save notes its time and author (`Document.richTextSavedAt`, `richTextSavedBy`). A save 10 minutes or more after the last one starts a new sitting, and the stored text is kept as a version first. Steady typing keeps one version per half hour: a save 30 minutes or more after the newest version (before the first version, after the first edit) keeps the stored text too. There is one version per revision, every version is kept, and none is made from an empty document. See version history (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+H, Search the menus, the clock at the title row's right end) covers the page editor's pane, and the notes tray folds while it is open, as it folds for a side chat; the Unitos rail stays. The top bar has the back arrow, the version's time (a named version's name over its time), and Restore this version. Back, or Escape, closes the view and gives the page the keys at its caret. The version is drawn read-only with the page's own classes and named styles: one sheet as wide as the page, fitted to the canvas down to half size, or the pageless text column. A pane narrower than 600 px (a phone) stacks the panel under the page, each the pane's width: the sheet fits the pane's width, and the list takes at most the lower 45% and scrolls. The panel (320 px) has Only show named versions, then the versions newest first, grouped by day (Today, Yesterday, a date), the current version on top. The panel is the notes tray's sand with a hairline edge, the days in small capitals, the chosen version on clay-100. Each version shows its time or name, its author's dot and name (without sign-in, "You", as the history names the local reader), and ⋯ (Restore this version, Name this version). Show changes (on) draws the version against the version before it: paragraphs pair by blockId at every level, words by the anchors' word diff; added words take the author's color, underlined; removed words stay in place, struck; the oldest version has none before it and shows no marks. Name current version (Search the menus) keeps the live text as a named version at once; a failure shows as a toast, and typing the server does not hold yet (offline) is a failure. Restore this version (the top bar's button, or ⋯) restores at once, with no ask: it saves the text on screen and keeps it as a version, and only then sets the version's text and named styles as one change: the save stores it like any edit, the anchors move, Ctrl+Z takes it back, the replaced text is the newest version in the list, and the restored text is the current version. When the text on screen cannot be saved or kept (offline, a refused save), nothing is restored: the page keeps its text and the panel says "Your current text could not be kept as a version, so nothing was restored. Check your connection and try again." Viewers see the view without Restore and naming. `GET/POST /api/documents/[documentId]/versions`, `GET/PATCH …/versions/[versionId]`.
- **The sync** (`syncRichText`, `lib/docs/sync.ts`). One save runs under a lock on the document's row: it derives the index, creates, updates, and deletes rows, reorders them in one statement, clears the search vector of every paragraph whose words changed, and stores the rich text with its revision bumped. The anchors on paragraphs whose words changed or left move with their words. The paragraphs that changed or left between two unchanged ones form a run — each paragraph its own run when none split, joined, came, or went, so edits in two paragraphs never reach each other's marks; the run's words before and after the save differ in one stretch. A mark outside the stretch moves exactly: into the new paragraph after an Enter, into the joined one after a Backspace. Typing right before or after a mark stays outside it. A change inside a mark grows or shrinks it; an Enter inside it cuts it, it keeps the larger part, and it covers its quote again when the Enter is taken back. A change that takes one end of a mark and words outside it leaves the rest of the mark. A change of letter case alone (Capitalization) keeps every mark on its letters. A mark inside a longer stretch is found by its quote in the run, as quoted or in another case, else by its paragraph's word diff (§5), else by its quote across the document. It orphans only when its words are gone. An orphaned mark on a paragraph the save changed or brought back looks for its quote again, so Ctrl+Z after a deletion brings the mark back with the next save. Link ends (§13) move the same way. The history (§12) gets one row per paragraph added, removed, retyped, or restyled; typing merges into the account's last row for the paragraph while it is under 10 minutes old, and a paragraph added and removed inside that window leaves no row.
- **Server-side edits.** The block routes — the assistant's approved plans (edit, insert, remove, format, style), the history's restore, an image dropped on a paragraph — act on blocks. On a blank document each one is applied to the rich text under the same lock (`lib/docs/ops.ts`, `editRichText` in `lib/docs/server.ts`), and the save brings the rows in line, so an editor's save and a server edit never overwrite each other. The reader saves the editor's typing before it runs a plan. On a document with rich text the assistant proposes no block actions: its changes to the words and styles are the assistant's suggestions (below).
- **Older blank documents.** A blank document made before the page editor holds only rows: no file, no source, no video, not generated, every block user-authored (`isOlderBlankDocument`). On its first open by an editor, its rows become rich text once (`richTextFromBlocks`), each paragraph keeping its block's id and its styles as marks, a list's lines becoming list items.
- **The page** (`components/docs/docs-editor.tsx`). Until the editor stands, its frame does (`components/docs/frame.tsx`): the title row (an untitled title drawn gray, as the title row draws it), the toolbar's row, the ruler's row, and a page of the document's size with gray lines where the words will stand, pulsing (a pageless document: the lines in its text column), drawn by the reader while the editor's code loads and the editor builds, so the pane says the document is opening, not that it is empty. Its look is `components/docs/frame.css`, which loads with the reader; the rest of the page editor's look (`components/docs/docs.css`) loads with the editor's code, so no other page loads it. A document opens with the caret at the page's start, unless something else has the focus. A document that opens at the reading position (§6) has the caret there: at the start of the block at the reading line, or at the start of that block's first line in view when its start is under the title row and the toolbar; while the reading position holds the pane, the caret follows it, until the reader's first press, key, wheel, or touch. The first key types where the reader looks, and the pane stays. The title row: the clay document symbol, the title (click to rename, Enter or leaving the field saves, Escape cancels), and the save state. Under it the toolbar. Then the canvas (the paper) and the pages: Letter at 100% is 816 × 1056 px, 1 in margins, white, a 1 px hairline and Unitos's soft shadow; the page is the reader's `article`, so every card docks beside it. The text defaults are Google Docs': Normal text is Arial 11 pt at 1.15 line spacing, no space before or after; Title 26 pt (3 pt after); Subtitle 15 pt gray #666666 (16 pt after); Heading 1 20 pt (20 before, 6 after); Heading 2 16 pt (18, 6); Heading 3 14 pt #434343 (16, 4); Heading 4 12 pt #666666 (14, 4); Heading 5 11 pt #666666 (12, 4); Heading 6 11 pt italic #666666 (12, 4); headings are not bold. Line spacing S draws as a line height of S × 1.15 of the font size; space before and after is padding, so a paragraph's space after and the next one's space before add up. Bullets run ●, ○, ■ by level; numbers run 1., a., i.; a list line is as tall as a paragraph line. A checklist's box is an outlined square in the text's color, with a check inside when ticked; a ticked line is struck through, and a line nested under it keeps its own look. Tables are a one-point black grid with 5 pt inside each cell; images sit on their own line; a page break (Ctrl+Enter) prints the rest on a new page. An empty line where the caret stands reads "Type @ to insert" (the "@" menu's hint). The page's faces are Google Docs' font list (`components/docs/fonts.ts`): a run stores the plain name, and the page draws it with a fallback of the same shape (Arial → Arimo, Times New Roman → Tinos, Courier New → Cousine, Calibri → Carlito, Cambria → Caladea); the web faces load from Google Fonts. Computer Modern, the face an import names for TeX's text fonts (§31), draws in KaTeX's copy (KaTeX_Main, the face of the page editor's equations: words and formulas take one weight whatever fonts the computer has; a letter it lacks, é, takes the computer's Latin Modern or CMU Serif) and is not in the font menu. Each face, size, and color is a mark on the run; each style, alignment, spacing, and indent is an attribute of the paragraph.
- **The pages** (`components/docs/areas/page.tsx`, `components/docs/css/page.css`). The canvas and the chrome are Unitos's paper, and so is a white page, so the page editor reads as part of Unitos: the page is set off by its hairline and shadow alone, and it prints white. A Letter page is 816 × 1056 px at 100% (the page size in points × 96/72), with a 1 px hairline outside the page box, square corners, and the soft shadow, which print leaves out. Pages sit 8 px apart, border to border; the first page's border is 10 px under the ruler. The page stands centered, and the canvas scrolls sideways when the page is wider than the pane, with its scrollbar at the pane's foot, where Google Docs keeps it. The title row, the toolbar, and the ruler stay put while the canvas scrolls under them. While the Unitos layer's cards sit in the page's right margin, the page moves left by the pane's `--docs-shift`, but stops 24 px from the canvas's left edge, or 16 px right of the open contents panel; while the panel would cover the page, the page moves right past it, and the canvas scrolls sideways when the page then runs past the pane's right edge: no text sits under the panel. With the pointer at the pane's right edge, a tip beside the scrollbar's thumb reads the page in view and the page count ("3 of 12"). In dark mode a white page is drawn as the dark card with ink words, its grays and table grid in warm sand; a page with a color keeps its color, and its words, links, headings, code blocks, and table grid keep the light theme's colors. A sheet and a pinned table row paint the same ground (`--docs-ground`).
- **Zoom**: Fit and 50–200% scale the pages, the text, the gaps, and the rulers' ticks together (CSS zoom on the page); the chrome keeps its size. Fit fills the pane's width, beside the contents panel when it is open. A pane narrower than the page opens the page at Fit. Ctrl + = and Ctrl + − (Ctrl+Alt+= and Ctrl+Alt+− too) step through the zooms, Ctrl + 0 is 100%, Ctrl+Alt+[ is Fit. A zoom keeps the caret's line where it was on screen.
- **Pagination** (`components/docs/ext/page.ts`, `components/docs/page/paginate.ts`), Google Docs' way: line by line. The text is one flow over one sheet per page (`data-docs-page-sheet`). A spacer at each page's end — a widget decoration, never content — pushes what follows to the next page's text top, so a paragraph splits between two lines. A paragraph keeps at least two lines on each side of a split (Prevent single lines), or moves whole. The Title, the Subtitle, and the headings never split and stay on the page of the paragraph after them (Keep lines together, Keep with next). A table row, an image, and a line move whole; a row taller than a page runs past the page's end. A table's pinned header rows repeat at the top of each page the table continues on. Each footnote stands at the foot of the page that holds its number, above the footer; the page's first footnote sits under a short line, a third of the text width. The page's text ends above its footnotes, and a line whose footnotes have no room left moves to the next page. A page break takes no room and sends what follows to the next page. A paragraph's own Line & paragraph spacing options win over its style's. A pass reads the layout with every spacer hidden, works out where the spacers go, and sets each spacer's bottom on its page's text top. It runs one frame after a change, skips an edit that left its paragraph as tall as it was, and waits out a composition and a mouse selection. A spacer, a repeated header row, and a footnote's place never touch the document, the selection, or the undo history.
- **Page setup** (`components/docs/page/setup-dialog.tsx`): Google Docs' dialog, opened from its command and from the page button in the corner where the two rulers meet. The Pages tab: the orientation, the paper size (Docs' eleven sizes) and the page color side by side, and the margins in inches (centimeters in Chinese). The Pageless tab: what the format does, and the background color. OK applies the tab's format and the settings (`PATCH /api/documents/[documentId]/rich-text {pageSetup}`). Set as default keeps the Pages settings in the browser, and a new document takes them. `PageSetup` adds the header and footer margins (0.5 in when absent), the header and the footer, Different first page, and the first page's number; an older stored setup reads as before. Format has Switch to Pageless format and Switch to Pages format.
- **Pageless**: no page edges, borders, or gaps; a white page draws on the paper, as the reader draws an article; a background color fills the canvas and, in the light theme, the chrome (in dark mode the chrome stays on the dark paper). The text column is centered, at most Text width's cap (Narrow 830, Medium 1030, Wide 1230, Full: the pane), a choice kept per browser, and at least 600 px; a pane too narrow for 600 px (a phone, the notes tray beside a small window) takes the column to its 24 px gutters, so no line runs past the pane, and a table wider than the column scrolls sideways. Below its cap, the column leaves the toolbar and the cards their room at its right (the page moves left for a card, as The margin says) while it keeps 600 px. There is no vertical ruler and no header or footer; a page break takes no room, and the footnotes sit at the document's end. Under the pointer, a heading shows an arrow at its left (Collapse heading, Expand heading): it folds the paragraphs under the heading, down to the next heading of its level or above, and a caret in them moves to the heading's end. Folds are not saved.
- **Show print layout** off (View, kept per browser): each page keeps only its text area, edge to edge, with a dotted line where a page starts; no headers or footers.
- **Full screen** (View, Search the menus): the title row, the toolbar, and both rulers hide and the page moves up, as in Google Docs; a toast says "Full screen. Press Esc to show the menus." The page keeps its keys (the toolbar stays mounted, hidden). Esc brings everything back, unless a menu or a dialog takes it first.
- **The rulers** (`components/docs/page/ruler.tsx`): a 15 px strip 8 px under the toolbar whose 0 is the left margin, a tick every 1/8 in (0.25 cm), a number every inch (cm) counted outward from the margin, and the margin areas a shade darker. The margin areas drag the left and right margins. Three clay markers drag the paragraph's left indent (the first-line bar goes with it), its first-line indent (a hanging indent too), and its right indent. A click on the ruler over the text column offers Add left tab-stop, Add center tab-stop, and Add right tab-stop, at the click snapped to 1/16 in (0.25 cm). A tab stop is a clay marker (left ▶, center ◆, right ◀); it drags along the ruler, and dragged down off the ruler it goes. A paragraph keeps its own tab stops (`tabStops`: "72:left 144:center", in points from the text column's left edge); the ruler shows the caret's paragraph's, and a change goes to every selected paragraph. A tab reaches the next stop: the words after it start at a left stop, center on a center stop, and end at a right stop. Past the last stop, and in a paragraph without stops, a tab reaches the next half inch. A line may break before a tab: a tab that finds no room goes to the next line, and the word before it stays. Words to a right stop at the line's end that find no room before it go to the next line with their tab and end at the stop there, as TeX sets a proof's box. An underlined tab draws its line under the whole tab, as Word draws a blank to fill in (Chromium draws no underline under a tab: `page/tabs.ts` gives the tab's span a line in the words' color, which prints). While a marker or a margin moves, a clay guide runs down the page and an ink pill shows the value to two decimals; the change lands on release, one undo step.
