A format change on a block a suggestion adds is part of that suggestion. In Suggesting mode, Select all starts at the first line the page shows. Review suggested edits (Search the menus; hold Ctrl+Alt, press O then U) opens a box under the toolbar: the number of suggestions, the previous and the next one, Accept all, and Reject all; Accept all suggestions and Reject all suggestions are commands too. The box's Accept all and Reject all leave the focus in the page, so Ctrl+Z takes them back. Opened on every suggestion, it gives the keys to the card of the suggestion at the caret (else the first): the focus is on its Accept, Tab moves to Reject, Enter presses, and the next suggestion's Accept takes the focus (the page, when none is left); Escape goes back to the caret. Viewing mode shows the document without its suggestions, and a copy made there (Ctrl+C) holds what it shows. The assistant is an author too: its suggestions' author is `assistant-<account id of the person who asked>` (`assistantAuthor`, `lib/docs/assistant-suggestions.ts`), and its person — ✦ on the assistant's kind color, named Assistant (`assistantPerson`, `lib/person.ts`; `useAuthor` returns it) — draws on its cards and marks.
- **The assistant's suggestions** (SPEC.md §4 `suggest`), Google Docs' Gemini edits the Unitos way: the assistant changes a document with rich text only through suggestions, which stay pending until an editor accepts them. Unlike Gemini's, they are not private: the document has one stored copy, so every collaborator sees them, and each card names who asked. **The landing** (`components/docs/suggest/assistant.ts`, `applyAssistantOps`): the ops go through Suggesting mode's own code (`suggest`, `ext/suggest.ts`) with the assistant as author. The whole landing is one transaction: one repaint, one undo step; the reader's caret and selection map through, and nothing scrolls. Each op is a suggestion of its own, never joined to one beside it. Words change by word: a replacement or a rewrite strikes and adds only the words that differ, one suggestion per stretch; changes two words apart or closer join, and when more than 60% of the words change, the change is one stretch. A "\n" is a line break. New blocks go between blocks, never inside a paragraph: after the block named (into its list as lines when they are a list of its kind, else after the list it stands in), or at the document's start; a list line replaced or removed takes its list item, and a list whose every line goes takes the list. A style change runs the list buttons' and the Styles menu's own commands, and lands after the other ops of the landing, so a line it makes a list line keeps the words changed in it; an alignment runs the toolbar's own command; a line spacing, a space before or after, or an indent sets the paragraph's attribute as Line & paragraph spacing and the ruler set it; a link, a color, a highlight color, a font, or a size goes on the words as the toolbar puts it, beside their other text styles. An image line of new blocks goes in as an image block, as By URL inserts it. A table's rows and columns change through the table's own commands (`prosemirror-tables`), a new row's or column's cells filled with its words in the same step; a row moves as its removal and a copy with new ids at its place, and a column cell by cell the same way; a merged cell refuses the move. A new footnote goes in as the footnote button puts it: its number right after the words, its text in the footnotes at the end, in order. A block equation's TeX and a figure's caption change as the node's attribute, shown on the card as Replace: “old” with “new”; an inline equation is replaced whole by one with the new TeX, so Reject all brings the old one back. Reject all puts the page back byte for byte. New words with a format of their own replace the words whole. Before an op lands it is checked against the page as it stands, and fails when its words no longer read `find` at their offsets or at `find`'s one place in the block, when a block no longer reads its `base` or another row stands between blocks replaced together, or when a style is no longer its `baseStyle` as the paragraph index reads it. A failed op is skipped as "changed"; "object" skips a change that would strike a smart chip, a footnote's number, an inline equation, or a bookmark, or cut through a chip's label or an inline equation's TeX; "overlap" skips an op that meets what an earlier op of the same landing changed (a list toggled joins the list beside it). The asker's pending assistant suggestions on the words an op changes, or on the blocks around them, give way: they are rejected in the same undo step (a style change gives way only to a style, an alignment only to an alignment, a spacing only to a spacing, an indent only to an indent). A word change in a block this landing restyled lands: a line made a heading keeps the word fixed in it. A follow-up in place of a pending command passes that command's ids (`replacing`); they are rejected in the same undo step. Another person's suggestions, and the assistant's for another account, stack. Viewing mode hides suggestions, so a landing in Viewing passes into Suggesting mode for this visit: the mode an import remembers stays as the reader chose it, and the page takes no keys. The suggestions read as Gemini's in Google Docs: removed words struck in the assistant's kind color, added words in the text's own color on a tint of it, light and dark, and on a page with a color in the light theme's color. The card shows ✦ Assistant, the time, "Asked by <name>" in a shared project, the change (an empty line added or removed reads as ¶, "Delete: “¶”"), and under it the op's why while the page that landed it is open; after a reload the card shows the change alone. A command's suggestions settle together (`settleSuggestions` with their ids, one undo step). Review suggested edits opens on one command's suggestions (`docs:review-suggestions` with `{ids}`): the count, previous and next, Accept all, and Reject all act on those alone, the first one selected. Search the menus has Ask the assistant and the seven commands, which fire `docs:unitos-tool` `{tool: "assistant", command}`. In development, `window.__applyAssistantOps(ops, replacing?)` lands fixed ops with no model. **The route.** `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/suggest` (editor; rich text and no format, else 400; no key, 503) runs a command over the named blocks (a heading grows to its section) or the whole document. **Windows.** The scope is cut into windows of 8,000 characters of text (`SUGGEST_WINDOW_CHARS`); a window ends before a heading once past half its size, and never inside a list or a table. At most 12 windows run (`SUGGEST_MAX_WINDOWS`, about 16,000 words; past it the command says so), four at once, the first wave's later windows a second behind the first so they read its cache. **The version.** A command over the whole document, or over more than one window, first keeps the stored text as a version named "Before the assistant's suggestions"; a version the reader named keeps its name. **The stream.** NDJSON (`SuggestEvent`), one line per window as it finishes. Windows not started by 270 s (`SUGGEST_DEADLINE_MS`) are reported, and Stop aborts every call. **The selection chat.** `/api/assistant/act` runs one call for a command (`suggestCommand`, no chat model) or its suggest action, on the selected words, and answers `plan.suggestions`. **The prompt.** `lib/prompts/suggest.ts` carries the command, the instruction and answer passed on, the scope, each block's style, the caret ("here"), the asker's pending assistant suggestions (a new op on their words takes their place), the conversation, the seventeen ops, and the rules. **Cost.** One Claude Sonnet 5 call per command on selected words; up to twelve, one per window, for a whole document. Every call records usage under `suggest`.
- **The assistant's bar** (`reader-interactions.tsx`). On a blank document, for an editor out of Viewing mode, the text toolbar's Assistant (its row reads Edit with the assistant there), the right-click menu's Ask the assistant, Search the menus' Ask the assistant, and Ctrl+Alt+G (⌘+Option+G) open a bar docked at the bottom of the pane, over the page. It works on the selected words, or on the paragraph the caret stands in when nothing is selected; the words stay selected, gray. It holds the field ("Tell the assistant what to do with the selected words…", the words of the toolbox's Assistant box too), the spoken command, Send, and seven commands as chips: Rephrase, Shorten, Elaborate, Make more formal, Make more casual, Turn into a bulleted list, Fix spelling and grammar, with Fast Thinking and Deep Thinking. Search the menus runs each command by name. A chip or an instruction is one request of the selection chat (`/api/assistant/act`; a chip sends `suggestCommand`), on the conversation note anchored to the words. While it runs, the bar reads Writing suggestions… with Stop. The suggestions land in the text as the assistant's suggestions, and the bar shows one status line: the summary, the number of the edit's suggestions still in the text, 👍/👎, Accept and Reject (the whole edit, one undo step; the bar closes), and the button that opens the conversation in the chat card beside the words. The field stays for a follow-up: its suggestions take the place of the edit still pending, which is rejected in the same undo step. A turn that lands no suggestions (an answer, a plan) goes on in the chat card. Escape or a press anywhere else closes the bar and stops nothing: what lands stays pending, with its cards. In Viewing mode and on every other document, the text toolbar's Assistant keeps its own box. A viewer has no text toolbar.
- **The suggestions row** (`components/assistant/suggestion-row.tsx`). It sits under an answer that landed suggestions, in the chat card and in the assistant panel. It shows the number of the command's suggestions still in the text, counted again as anyone accepts or rejects one; Review (Review suggested edits over the command's suggestions, the first one selected); Accept all and Reject all (the command's suggestions, one undo step each); 👍/👎 (rating tool `suggest`); "N skipped", which lists why each change did not land (the server's reasons, and the page's: the words changed while the assistant worked, or the words hold an object the assistant cannot change); and the run's notes (a failed window, too much for one run, out of time). In the panel it leads with the summary. A typed command in the chat card that the assistant turns into suggestions replies with their summary, and a follow-up there replaces the thread's pending suggestions, as in the bar.
- **The whole document from the panel.** At This page scope on a blank document, an answer's `suggest` action never reaches the plan card. The panel hands it to the open document's reader (`dissect:assistant-suggest`, by document id; one pane takes it). The reader saves its typing and runs `POST /api/documents/[documentId]/suggest` with the reader's message, the instruction, the named blocks, the paragraph the caret stands in, the answer, and the conversation so far. Each window's suggestions land as the window returns; the row reads Writing suggestions… with Stop, and counts up. Stop ends the stream and keeps what landed. Another document opening stops the run the same way, and the row keeps its count without its buttons. Every message at This page scope sends the caret's paragraph (`caretBlockId`), so "here" is the caret.

## 30. Imports: PDFs, web pages, text files, and Word files in the page editor

An import is a document made from a PDF judged an article, a web page, a Markdown or text file, or a Word file (.docx). It opens in the page editor (§29) as one continuous rich text, the way Google Docs opens a converted file, with Unitos's layer on top: the selection tools, the annotations, the notes, and the assistant's suggestions. A PDF opens in pages at its own paper size, with its own page numbers in the margin. A web page, a text file, and a Word file open pageless, with no page numbers. Handwritten pages, slides, sheets, video and audio, generated documents, and a media document's article keep their own views.

- **The switch.** Imports are off until the operator turns them on from the admin dashboard (`/admin`, the Imports section at the top; `POST /api/admin/imports`, `lib/docs/import-switch.ts`; the `AppSetting` row `imports`, no row = off), as billing's switch works (§24). On, an add is an import (`importPageEditorOn`); off, every add is a block document, as before. It is read at import only: a document made while it was on keeps opening in the page editor, since `Document.richText` decides.
- **The conversion** (`richTextFromImport`, `lib/docs/import.ts`). One converter over the parse's blocks for every kind; the Block rows are never copied from the parse, they are `deriveBlocks` of the rich text (§29 The paragraph index), so the first save changes only the paragraph typed in.
  - The title is a Title paragraph when it came from the original (the PDF's title, the page's title, the front matter or the first "# heading"), none when it fell back to the file name or the address. A PDF's Title keeps its lines where the writer broke them (`ParsedDocument.titleLines`); the document's name stays one line. A heading among the first 12 blocks that repeats the title becomes the Title where it stands, so the title stands once. A PDF's Title opens the page its title stands on, its page start naming that page: a later page where an earlier page's words come first (an archive's notice page, a deck's first slide; `ParsedDocument.titlePage`). The Title keeps the alignment the parse read (`titleAlign`; a PDF's title it read flush left stays flush left, a scan's too); where the parse read none (a web page, a text file), a centered kicker, metadata line, or first heading centers it.
  - The layout tokens (§6) become the page editor's formats: a kicker, a label, and a caption 9 pt (a caption centered), a display line 21 pt, the metadata line the Subtitle, quote lines one blockquote, and center, right, and justify the alignment. A paragraph's indent is its indents in points as the page sets them: left, first line or hanging, and right (`ParsedBlock.indent`: a PDF's as the parse measures it, a Word file's w:ind, the right indent from its right or end); a token that names the kind without the size is half an inch (`indent-first` a first-line indent, `indent-hanging` a hanging indent, `indent-block` a left indent). A paragraph, a heading, and a list's lines keep the tab stops the parse gives (`ParsedBlock.tabStops`), so a tab stands at the page's stop. A PDF's right stop stands at its column's right edge, so at the page editor's (less the paragraph's right indent), and no stop of a PDF lies past that edge; a Word file's stops stand where Word sets them. A tab after a list's marker goes with the marker. A paragraph of tabs alone (a line to write on) stays, and a list that resumes after a line or two between its items does not count it. A Word paragraph's borders (`ParsedBlock.borders`: a rule under a heading, a bar beside a quote; each side's width, dash, color, and padding to the words) become the paragraph's borders: a bar at the left stands inside the indent, so the words start where Word starts them, and a quote that draws its own bar shows it in place of the blockquote's. Paragraphs with a bar at the left and no line above or below, one after another, draw one bar, as Word draws them: the space between them lies inside it.
  - The page's look (a PDF's or a Word file's: `bodyFont`, `titleFont`, `ParsedBlock.font`, §31) sets the named styles, stored the way Update 'X' to match stores them (§29 Named styles): Normal text takes the body's face, size, and color; the Title the title's look; each heading level the look most of its headings' letters take, bold or italic only where every heading of the level is. A face a style shares with Normal text is Normal text's, and where the parse measured the page's spacing a heading style has no space before. A block set otherwise than its named style carries a mark only where it differs: its face, its size (a kicker, a label, a caption, a footnote, and a display line keep their role's size), and a heading's bold its style lacks; an italic heading keeps its italic runs. An import's Title and Headings 1 and 2 keep their own color, as in Docs: Normal text's color does not reach them (`docs.css`; a blank document's take Normal text's color, as they always have).
  - Headings keep their level, 1 to 6. A run-in heading (the parse's `run-in` token) is a heading with `runIn`. It draws at the start of the paragraph under it, on its first line, in its level's look, with a word space before the paragraph's words. It stands at the page's indent (the parse's indent of its line: `indentLeft`, `indentFirstLine`), and its box reaches one pixel under its first line, so a lead with a tall formula moves no later line. The contents panel, the paragraph index, and Keep with next read it as a heading. Lists come from the marker lines: the kind and the start number from the first marker, nested by the indent, a break in the numbering a new list at its number, "☐"/"☑" lines a checklist. A checklist keeps its page's depths as any list does (`listIndents`): its box at the first line's start, its words after it, never closer than the box's width, and every wrap back at the depth's left (`toolbar/lists.ts` listSheet); a checklist of the page editor's own keeps its box beside its words. Each level draws the page's markers (§29 List presets): a counter's words around its number ("(a)", "a).", "[12]", "A-1.", "15" with no stop) are that level's glyph format, a legal number ("2.3.1") draws the numbers above its own, a one-letter numeral after the letter before it is a letter ("(h)" then "(i)"), and a PDF's or a Word file's bullet draws as printed ("◦" as ○, "▪" as ■; a Word file's "•" as Word's small bullet, a PDF's "•" the level's own), a web page's or a text file's as the level's own. The outermost list takes the preset that draws its levels, else its own levels (`listLevels`); a line that draws its level otherwise than the list's lines so far starts a new list. A list that picks up again after a line or two goes on the list before, its last item holding those lines. A line that opens with no marker (a bibliography's entry, its author-year label such as "[Knu84]" and a capital's initial under an entry with no marker ("H. rept. 106-371.") kept as its words; an algorithm's line; a line that opens with a formula, whose characters are never a marker) is a line of a list with no marker (§29 List presets). A list whose lines all have no marker draws none at every level, so a line nested or lifted stays unmarked; a line with no marker among marked ones starts its own list at its depth. A list with a line whose counter the page editor cannot draw stays one paragraph per line, indented as printed, its markers as words. A contents list is one paragraph per entry, each a link to its heading (`#heading=<blockId>`).
  - A paragraph and a list's last line take the space the page leaves under them (`ParsedBlock.spaceAfter`), or the document's most common where the parse measured none for the block; a heading takes its own where measured. A PDF's display equation takes the space the page leaves over and under it, measured from its glyphs to the lines' own boxes, their scripts left out (§31 `glyphBox`, `lineBox`), less 0.1 em of the words' size over it and 0.07 em under it at Docs' 1.15 line spacing, and half of what their line is set closer than that (the page editor's line reaches further past its baseline than the parse's box); where the parse measured none, 4 pt. The page editor draws a PDF's display as tall as its formula (KaTeX's line box off). A Word file's display takes Word's spacing. A Word file's and a PDF's lines keep the spacing the page sets (`ParsedBlock.lineSpacing`, a multiple of single spacing: Word's w:line, a PDF paragraph's line pitch over 1.15 of its size, §31 step 9): Normal text takes the one most of the paragraphs' letters take, and a paragraph set otherwise keeps its own (a PDF's 3% or more off, its measure's noise; a Word file's 0.01). Docs' 1.15 drew a single-spaced file's lines, and its table rows, a tenth taller than Word draws them, and a PDF's lines up to 15% farther apart than its page. A PDF's table keeps its own lines (below); Normal text's spacing does not reach its cells. In a PDF or Word import the page editor draws a display with no margin of its own, and KaTeX's display none either (`css/import.css`). A blank document's displays, and a web page's or text file's, keep theirs. Where the parse measures no spacing (a web page, a text file), a paragraph and a list's last line get 10 pt, Docs' Add space after paragraph. A list's other lines take the space the page leaves between its items (`ParsedBlock.itemSpace`), every line takes its list's alignment (items set justified), and the outermost list takes its page's depths, each from the first line set at it, with the words' place after a marker that does not hang (`ParsedBlock.listIndents` as `listIndents`, §29 List presets).
  - Inside the words, the parse's bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, and code are marks; a text color and a background color are the page editor's Text color and Text background (a color the named style gives is none), and a run's face and size are its own; a code run takes no face, size, color, or highlight color (the page editor's code mark holds no other mark); small caps (a theorem label, a defined term) is a mark of its own, drawn in the font's small capitals (Docs has no such format: no command sets it, Clear formatting takes it off); small capitals a page draws as capitals at a smaller size (a browser's or a word processor's own) are that mark over the letters in lowercase, at the words' size (`importWords`); a lowered or raised run outside math (H₂O, 10³, "1st") is Docs' subscript or superscript. An inline formula (`ParsedBlock.math`: the text keeps its readable characters, the span holds its TeX) is an inline equation of its TeX in place of those characters; a page that begins inside one begins at its start, and a formula without TeX or past 2,000 characters stays words. An import's inline formula of 16 drawn characters or more may break after a relation or an operator at its top level, where KaTeX lets a line break, as TeX breaks one; a shorter one, and a blank document's, stays whole. A formula's closing marks and the space after it stay on its line (`insert/math.ts`).
  - A footnote the parse linked to its reference is the page editor's own: its number where the label stood, its words (the label gone) in the footnotes at the document's end, in the numbers' order. A footnote the page marks with a symbol (`ParsedBlock.footnote.label` of one to three of "*", "∗", "⋆", "†", "‡", "§", "¶", "‖", "∥") draws that symbol in place of a number, at its reference and before its words, and takes no number, as Word draws a custom mark (`footnoteReference`'s `symbol`). The Word download writes it as Word's custom mark (w:customMarkFollows and the symbol, the symbol opening the footnote), the web page and plain text as the symbol. A PDF's and a Word file's footnote keeps the size the page sets it in (a text size on its words; the page editor draws footnotes at 10 pt). A footnote cited in a table's cell is the page editor's footnote in that cell, a Word file's and a PDF's alike. On a PDF's first page, a footnote whose label ends the title (LaTeX's `\thanks`) is cited at the Title's end, its number in place of the title's mark. A footnote the page marks nowhere stays a paragraph where the parse sets it, at the size the page sets it in: a number on the Title would be a mark the page lacks. A footnote with no reference stays a paragraph where it stands, at the size the page sets it in, its label as words.
  - A code block, an equation (its TeX, its number at the left margin where the page sets it there: `leqno`; past 2,000 characters a code block of the TeX), a horizontal line, a figure object, and a citation mark keep what the parse found. U+200B leaves the words (the page editor holds a suggested paragraph break with it), and a run past 200,000 characters splits. The sanitizer, which the import and every save go through, keeps no half of a surrogate pair (a letter past the Basic Multilingual Plane cut in two fails the database write): in development the save fails with the words around it; in production the half drops and the server logs it once.
- **Tables** (`lib/docs/import-table.ts`) become page-editor tables from the parse's table html: header cells (their rows repeat on each page, unless every row is a header), colspan and rowspan (a colspan past the table's columns spans the table: the columns are the colgroup's, or the most a row fills), one paragraph per cell paragraph with its alignment and left indent (a left indent counts toward its column's least width), a cell's vertical alignment (middle, bottom), a `<br>` a line break, an inline formula an inline equation, and the words' marks, color, highlight color, size, and face; a cell's fill and sides, a row's least height (the height the parse writes on a cell of one row), and the table's text size and column widths as the page sets them. In a PDF import, a grid's cells take the room the page leaves over and under their words (`data-cell-padding`). Otherwise a PDF or Word import's cell has no padding above and below its words and 5 pt at the sides. A Word table's cells take the margins most of its cells have (`w:tcMar` over `w:tblCellMar`; `data-cell-padding`, the table's `cellPadding`). A table whose words all carry a size draws each cell's line at the size most of its letters take (`cellSize`). A blank document's cells, and a web page's or text file's, keep 5 pt on every side. Each attribute of the parse's html is read as untrusted and kept only under its test (a color #rrggbb, a length in points within range, a face of letters, digits, spaces, and hyphens). A table's caption is a centered paragraph above the table, or under it where the parse sets caption-side: bottom. A PDF's caption is at the page's size and 9 pt at most; a Word file's is at its own size and the body's at most. It keeps its marks and its page start. A caption under its table gives its page start to the first row. A PDF's table sets its lines about 1.2 of its words' size apart, as TeX and Word set a table (9 pt words on 11 pt; `css/import.css`); a cell's own line spacing still wins. A formula in an import's cell takes the room of its ink (KaTeX's struts), not KaTeX's own line, so a cell of "G₁₃" or "g⁴" keeps its words' line; so does a PDF's inline formula in a line of words, as TeX sets one: only its ink pushes the lines apart (KaTeX's 1.2 em line stood every line with a formula 1.27 em apart where the page sets 1.2). A cell's paragraph is its own row of the paragraph index, as in a blank document, and its row names its place (`Block.cell`: the table's number in the document, the row, the column of the cell's first grid slot), so the AI tools read a cell as `[block <id>] (PARAGRAPH, table 2, row 3, column 1)`.
- **Import in one transaction** (`createImportedDocument`): the Document with its rich text, page setup, and page labels; its `FigureMedia` rows; the rows through the sync's bulk mode (`syncRichText({ bulk: true })`: no history row per paragraph, no sitting version, `importRev` set to the new revision); and the named version "Imported" (`keepNamedVersion`), so Version history always holds the original's words.
- **The size guard.** An import past 1,500 rows or 1,500,000 bytes of rich text (of the chosen pages alone, §15) is saved as a block document; the upload box says "Too long for the page editor, so it opens in the reader instead." (a PDF's line adds "Add it again and choose its pages.") and stays open until Close (§15), and the document bar's notice says the same.
- **Pages of a PDF.** The page setup is pages at the size of the PDF's first page (clamped to the page setup's bounds; margins 1 in, a sixth of the page on a page narrower than 6 in). The page editor's own pagination runs as for any document; the PDF's pages are page starts:
  - Chosen pages (§15) parse as if the PDF held only them (`parsePdf(bytes, { pages })`): the first is its first page, so a chapter's heading is the title and the Title's page start names that page; each block keeps the PDF's own page number, so page starts read "p. 45" and page labels stay the PDF's own. A choice of fewer than six pages also reads the nearest pages for the furniture's evidence and keeps none of their words; a paragraph never joins across pages the choice left out. `Document.pdfPages` keeps `{ranges, count}` (null = every page): a re-parse reads the same pages, a PDF judged handwritten holds one PAGE block per chosen page, and the scroll tip counts the PDF's pages ("p. 50 of 409").
  - A page start (`pageStart {page}`) is an inline atom at the first word of each PDF page; where a page begins at a figure, an equation, or a code block, that node carries `pageStart` as an attribute. It adds no words: offsets, anchors, Find, copy, and the word count never see it.
  - It draws the PDF's number for that page in the left margin, level with its line ("p. 7"), in the gutter when pageless, and at the right end of the line's first row in a pageless column under 600 px, which has no gutter for it: the PDF's own label when it names its pages otherwise than 1 to n (`Document.pageLabels`: "xii", "1043"), else the number. Page starts on one line draw one label ("p. 7 · p. 8"; three pages or more in a row "p. 7–9"), and a page start in a table cell, in any column, draws in the margin too (`placedPageLabels`). A table's header rows drawn again at the top of the next page draw no page start: the page began once. It never prints. The scroll tip reads the PDF's page in view: "p. 7 of 30". A page the PDF leaves unnumbered among numbered ones (an empty label in `pageLabels`) draws no label, and the scroll tip counts the page editor's pages there.
  - It is kept: a change that takes a page number away (a deletion, Accept of a suggested deletion, Undo, Redo, a collaborator's stored copy) puts it back where the deletion closed, or at the next paragraph's start (`insert/page-start.ts`). The arrows, Backspace, and Delete step over it; Enter right after one moves it into the new line. A paste never brings one.
  - Every row of a PDF import carries `page`, the page its first word stands on. The AI tools, the digest, and Stitch read it after the row's type and any cell place, named as the margin names it: `[block <id>] (PARAGRAPH, p. 7)`, `(PARAGRAPH, table 2, row 3, column 1, p. 7)` (`pageNames`, `renderBlockLines`, `lib/derive/context.ts`), so the assistant can cite pages. Only an import's rows name a page.
- **Figures.** A figure is a figure object (`figure {mediaId, caption, captionStyles, captionMath, captionSize, page, region}`, a block atom; `insert/figure.ts`). Its media lives once per document in `FigureMedia` (`html`, `caption`, `page`, `region`), never in the rich text: a chart's svg runs to hundreds of thousands of characters, and every save sends the whole rich text. The page data sends only the media the rich text names (`figureMedia`, `lib/docs/server.ts`), and `GET …/versions/[versionId]` answers a version's rich text with the media it names. The figure object draws the figure as the block reader does (`.reader-figure`, `figure-media.tsx`: rows side by side, lazy videos, the white plate under a chart in dark mode, and in dark mode a box the page painted under a web figure keeps the light theme's words), a web figure fitted to the page's text width. A PDF figure draws its crop (`/api/documents/<documentId>/figure/<mediaId>`, the URL the page data carries so the offline copy keeps it, §17) at the size the PDF prints it, scaled down to fit the page's text box (`figureCropSize`, `lib/figure-crop.ts`: the page data sends the size, so the crop keeps its place before it loads), over its caption, which is drawn upright in the marks the page sets it in (`captionStyles`: a bold label, italic words, sub and sup). A PDF caption's inline formulas (`ParsedBlock.math` over a FIGURE's caption) are its `captionMath` ({start, end, latex} over the caption's characters): the figure draws each as an equation; the Word download writes its TeX, as an inline equation's; a translated copy drops them. The paragraph index reads the caption's words, a formula as its readable characters (a figure row reads its object's caption). A PDF's and a Word file's figure caption stands in the text's color; a Word file's caption also at its own size and in its runs' marks (the figcaption the parse writes). A figure object's `captionSize` (points) draws its caption at that size, and the Word download writes it; the import sets it from a PDF figure's `ParsedBlock.font` where that size is not Normal text's. A display equation's crop (`ParsedBlock.mathCrop`, §31 step 9) has no caption. Its row is FIGURE: text the caption, html from its `FigureMedia` row. The caption is the figure's, not words in the page: it is not typed into, and an anchor takes the whole figure. A click on a figure opens its tools (Explain, Analyze, the assistant), as the circle does; in Viewing it draws no selection frame, and Escape leaves the figure selected. The save drops a figure whose media is not this document's and resets its caption, page, and region from the media; a pasted figure from another document is dropped with a toast.
- **Citations.** A citation is a mark (`citation {refId}`) with a quiet dotted underline; its row's `citations` carry it, so it moves with its words. In Viewing a click (Ctrl+click in any mode) opens and flashes its entry in the References section, which stands under the page for a web page or a Markdown file. The pointer over a citation shows its card, as the block reader does: the reference entry, and the link's site on its own line.
- **Reading and editing.** An import opens in Viewing, remembered per document per browser (`unitos-docs-mode:<id>`); the page takes no focus, so the pending queue's keys reach the notes tray. Editing and Suggesting are one choice away in the mode menu; a switch out of Viewing with the caret out of view puts it at the start of the block at the reading line (or of its first line in view), so the first key types where the reader looks and the pane stays. Viewing shows an import's comments (a blank document's Viewing hides them, Google's way): on an import they are the reader's annotations. The notes tray keeps its place, open, as the block reader has it. Make a copy (§29) is on for an import: the copy is a blank document of the reader's own, with the import's figures, page labels, and references. An edit changes the import for every project that holds it; `richTextRev > importRev` means edited since import. A PDF import whose pages Fit draws under 75% (the notes tray beside a small window) offers Read pageless over the page in Viewing, and on a pane narrower than 600 px (a phone) or shorter than 500 px (a phone held sideways) every paged document reads pageless at once, in every mode, with no bar and no ruler (on the short pane the text column starts 24 px under the toolbar, not 70) — there the pages are drawn at about 40%, or leave six lines of words under the margins, where no one reads or writes them, and Show pages (Search the menus > View) goes back to them: its words wrap to the pane as the Pageless format draws them, the page starts in the gutter (at a line's end in a column under 600 px). It is a view of this browser, kept per document (`unitos-docs-reflow:<id>`, `page/reflow.tsx`): the page store keeps the drawn page apart from the saved page setup (`page/store.ts`), so the page setup is never changed or saved by it; Page setup opens on the document's own format, and what acts on a drawn page (Header, Footer, Page numbers, Watermark, Line numbers) waits for Show pages. A PDF import on a wider pane draws its pages again in Editing and Suggesting; Show pages (the bar, Search the menus) goes back, and Keep pages ends the offer for the document; Search the menus has Read pageless. Every swap between pages and pageless (Read pageless, Show pages, a switch to Editing or Suggesting and back) keeps the block at the reading line under the line, at the same share of the block, and holds it while the pages settle, until the reader scrolls (`page/keep-place.ts`).
- **The import line** in the title row after the title: "Imported from" the site, a link to the page; "PDF · 30 pages", or with chosen pages "PDF · pages 45–60 of 409" ("page 45 of 409"); "Text file"; or "Word file". Muted, clay under the pointer, hidden when the title row is narrower than 560 px. In a split view, where the title row starts hidden, the same words go under "Choose the document this pane shows" in the tooltip of the pane's document select (`import-line.ts`), so the pane names its document once.
- **Shared imports.** Every add is its own document (§15), so a new import is never shared; a document linked to several projects before that rule stays linked. An import whose projects have two or more distinct owners (`importShared`, `lib/docs/server.ts`) is Viewing only: the mode menu and Search the menus name the reason, the save and every server-side edit answer 403, and the assistant proposes no edit on it: its plans offer no edit action (`DocumentEdits` "none", `lib/assistant/plan.ts`), and the prompt says why. A collaborator on the owner's own project edits as usual.
- **An add never hands out an import**, edited or not: the same file or address added again imports anew, after the ask (§15). The ask matches a PDF by its bytes and its chosen pages: the same file with other pages is another document, and adds without asking.
- **Re-parse of an import** (`reparseImport`, §16): the original is read and converted again; each new row takes the id of an old row with the same type and words, in order (FIGURE rows never), so anchors on them stay exact and the rest move by quote. An import edited since its import re-parses only with the reader's yes: the route answers 409 `edited` unless the body carries `replaceEdits`, and the document's actions ask first ("Re-parse replaces the edits since the import. Version history keeps them." — Replace the edits, Keep the edits). The ask also names the notes and the annotations whose quotes stand on words added since the import, five of each at most ("1 note quotes words added since the import. Its quote will lose its place:"): `GET /api/documents/[id]/reparse` finds each source's quote in the version kept at `importRev` (`resolveAnchor`, `lib/docs/reparse-losses.ts`), and a quote not found there loses its place after the replace. Replace the edits waits for that answer. The replaced text is kept as the version "Before re-parse" (when it was edited; an unedited import's text is already "Imported"), the new text as "Re-parsed", and the history gets one row (`REPARSE`). A silent run (the figure run on open) never replaces an edited import. Re-parse as Handwritten pages leaves the page editor; back to Computer text makes an import again. An open page editor rebuilds on the new `importRev` rather than patching the new text in.
- **The Unitos layer on an import.** Every tool of the selection toolbar works in Viewing and in Editing. A selection that crosses a page start is one passage whose words run on both sides; its mark paints both sides and never the page start. A passage across paragraphs (over a page start, or a paragraph the PDF split) carries its chips once, at its end: the tool's symbol, the comment's icon, and the link's chain. A figure is left out of a passage, as an image is, and the toolbar says so. A mark on a figure (or an equation) is a ring in the kind color with its label chip before the object ("A1 · A2"); each label jumps to and flashes the figure. A link end or a Match-it span on a figure draws its ring too (a link slate, a link's waiting first end sage, Match-it gold), and the chip holds one row per kind — the annotations' labels, the links' chains, the matches' labels ("M1") — each part opening what its chip opens in the text. A jump to any mark on a figure centers the figure. A jump that opens the document waits for the page editor (up to 30 s) and lands on its mark; a jump's own address does not scroll the pane a second time.
- **The assistant's suggestions on an import** (§29): the bar and the panel work as on a blank document. A page start passes through a change: the new words go before it, and the words on both sides are struck. A figure is never changed: an op naming a FIGURE row is skipped with its reason, the windows read a figure as its caption for context, and a figure's tools offer the chat, not the edit commands.
- **A Word file** (`parseDocx`, `lib/parse/docx.ts`; OMML equations to LaTeX in `lib/parse/docx-math.ts`) parses from Word's own structure; nothing is guessed from how a page looks. Headings come from the style or the outline level (the Title style is the title; Subtitle, Caption, Quote, and code styles give their roles); runs keep bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, small caps, sub and sup, their color and highlight color (Word's highlight, else the run's shading), and their face and size where they differ from their block's, and a monospace run is code, unless the body font is monospace; a paragraph keeps its alignment, justified too, its indents (w:ind), its borders (w:pBdr, its style's and its own), its tab stops (w:tabs, its style's and its own: a cleared stop goes, a decimal stop is a right stop, a bar stop is none), and its tabs (a tab stays a tab; a tab at the paragraph's end stays when it is underlined, and a paragraph of underlined tabs alone stays: a line to fill in). The look reads as a PDF's does (§31 step 3): each text block keeps the look most of its letters take, the body's look is the one most of the body's letters take, and the Title paragraph gives the title's look and alignment. A text block's space after is the space Word sets between it and the next text block: the larger of the upper paragraph's space after and the lower one's space before, as Word sets two paragraphs apart (their sum where the file turns Word's HTML paragraph spacing off: w:doNotUseHTMLParagraphAutoSpacing; a paragraph's own is none beside one of its style under contextual spacing), plus the blank paragraphs between them; none where a page ends. A paragraph's line spacing is a multiple of single spacing (w:spacing w:line over 240 under the auto rule; `ParsedBlock.lineSpacing`). Hyperlinks, HYPERLINK fields, and cross-references link to their bookmarks; lists come from numbering.xml with Word's markers ("1.1", "(a)", "(i)", restarts and overrides), a level whose number format is none gives lines of a list with no marker (the first line at the indent, where Word's tab takes it), and a marker typed before a tab is a list line too; a list keeps the space most of its items leave between them, its lines' alignment when they share one, and each depth's indents (the paragraph's own, else its level's, else its style's); a table of contents is a contents list linked to its headings, and a contents field with no entries (LibreOffice leaves it empty) is built from the headings at its levels, each linked, the heading right over the field (its title) left out; tables keep merged cells, header rows, fills, each cell's borders (its own, else its table's and its table style's edges and inside lines), column widths, the text size, each cell paragraph's alignment and indent, and colored words, one shaded cell holding one paragraph stays a table (a label bar), and a nested table is flattened; footnotes and endnotes follow the block that cites them, as a PDF's do (§2 Parsing); pictures and text boxes are figures with their captions (a figure's font is its caption's; a table's `<caption>` holds its words in a span with the face and the size the page sets them in, as a PDF's does), and Word's horizontal line is a horizontal line; an equation is an EQUATION block (display) or a math span (inline), each drawn by KaTeX first, and one KaTeX cannot draw keeps its readable characters. A style defined twice takes its later definition. Tracked changes read as accepted; hidden text, headers, footers, and comments are left out. The file is kept (`Document.fileData`) and dedupes by hash; a re-parse reads it again. A .docx in Drive adds the same way, and so does a Google Doc, through Drive's .docx export (§14).
- **A build that does not know a node.** A stored copy that holds a node or mark type the open build does not know reloads the page once per tab (`unitos-docs-reload:<id>`), then shows the frame with the reason; it is never saved over.
- **Not yet in the page editor.** Figure places for a caption left without its figure.

---

## 31. The PDF parse

`parsePdf` (`lib/parse/pdf/`) reads a PDF, or the pages of it the reader chose (§15), into blocks with no model pass. A perfect parse holds the page's words in reading order; no running head, running foot, or page number in the text; headings, paragraphs, lists, and indents as the page sets them; every table cell's words inside its cell; math as TeX, inline and display, never a picture of a formula or a wrong character; a figure's labels inside the figure; bold, italic, small caps, sub, and sup as the fonts set them; underline, strikethrough, highlight, and color as the drawing sets them; each block's face, size, and alignment as the page sets them. The steps, in order:

1. **The drawing** (`drawing.ts`). One walk over each page's operator list, the way pdf.js draws text, gives every glyph (its font, character code, origin, advance, size, and fill color; hidden when drawn outside the clip or the page box, or with its middle off the page box: a scan's OCR read the paper's edge as "—"), the rules (a fraction bar, a table border; an image mask drawn as a line is a rule too) and filled boxes with their fill colors (a see-through fill mixed with the white under it), the boxes of images and vector paths, and the box a shading paints, its clip (Beamer paints its list bullets so). A rule, an image, and a path show only inside their clip: KaTeX draws a radical's bar 400 em long and clips it to its formula. A box that the fill rule cuts out of another box of the same filled path (even-odd, or turns summing to zero under nonzero) is a hole and paints nothing; the box around it paints only the bands around the hole, each a rule or a filled box (OpenStax draws each table's border so, and the hole read as a black box under every cell). Text under a point tall and wide is not on the page for a reader (LaTeXiT stores a formula's source that way) and is dropped. Every position is measured from the page box's corner, so a figure's region is a share of the page as it is drawn (a page box that does not start at (0, 0) moved every crop up and right). A CID font without a Unicode map reads through Adobe's CMaps, which the app ships (`lib/parse/pdf/cmaps/`, pdf.js's packed CMaps; `PDF_CMAPS` in `lib/pdf-runtime.ts`, passed wherever the app opens a PDF; `next.config.ts` traces the files into the API functions), so a Japanese or Chinese PDF keeps its CJK words.
2. **The glyphs** (`glyphs.ts`, `math-fonts.ts`). The text layer's string is whatever the PDF's font maps each code to: a TeX math font without a Unicode map reads ϵ as a control character and ≠ as "6=". So a TeX math glyph is read by its character code in its family's table (`math-fonts.ts`, generated by `scripts/math-fonts/generate.py` from TeX Live, KaTeX's metrics, and the MATH tables of OpenType math fonts: each code's LaTeX, Unicode character, class, and box). A math font set in Unicode — KaTeX's (a web page printed) or an OpenType math font (Latin Modern Math, STIX Two Math, XITS; Cambria Math from Word, whose size variants are not in the tables yet) — reads as TeX's fonts do, though its codes are glyph ids: each of its glyphs takes the family and code of the same symbol in TeX's tables (one no table knows fails its formula's check), a size variant keeps its own box (KaTeX stands a big ∑ on the baseline, TeX hangs it from its origin), and every later step reads it as TeX's. A math font of TeX's world whose text reads right but whose codes follow no TeX table throughout (STIX's first fonts, newtxmath's, Libertine's, MathTime's, MnSymbol, Euler, OpenSymbol) reads by its characters, as an OpenType math font does; its big operators, sized delimiters, and pieces stay unread, and a formula with one fails its check. STIX's five size fonts (MathJax's, in OpenStax's books) read by a table of their own, each glyph's box from the font's outline. MathDesign's math fonts and BLEX keep TeX's codes and read as the families they copy. MathTime Pro 2's math italic draws its upright Δ at 0x31, which the PDF's Unicode map leaves out: a glyph there wider than a digit reads Δ. TeX's text fonts under other names (Latin Modern, cm-super, MathDesign's OT1 fonts) read as CMR where most of a font's glyphs draw OT1's characters at OT1's codes. A symbol font with no Unicode map (Symbol, Wingdings, ZapfDingbats, MarVoSym, Advent's math symbols) reads by its codes: a character pdf.js read as its code's letter or as a private-use character is the symbol the font draws at that code (Wingdings' "Ø" is ➢, Symbol's U+F061 is α); a character its own map read stays. A letter Word's text layer doubles ("𝑝𝑝") reads once. A text item whose font the page never draws at its place reads as the run of drawn glyphs no item took that starts at its origin, in one font on its baseline, when that run spells its letters: its words, width, and font (the Earth Observer's masthead read "The Earth O b server"). A font's name gives its flags, short names too (LinLibertineTB is bold; Adobe's Bd, Blk, It, and Obl: HelveticaNeueLTStd-BdIt is bold and italic): bold (so does text drawn filled and stroked, Google Docs' fake bold), italic, monospace, small caps, and its math family.
3. **The look** (`look.ts`, `faces.ts`). What the drawing shows beyond the fonts' flags: a rule under a run's baseline is an underline and a rule through its middle a strikethrough, when it starts and ends at word edges (a fraction's bar over its denominator is no underline); a filled box about a line tall behind a run is a highlight of the box's color (white is the page, a light gray a table's shading); a glyph's fill is its text color (black, near-black, a link's blue, and a color too light to read are none). Each item takes its face as the page editor names it: the font menu's face its name spells (TimesNewRomanPSMT is Times New Roman), the face a metric twin or a producer's name stands for (Helvetica is Arial), Computer Modern for TeX's fonts, else Times New Roman, Arial, or Courier New by the shape its name says (Libertine and Charis are serifs, Biolinum a sans), and by pdf.js's reading of its flags where the name says none (a CJK face is never monospace); a font cut from a face for one block of Unicode takes its parent's face, and a symbol font takes the face of the words around it; and its size, to a half point. An item that looks two ways is cut where its look changes. Every offset the parse emits (a formula's, a style's, a link's) falls between whole characters: a glyph whose text reads otherwise than its string takes one whole character. Lowercase letters a browser or Word draws as smaller capitals are small caps, and so are capitals one run draws at about 0.8 of its size ("PER CURIAM."). Each text block keeps the look most of its letters take (`ParsedBlock.font`: face, size, bold, italic, color), and a run in another face or size keeps its own. The body's look (`bodyFont`) is the one most letters of the body's paragraphs take (of every paragraph and list where the body's paragraphs hold under 400 characters: a slide, a résumé); the title keeps the look of its largest letters and its alignment (`titleFont`, `titleAlign`).
4. **Tables and graphics leave the text** (`ruled.ts`, `lattice.ts`, `tables.ts`, `figures.ts`). A table the page's rules draw is found before the column split: a grid of rules and filled boxes (two rows and two columns at least, words in two cells or more), or the band between horizontal rules of one width (booktabs). A grid gives the rows, columns, and merged cells: an empty sliver between two grid lines is no column or row, a fill in the page's own color is no cell, a box inside a box of its color is no cell unless it is as tall as that box and inset past a cell's padding (a cell of a shaded row: its sides are column lines), and two stacked grids whose column lines do not meet are two tables, but a grid its shading cuts apart stays one table where its words set more columns than its lines draw. A grid drawn open at one side (a column rule that most of the row rules it meets start at, three at least, the rules running on to one end where no rule closes them) closes at that end; a filled box's side is never an open side. A part of two stacked grids that draws one column line is no table of its own: its rows join the other part, a cell merged across (a form's fields beside a box). A grid the page does not show (no rule on it, every box filled white) is no table. A box to tick (an empty square, its four sides ruled, about the size of the words beside it, apart from other squares) reads ☐ where it stands, set as those words, and is no rule of a table and no mark of a chart; a square filled in a color is a legend key. A table between horizontal rules takes its rows and columns from its words, and a band of prose or a caption ends its region; a line alone at a band's foot, set larger than its other lines, is the heading its rule underlines, and a stack of rules one column of a table wide (a statement's underlines under each column of values) bounds no table. A drawn column rule is a column. A grid holds no picture but one a cell holds alone (a badge), and counts its filled cells by its words cut at its column lines. A mark alone (✔, ✘) is a value, not a tick label. A column rule drawn between two words of a line parts them into two cells. A table whose column rules run on, level, under its lowest rule ends where they end: that is its last row. A table that rules its rows (a rule under each, or column rules drawn row by row) in three bands or more reads each band as one row, each cell's lines joined, unless a column holds amounts on two lines of the band: then each such line opens a row. A line that stands across a rule sends the rows back to the lines' rhythm. A band ruled over and under, its column rules running from rule to rule, is one row when it holds two lines and no amounts. A grid's cells keep the room the page leaves over and under their words (`data-cell-padding`: top, right, bottom, and left, in points; the sides 5). A cell's words sit at its foot when the room over them is more than twice the room under and an em more. They sit in its middle when both rooms pass half an em and differ by a quarter at most (`vertical-align`). A column set flush right ends where most of its lines end, so a negative amount's parenthesis hangs past that edge. The head is the lines above the rule under the head, or with no such rule, the top lines with no words in the first column; the head's spans come from its partial rules and drawn cuts, and a lead-in over the first column (a sentence that ends in a colon) ends a grid's head. A value is never merged across a drawn rule. A cell merged across columns whose phrases each stand in one of those columns (none crosses a column line by half an em), two columns with words at least, is those columns' cells. A row whose cells with words each hold as many lines on the same baselines, one cell's lines all amounts, is that many rows. A word a column line crosses stays whole, in the cell that holds most of it; Chinese, set without spaces, is cut at the line. A cell's lines join as a paragraph's: a line-end hyphen is decided by the document's words. Lines each as far from both sides of their cell, one short of both paddings, are centered; so are lines that fill a cell of a row whose other cells are centered, two at least (a form's head row). A ruled row taller than its words (the lines' baselines and one line's height) by more than 0.6 of the text size keeps its height rule to rule, as the height of its first cell of one row (`<td style="height:24pt">`; a `<tr>` carries no attribute: `merge.ts` joins a table's rows across a page break by `<tr>`). A currency sign is its amount's. A lowercase line continues its row only when the row does not end a sentence and the line leaves a column empty. A lone box, a signature line, a chart's frame, and a slide's background are not tables, and a region with drawn column rules is no chart. Header cells are `<th>` and keep their bold; a cell keeps its words' styles, colors, and scripts, and a formula in it is read and checked as any other (step 6). The table html carries only style on table, col, td, th, p, and span, limited to font-size, width, height (a cell's, in points), background-color, color, text-align, margin-left, font-variant: small-caps, and a caption span's font-family (letters, digits, spaces, hyphens; 40 characters at most), each value tested before it is written. The page's graphics (images and vector drawings outside the tables) take the words drawn inside them and their caption, so a chart's ticks and legend never reach the text. Images that touch make one figure while their box holds no line of the page's text; lines in a photo grid's empty corner, set apart from the text, are its caption; any other group splits into the pieces that hold no text; a picture under the page's words is a background; a picture may fill up to 97% of the page. Word-sized shapes stacked just over or under a float's label ("TABLE 3.") are one drawing: a table drawn as glyph outlines. Rules with numbers in a row or a column along them (a line chart's ticks) are a drawing. A frame around a chart and its words, none of them prose, is one figure with its words (a panel). A graphic joins the nearest graphic in its row only, and never one with a label of its own. A caption beside a stack of pictures takes the row it is level with, and a caption beside a map takes the map. A caption's band widens to the graphics centered in it, and its column takes no body line level with the caption; two framed steps or more over a caption are its diagram's; the sweep up from a caption stops over a picture at the top. The cut over a figure takes out only the page's text, and a chart's axis labels are matched by the plot's width. A banner (a drawing across half the page, two lines tall) is a graphic. A path under 1.5 pt both ways is a dot of a drawing, never a rule. A line that opens with a figure's or a table's label is never a graphic's label or tick, and the graphic's crop stops at it. A number alone at the page's head or foot is no graphic's label, and a graphic's crop leaves out a line the page dropped at its edge. Lines under a caption become part of a figure only where something is drawn near them; with nothing drawn they stay text (a list of figures, a caption set under its chart). A caption in a side column, level with an uncaptioned graphic beside it and nothing drawn in its own column, is that graphic's. With no text over a caption in its column (under it, for a caption set over its figure), the figure ends at its own edge: its drawing, its graphics with their words, and the words it took in; it never takes in a line the page dropped (a running head, a running foot, a page number, a footnote). Above a caption, a chart's tick labels and a display's crop inside a diagram are the figure's: one crop. Under another figure, a figure ends at its own edge too, and a note ("Note: …") or a panel's caption at its foot is the caption's words, not the crop's.
5. **Lines and reading order** (`lines.ts`, `columns.ts`). The words near one baseline make a line; sub- and superscripts, accents, and big operators join their line, and so do a text-size operator's stacked limits; a label stacked over a relation goes to the relation's line, and a script (a glyph that touches the one it is set on) is never a label or a limit. A group of prose is a line of its own (an author line under a large title), and a glyph half again the size of the text beside it takes one line of that text, never two (the W-9's "W-9" beside its title's two lines); letter-spaced capitals collapse; a wide gap starts a new cell. A gap past 0.12 of the size (0.2 after a script) is a word space; small capitals set a letter at a time keep their word spaces, and the words of two baselines that one line took keep a space between them; a dotless ı under an acute is í. A drop cap (a paragraph's first letter set two lines tall or more, the lines beside it set in by its width) opens its paragraph's first line and joins the first word with no space, unless a word space stands between them: 0.3 to 0.7 em, where the lines beside it start at different places (a CSS float's first line is set in by the paragraph's indent, 1.2 em from the letter: "S" + "CIENTIFIC"). An OCR layer's words take the widths the scan shows, a word whose box runs into the next word's ends a space short of it, and a letter over four times the text size (a drawing read as a letter) drops. An OCR layer's words are never monospace: the layer's stock font is no face of the page (a scan set in Courier is no listing). Text in a vertical font on a page of horizontal text is no line of the text. Reading order is a recursive XY-cut: a region is cut at a gutter almost no text crosses, and each side is a region of its own (a band with one line a side reads whole when the band above it did and neither side is prose, so a table's last row stays in its table), so a two-column page reads column by column and a full-width heading, table, or figure reads where it stands. A side may be two columns of its own (a page of three). A line that starts at the right column's edge, where four lines or more start, opens that column's line however near the left column's line ends, when that line starts at the left column's edge (the Federal Register sets its columns an em apart). A region that is not two columns may have one band that is, or a column with a narrow side beside it: it reads as what stands above the band, the band's two sides (each side's lines at the text's leading), and what stands under it. The band's gutter is the x beside which one clear band (between the items within half an em of it) holds the most words on both sides. Two sides read as columns when both are prose columns, or when one is and the other is a column of text and display equations whose lines stand on the first's baselines less than half the time (a table's rows share theirs); a side spaced a line a paragraph is a column under the same test. A band of two columns ends where its shorter side ends, before a heading set under it. A narrow side (a seventh of the band's characters or fewer, set in another size or in italic, an em or more from a prose column) reads beside the paragraph that holds each of its groups' tops: after it on the right (a pull quote), before it on the left (a sidebar, a column of labels). Blocks such a band's gutter finds stand abreast and hold words on both sides, and are no figure's labels over its caption. A ruled table across four fifths of a region cuts it: what stands above and under it read as regions of their own, in the region's column. A rule the page draws across a gutter, in the white between two lines, outside a table (two ems past the gutter on each side, a fifth of the region wide or more), ends a band as a row across the gutter does: what stands over it reads before what stands under it, each band column by column (an appendix over its references, each set in two columns; a slide's rules between its parts). A script raised beside a row across the gutter (set smaller, half the row's size over its baseline at most) stands in the band under the row. Two sides of a gutter three ems wide or more whose lines share a baseline once at most are no table's columns: they read one after the other in the region's column (a form's header boxes, a form's foot). A table comes back as one line at its place in the order; a graphic takes its place as a block.
6. **Inline math** (`math/zones.ts`, `math/layout.ts`, `math/check.ts`, `math/display.ts`). On a page set in math fonts (TeX's, or a font set in Unicode that step 2 reads as TeX's), the fonts say which glyphs are a formula (a zone); every glyph of KaTeX's fonts and of an OpenType math font is math, its digits and roman letters too. A zone's glyphs and rules become LaTeX from their positions and sizes: fractions from their bars, scripts from their raise and size, radicals (a sign KaTeX draws as a path too), big operators and operator names ("lim", "max") with their limits (each takes the stretch of a run of small glyphs centered on it, so two side by side keep their own), delimiters built from pieces, matrices and cases by alignment, an array with its column rules and `\hline` (a grid framed by rules above and below is a table's), and a label stacked over a relation as `\overset`. A text font's letter in a formula is `\mathrm`, `\mathbf`, or a math letter as its font is, and a word set apart in a text italic is `\textit`; on a page whose math fonts set no Latin letter, the text's italic letters join the math beside them, and on one whose math fonts set no digit, the text's digits do. A page with no glyph of a math font has no formula for them to join. Where a page's math takes the text's italic letters, a lone italic letter between upright words is a formula of its own and one in an italic phrase ("E. coli") a word; such a letter up to 0.6 em from a relation is the relation's, and small italic letters set tight after a formula are its script (`k_{sp}`). A citation after a formula ("γ > 1 [57]") is the sentence's. Two operands set a word space apart keep the space (`\ `): the gap, less its left glyph's italic correction and a twentieth of an em for each script level of that glyph, is over a fifth of an em, so two formulas a space apart never read as a product. A bullet a math font draws at a line's start, and a bold number before a word space, are the list item's, not the formula's; a run whose only math is a URL's slashes is no formula; a set with a few words inside its braces reads as formulas around the words. Where a line's text is set in a font of its own (Times), not TeX's, a letter of TeX's bold or sans is a formula's (`\mathbf{g}`, `\mathsf{G}_1`), and so is a cell of such a letter and its scripts alone (a table's "G₁₃"). The rows of a structure set inside a sentence (a matrix, cases, an aligned pair, fractions side by side) and a label under or over a brace join the sentence's formula, laid out and checked with it. So do a fraction's parts that stand off the line where a bar on it spans them, and a fraction's parts set in the text's font. A bar that runs under a sixth of an em past the glyphs over and under it is their fraction's (LibreOffice). A fraction at the size of the glyphs beside its bar is `\tfrac` in a script (MathJax), and the check takes a script's script as its script's level where no glyph is under three quarters of the text's size; in cases and matrices a fraction at the text's size is `\dfrac`. Columns split at 0.9 em or 9.5 pt, whichever is less; a grid of four columns or more with no delimiters is a matrix; rows align at their first relation's left edge or center; bars pair as |…| on one row only; a radical drawn in strokes is one sign. A limit's letter keeps its script's script ("𝒞_{IPC_{0:k−1}}"); "arg min" and "arg max" are one name (`\argmin`); a limit's delimiters and a word in the text's size are the limit's; a formula's size and baseline come from its symbols, delimiters aside. Tall delimiters pair on their own row. A radical stands on its radicand's built baseline, and KaTeX's index stands over its sign's part. A fraction in a script, its bar shorter than 0.6 em, measures its parts' gaps at their size. Integrals in a display with limits over and under them are `\int\limits`; a display-size operator in a text-size row is `{\textstyle …}`. A tall bar alone keeps its drawn size (`\bigg|_{a=0}`). A text italic word a word space from a math letter is text. A set of names in the text's font is one formula, the names its `\text` ("E = {HHH,HHT,HTT}"). The glyph check draws the LaTeX with KaTeX and compares it with the glyphs: the same symbols, each at its script level, nothing lost or added (a radical sign no vinculum took is lost); no other glyph of the page inside the formula, where a brace's label stands, or starting just past its end at a script's height; and no shape drawn inside it left unread (KaTeX draws an accent, a brace, or a tall delimiter as a picture: the formula fails rather than guess it). A picture one formula read is no other formula's (a determinant's closing bar, drawn as a picture, opened the formula after it). The glyph check reads a formula's script levels from its own sizes: of two script sizes the larger is a script's and the smaller its script's, whatever their share of the text's (IEEE Access sets 10, 7.6, and 6 pt); a bracket counts at any level (a limit's terms stand in parentheses at the text's size); a word in the text's size on the baseline of a script's letter beside it is that script's (`\mbox{odd}` under a ∑); a tall bar is a sized delimiter. The structure check then places each symbol: KaTeX's HTML tree gives where it draws each (its fonts' boxes, no browser), the page where each glyph stands; mapped onto each other by the symbols each side has once, every glyph must be matched to a symbol of its character on its row (0.7 em up or down at most). A formula built wrong with every symbol present fails (two cases rows whose fractions interleave), and a display that fails is a crop. A formula that passes is a math span (`ParsedBlock.math`); one that fails keeps its readable characters, its scripts as sub and sup.
7. **Page furniture** (`furniture.ts`). Running heads, running feet, and page numbers drop before anything is segmented. Each page's first and last rows are the candidates, and a candidate drops only on evidence from other pages: the same words at the same place (digits aside, and in any order: facing pages may set a head's parts in mirror order), a number that counts with the pages ("7", "53.", "Page 7 of 30", roman numerals), the place, size, and weight of furniture found on other pages (a row's size is the size most of its characters take), or a cell that reads as a row of furniture dropped on other pages, or, in a row of two cells or more, as one cell of such a row (a form's name beside its catalog number). Odd and even pages may set different heads. A page whose only words besides its number say it was left blank drops them. On a scanned page, a printer's mark at the foot drops: three words and 16 characters at most, under the text, a job number ("13061°—22") or words of four letters or more found on no other line of the document. On a scanned page, a row of 8 characters or fewer with no letter or digit at the head or the foot (a speck of the paper's edge read as "'" or "—") drops before the candidates are read, so the running head and the page number are the page's first and last rows. A parse of fewer than six chosen pages reads the nearest pages too, for this evidence alone.
8. **Footnotes** (`footnotes.ts`) leave the page's lines here and come back after the block that cites them (§2 Parsing). A footnote's mark is a raised label, a note symbol (*, †, ‡, §, ¶, ‖, then doubled; ⋆ and ∥ too) set level after a word or after a CJK character and a gap, or a symbol TeX sets in a math font after an affiliation's raised number; a mark read as a formula is words again once its footnote links to it. A scan's text layer raises no mark: its footnotes are the lines after the column's last wide gap, each opening with a number that counts on from the one before, read as a scan misreads it ("I I." for 11). Small lines with no label over the first labeled footnote at a page's foot are footnotes too: on the first page the title's (IEEE sets them unmarked, one at each first-line indent), on a later page the end of the footnote before. On the first page, a column's last small lines, no more than three lines apart, are the title's footnotes when one opens with a label the page raises or the first reads as a subject classification, keywords, a date, a funding line, or IEEE's editor note ("The associate editor coordinating the review…") (amsart, acmart, IEEE); a labeled line or a line after a gap opens each. With no label and no such line, the title's notes are the page's foot: lines set smaller than the page's prose under the other columns' words, more than a line and a half under the words above, and wider than their column (Nature's affiliations and e-mail line; Elsevier's e-mail, DOI, dates, and license lines). An abstract is never a note. A note with no label ends at a line set in, or after a line that stops short on a sentence's end; a line that opens with ✉ opens one. A note with no mark stays unmarked: on the title's page it stands at the front matter's end, before the body's first heading (an abstract, an author summary, and keywords are the front matter's own), else after the page's last words. A footnote line's size is the size most of its letters take: a symbol set larger leaves the line at its words' size (Springer's ✉ at 11.6 pt before an 8 pt contact line). Lines under a short rule inside a column that open with a note symbol only other pages raise are endnotes (REVTeX's author notes at the bibliography's head).
9. **Segmentation** (`segment.ts`, `headings.ts`, `lists.ts`, `markers.ts`, `paragraphs.ts`, `contents.ts`, `math/display.ts`). A page's lines become blocks; the readers are tried in order: contents lists, tables, code listings, label lines, an algorithm's lines, headings, references, lists, paragraphs. A contents label set larger than the body or bold is a heading; an entry is a numbered title or a title with its page number in a cell of its own ("Glossary  88"); an unnumbered line under an entry with no page number is its wrap. A text table is two columns at least, not a prose column beside another, not a two-line display, and it never grows over a figure's caption. A line whose cells after the first hold no letter or digit, and whose first cell goes on the sentence of the line above it, is prose beside a form's boxes, no table row. A line that opens with a bullet is a list's item, never a cell's wrapped line, and a run with no gutter down it, over lines of one cell, goes back to the other readers. A line of prose is one cell, whatever its gaps. Two signs mark it: a cell that opens with a mark the words before it take (", where"), or words spaced over 0.6 em apart, four spaces or more. A short line of one cell whose short words stand an em apart, with a number after them, is a row of those parts; a list's item and a numbered heading are not. Lines closer than 0.7 of their size, one of them three characters or fewer, are a formula's stacked parts, not a table. A line of short cells right over a run's columns is its head row. A line in the first column starts at the table's left edge, or is a row of numbers, as many cells as a row at the edge, that starts left of the first gutter. A group's label is a phrase at the left edge, short of a sentence, with at most a short value after it. It closes no gutter where the rows alone part more columns than all the lines do. Rows that fill every column, three or more and four fifths of the rows, are a table however short their cells. Prose side by side is not a table. It reads as one paragraph, column by column, with a line break between columns. Three cases count as prose side by side: a run whose first column runs on from line to line, with a sentence among its cells; a run whose later column carries on the bullet or the sentence right over it (that column reads first, and the other columns' lines, a picture's labels, follow one line each); and text boxes whose lines interleave (most lines closer than 0.7 of their size to the line before, their words beside its words). A bold row in a color under a head in the page's ink belongs to the body. The lines over a text table's first line at its left edge are heads, and its columns come from the lines under them, so a head set over several columns closes no gutter; a lead-in (a sentence that ends in a colon) or a title over the table is no head; a statement's group name, and a label wrapped over its values, open a row. A heading is set larger than the body, or bold or in small caps at the body's size, or numbered ("3.1 Results", "II. RELATED WORK", "第2章"), or a run-in lead ("3.2.1. Two examples.", "1) Implementation:"), or a division's label alone on its line ("EXHIBIT 3", "SCHEDULE 1", "ARTICLE IV"), or a statement's title in capitals, centered between two centered lines over its table; a numbered question or sentence (an exercise) is no heading, and neither is a line of panel letters ("(b)"), a line with no letter, a numbered line with a sentence's end inside it (amsart's "2.1. Case of imaginary zj's. If n = 1 then"), a bold label closed by a period on a line not set in capitals (amsthm's "Theorem 1." and a paragraph's bold lead: a heading's label takes a colon, or its line is set in capitals: "F. DESPOT"), or, on a scan's text layer, a line with no word of two letters. A numbered heading may close with a period when its title is set bold or italic (amsart's "3.2. Proofs of …"). A page's first line set two ems in over regular text is apart above. A line set as far in from its column's right edge as from its left, half an em or more, is centered, and so is a heading at the middle of two or more centered headings of its size (a run-in head never). An italic line alone at the body's size, apart above and below, over regular text at its left edge, on a page whose headings are bold, is a subhead a level under them (never on a TeX page: a theorem's italic words). A line that opens with a dash signs a piece: no heading. Past 200 characters, lines set a little larger than the body that end their column are a paragraph the page break cut. A capital alone before a title numbers it only beside a heading with the letter before or after it. A run-in head keeps the page's indent (`ParsedBlock.indent`, the first line's, to the point). A run-in lead makes a run-in heading, which carries the token `run-in` (`<hN class="run-in">`, after the align token when it has one); the page editor draws it at the start of the paragraph after it (§30). A heading set large has most of its letters large, and so does the title (the W-9's "Form W-9", "Form" at 7 pt before "W-9" at 24 pt, is neither).
