Its level, 1 to 6, comes from its number and its size (a paper's unnumbered parts, "Abstract" and "References", stand at its sections' level; a form's parts that open the document's headings ("Part I Taxpayer Identification Number (TIN)") stand at the top level; the sizes over the first numbered size stand over that size's highest numbered heading; at one size an italic heading stands a level under the bold ones; a number met before votes for no size's level; a size takes the level more than half of its numbered headings take, and where they are fewer than half of its headings, never above a larger size's; over the first numbered size, each size a heading takes stands a level over the next where the levels leave room, else a size takes its rank under the biggest, two levels under it at most; under the numbered sizes, a size no numbered heading takes stands a level under the next larger size; a size one heading alone takes, the biggest aside, ranks no size under it; the title counts in no numbering; on a scan, each smaller size takes the level under the nearest numbered one, or h3 at most; a division's label stands at its kind's level, an exhibit's above an article's, and the title under it one level deeper), and a slide deck ranks each slide's heading sizes apart (a slide's biggest heading is h2). A heading wraps onto lines of its size and look: centered lines under a centered line; else lines at its left edge under a line that fills its column (the Federal Register's italic "C. What is EPA's authority for taking" over "this action?"); a bold heading goes past three lines only while each line fills its column, and a numbered heading's wrap may hang under the first word after its number. A wrapped heading is one line of text, but a centered heading keeps its lines where the writer broke them. A roman section may be bold ("I. Executive Summary"), and an "I.", "V.", or "X." set as the lettered headings are and as no roman one is, is a letter. A numbered section right over its first subsection is a heading, and so is a line set bold or in small caps with a gap above it, right over a list's first item (OpenStax's "Solution 6.2"). The title stands on the title page: the first page with words that is no library's notice ("Historic, archived document…"). It is the first heading there set larger than the body, half or more of its characters letters (a report's number is no title), and at 97% of the biggest there or more; a publication's name that opens the running heads of the pages after it yields to a longer heading within a tenth of its size (a journal's "Applied Energy" over its paper's title); on a scan, half of its words stand elsewhere in the document. Else it is a centered heading that opens the page over prose (amsart sets its title at the body's size), else a heading of ten words or more before the page's prose (the Federal Register's subject). A deck's is its title slide's (one of its first three slides from the title page, its title a fifth larger than every heading of the other two). The document's title is that heading's words on one line. Before the title, on its page or a notice's, a heading is a paragraph (a masthead's lines, a report's number, a rule's agency and docket lines). The masthead over the title: a line the running heads repeat drops, whole or as the first cell of a line of cells (PLOS's journal name, set beside the paper's title in its running heads; Nature's "Article" and DOI, Elsevier's journal line); short lines over the title, four blocks at most, are its kicker (`<p class="kicker">`, set over the Title: a journal's name, "RESEARCH ARTICLE", IEEE's "Received …" and DOI), and a rule between them drops (more blocks are the page's own words: the Federal Register's p. 1); lines beside the title, stacked at one left edge with no paragraph gap, are one paragraph, a line apiece (the W-9's form number, revision, and agency); a side column set small under the title's foot that runs down the page goes to the front matter's end (PLOS's citation, editor, and dates). A heading right under a centered title, centered, smaller, and not bold, is its subtitle: a paragraph. The document's first line in two cells far apart is two paragraphs, the second flush right ("一般論文" | "Peer-Reviewed"). A heading between the title and the abstract (an author, a place) is a paragraph, and so are the headings under a title slide's title; a paper in two languages sets its title again over its second abstract ("摘要", then "Abstract"): that title stays a heading, and the headings between it and its abstract are paragraphs. Four lines or more set large at one left edge, each ending short while the next line's first word would fit, are lines of their own, not a heading (an author list set large); the longest line says nothing of its own end. A title page's grid of authors (stacks of three or more short centered lines side by side, tops level) reads each stack as one centered paragraph, in reading order where the grid starts (an ACM paper's authors). A line of one to four note symbols or of a one- or two-character label, two thirds of the size of the line under it or less, raised over that line's end within an em, is that line's raised mark (a Chinese paper's author "许 为" and its "*"). A list item opens with a marker read by family and value (a bullet, a box, "1.", "(a)", "(iv)", "1.1", "A-1.", "[12]"), or beside a bullet the page draws (a small path, fill, or shading left of its first word, never before a caption's label), so a list's items follow one another; its depth is its indent. A bullet keeps its printed glyph ("–", "◦", "▪"), and a drawn bullet reads "•"; a list a drawn bullet opens needs two items, and lines set in with no marker are a list only where the page draws their bullets, else paragraphs set in as printed. A legal marker closed by a period over a title set italic or bold and closed by a period is a run-in head, no item; a number opens an item only where its words stand near the body's size; a list spaced wider than its lines' pitch goes on while its items stand as far apart as before. A lone line that opens with "*" is no list item (the asterisk is a note symbol), and neither is a line whose marker is words of the line above (a number that closes a parenthesis the line above opened; a marker at the item's words under a line that ran to the column's edge mid-sentence); one-line labels that end in a colon, with no marker, are a form's fields, each a paragraph. An algorithm's lines under its caption ("Algorithm 1", the label bold or the line set apart) are a list with no marker, each line an item at the depth its indent reads, and so are the entries under a references heading that the page sets with no marker and a hanging indent (the references heading in Chinese, Japanese, or Korean too; entries hang up to four ems). A list keeps where the page sets each depth (`ParsedBlock.listIndents`, in points from its column's edge, which is where a line just above or below the list starts when that is further left, and never right of its markers: an item's marker at left + first, its wrapped lines at left, and, where the wraps come back under the markers, hang, the words' place after the marker), the space the page leaves between two items beyond the line pitch (`ParsedBlock.itemSpace`), and "justify" where its items are set justified. A paragraph is continuous lines in one column; its layout tokens (§6) say what the page shows: centered (in the column the line was read in, whose middle is the one three lines or more of the page share where no line reaches the column's edge, or a title's lines on one another; a line of prose at the body's size that runs to the column's edge is no centered line), flush right (lines that end at one place; short lines, half the column or less, may start a few points apart), justified (every line but the last fills the column, on a page set justified; a formula's line and the line before it may stop short; the column's edge is where two of its full lines end, and a line that runs past it by an em at most, TeX's overfull line, fills it; a paragraph at the page's foot is measured against its column's full lines over the page), a caption, a quote, a first-line indent, a hanging indent, a block indent. Lines that start together and end apart are never centered (a box's paragraph set in by as much on each side); lines that start apart, each set in two ems or more from both edges of its column with its middle within 0.75 em of the column's, are centered (an author list's lines, their raised marks setting one off-center). A line the column split cut into cells at a gap of 2.5 ems or less is one run of words for its alignment. A page is set justified when, of its lines of prose that end within three ems of their column's edge, three in five end at it (of four lines or more) or every one does (of three or more); a page with fewer such lines is set as the last page that told (a report's pages of tables with a paragraph or two). A column of one line shows no edge (a foot line alone read as a wrapped paragraph's end). An indent keeps its size in points (`ParsedBlock.indent`), measured from its column's edge: the leftmost place that two of its column's lines start at, and a third as many as at the busiest place (lines of one cell read in its column that overlap it, from 85% of its size to half as large again), not the column split's extent, which reaches past that edge where anything stands in the margin. A block indent is a quarter of the column's width at most and leaves its widest line room. One line alone reads as a first-line indent where it is set in by the page's own first-line indent, or runs to the column's edge over a line back at the edge (a paragraph's first line read alone), and as a block indent where it is set in otherwise. A line set in by the page's first-line indent opens a paragraph, under a full line too; the paragraph after a run-in lead starts at the column's edge. On a page set justified, a line after a sentence's end that stops short of the edge its block's lines reach ends its paragraph when the next line opens a sentence, though the next line's first word would not have fit: there only a paragraph's last line stops short (a box's paragraph ends its lines at the box's edge). An OCR layer's line ends are too rough to tell. A line set in by up to 5 em over a line back at the column's edge is a paragraph's first line when it wrapped: it runs to the column's edge, or the next line's first word would not have fit after it (Word's half-inch indent, 3.6 em at 10 pt). A line set in that runs to the column's edge as its column's last line is a paragraph's first line cut by a page or a column break: a first-line indent. A block set in on both sides by one amount (an abstract, a quotation) has its own edges: its first line, set in further, runs to its right edge, and it is justified where its wrapped lines end together there (three of them, or two on a page set justified). A marker that opens its sequence ("(a)") starts an item under a full line too when a line a few under it, at its place, goes on the sequence ("(b)"); where no full line tells the column's edge, an exercise's number alone on its line keeps its items. A mark set apart after a line's words (a box's dash) ends no sentence. A number that opens no list, under a line that stops mid-sentence, is words of the paragraph ("40 CFR part" | "178. To ensure …"). A page set double-spaced (most of its pairs of long lines two sizes apart or more) reads at its own leading. A paragraph whose words, four in five of them, stand in a paragraph of its page over twice as wide is a pull quote: a quote. A rule the page draws between two lines of text outside a table is a separator (a horizontal line before the lower line's block) when it spans half their width and ten ems at least, with room over and under it, the lines beside it at the body's size or larger; between short lines or small words (a chart's axis between its labels) it is one only when it runs across the whole column and boxes no smaller type with a rule of its extent on the small lines' other side (a listing's frame). It is none over the page's notes (every line under it, across its extent, set smaller than the line over it), none where another rule across half of it stands within three lines (a float's frame; a ruled table's own rules, a heading's side marks, and a double rule's second stroke frame nothing), and none where a rule drawn down from its end meets it at a corner (a box's edge: the frame around a story and its photos). Each text block keeps the space the page leaves under it (`ParsedBlock.spaceAfter`), and so does a table, from its bottom edge to the next text block's first line (the page editor does not draw a table's yet). Between two blocks of text it is their baselines' step beyond the step two lines take with nothing between them: the upper line's part under its baseline (a fifth of its line) and the lower line's part over it, each at its own size, at the pitch per point of the upper paragraph, else of the lower, else of the page (a heading's or a list's lines give no pitch). A paragraph's pitch is its smallest baseline step that another step matches (within 0.1 pt or 1%) or that stands within 3% of the page's leading, else a lone step under the page's leading (an abstract set tighter than the text): a page pushes lines apart (TeX's lineskip under a tall formula), never closer. A step between lines that tall glyphs push apart, or under half the lines' size, is no pitch; an OCR layer's pitch is the median of its steps. A paragraph of two lines or more keeps its line spacing (`ParsedBlock.lineSpacing`): its pitch over its size over single spacing's 1.15, to a hundredth. A block's lines are measured at their baselines, their scripts left out (`lineBox`: 0.85 of a line's size over its baseline and 0.3 under); where a line's glyphs reach more than two sizes under its baseline (a matrix's rows read as one line of text, its words read last), the space after its block runs from its lowest glyphs. Over and under a display equation the space runs to its glyphs' box (`glyphBox`: each glyph's box by its font's metrics, the rules, a radical's sign). None where a figure, a table, or the page's end follows, and none where a gap over three lines of the page's text is no blank the page leaves: another block, a picture, a shape, or a rule reaches into the band between them, anywhere across the page (a background or a frame around the upper block aside); the lower block opens the notes at the page's foot (set smaller than the body, with no block at the body's size under it); the upper block is small print right after a table or a figure (its notes: the notes reader took the rest of a table's notes from the band); or the page is a scan. A paper's author line and abstract, set smaller than its text, keep the blank the page leaves between them. Two parts of a line outside a table and a display read with a tab where the page sets them apart: a gap of 1.5 em or more between words (not beside a formula's glyphs; on a line of three gaps or more whose middle gap is 0.8 em or more, a justified line with few words, only a gap 2.5 times that), not on a centered line, and not a gap between prose on both sides of a line that runs to its column's edge (TeX's \qquad) unless what stands before it is a short lead (a letter, a label). The tab's stop (`ParsedBlock.tabStops`, in points from the column's edge the indents are measured from, or from the line's start when it stands left of that edge) is where the words after it start; where they are the line's last part, short, and end at the column's right edge (the furthest place two of its full lines end together), it is a right stop at that edge (a pair of boxes set flush right). A proof's box (□ ■ ∎) set at the column's right edge after the last words, on their line or on a line of its own, takes a tab and a right stop at the edge, measured from the left edge of the block it closes. A thin rule (1.6 pt at most) outside a table, meeting no other rule and no drawn shape (an arrowhead, a diagram's box, a chart's bar), is a fill-in rule where it is a blank of a line: at the line's baseline, in one gap of its words (a rule that runs under a word underlines it), inside the line's column, with no formula beside it and no words right under it or beside it on another line. A blank is an underlined tab to the rule's end (a word space before it where the page leaves one, a tab to it where it stands further off). Rules on a line of their own are a paragraph of underlined tabs (a tab between two rules set apart) after the block over them, between the page's lines and inside their column, on a page that reads as a form: a line of it has a blank, two rules have a label set under them (one phrase from the rule's start, no larger than the page's words, with room over the rule to sign in), or two rules of one length stand a line apart with nothing between them. A page set in TeX's math fonts has no fill-in rules (a fraction's or a radical's bar). A fill-in rule is never a separator. On a page set in math fonts (step 6) a display equation's lines (a fraction's numerator and denominator, a sum's limits, even under "=∑" set as one item, a matrix's rows, a brace's label, the label, and a row close under or over the display within the width of all its lines) first join into one line, rows inside one pair of tall delimiters are one display, and a row that opens with a relation or an operator ("= …", "+ …") joins the display above when the two read as one formula or neither reads alone; a line of scripts under an operator name ("lim", "max") is its limits, on the page's last line too. A big operator taller than a line and a half (an integral with limits of two levels) makes its line a display's row; its limits' lines and the rows around it join, a line centered under or over it with a word or two is its limit, and a limit of an operator in a later row stays with that row. A line that opens with its formula keeps "for", "if" as the display's words; one that opens with a word, holds a citation, or starts a sentence is prose. A marker a formula opens with ("(Mu)") is no list item's. A display too wide to center starts at the column's edge: all math, most of the column wide, with the display's space over and under it. A label on one row of a display of several rows is its label, whatever the other rows reach. A glyph no item reads (a composite's second half) is a display's only where no other line reads a glyph at its origin: the arrowhead of a ↦ in the sentence over a display is the sentence's. A row whose first relation stands under the first relation of the display above joins it when the two read as one formula; a label on a row of its own under a display's end is its label; cases take no cases set after them on the line; a tall delimiter whose group stacks nothing holds no group around it back; a formula's name in capitals is no prose; a right-column line is measured from that column's edge. The display is an EQUATION block of its LaTeX, the label as `\tag{1.2}` (a label at the left margin adds the `leqno` token) and a proof's end square as `\tag*{$\square$}`, when it passes the glyph check, which a display also fails when a glyph of TeX's fonts stands within an em past its right edge on its rows, a brace piece it did not read stands within an em over or under it, a glyph stands where a limit of its big operator or of an operator name would, or an unread glyph of a math font hangs over it; else it is a FIGURE, the crop of the page (§16), marked `mathCrop`. Rows all in math that touch a display the check failed (a matrix's lower rows) join its crop. On any other page a display is found by its share of math glyphs and stays a crop.
10. **Joins** (`merge.ts`, `text.ts`, `tables.ts`). A paragraph cut by a page or column break joins its other half, on one page too, past a float set between the halves and the small lines its figure left: a float atop the next column or page follows the paragraph, and a float, a pull quote, or a paragraph set smaller than the page's text at the foot of the first page (a license line, a side caption with no label, notes the notes reader missed) comes before it; pages that hold only floats keep the halves apart, and so does a float a join already set after the part on its page (a list over three pages, a table atop the middle one): after the next part it would stand past that page's start (an import's page starts only rise); a caption never goes on over a page, and a display's crop stays where the sentence puts it. A list joins its items, a table its rows, and the block keeps where each later page begins. A part that ends in an abbreviation ("et al.", "e.g.", "Fig.") ends no sentence; the next part goes on the sentence when it opens lowercase or with a parenthesis, or with a number that closes a parenthesis the first left open or follows a word that takes one ("part", "section", "page"). A lone line set in at a page's foot goes on into the next page's part that opens lowercase, and a list's last item into a part that opens lowercase higher in the next column. No paragraph goes on into a caption, and a caption goes on only into its own next line right under it (a table's caption whose first line is set larger). Two parts join over a page only when each is set alike against its page's text, within a fifth (a caption's last line, or a note set smaller, goes on into no paragraph of the next page); a page's text is the size most of the document's paragraphs are set in where the page sets a tenth of its text in it, else the page's own (an OCR layer sizes each page from its scan). A paragraph a join carried over the break goes on into a paragraph after it on the next page only as the joins on one page say. A paragraph whose last line runs to the column's edge on a page set justified goes on over a page or a column break into a part that opens at the column's edge on a page whose paragraphs open set in: its sentence ended at the line's end ("…in the context of SMT solving." | "Their tool OLSQ2 …"). A joined block takes the space under its later half; a list keeps its first half's depths, space between items, and justified items, and takes the later half's where the first shows none. Lines that open a page at the hanging indent of the entries under them end the entry the page break cut (a reference's last words). On one page, a second part that opens with no lowercase word, set at half the first's size or less, stays apart (a keywords line, a license line at the page's foot); two links on lines of their own stay two paragraphs. On one page, a part that opens with a sub-figure's or a sub-table's label ("(d) Ablation …") or a pull quote goes on no paragraph, and neither does a part set smaller, a line's size or more under the first (a note at the page's foot). A pull quote set between a paragraph's halves stands after the paragraph, as a float does. In Chinese and Japanese, a part whose full last line ends with no stop goes on into a part that opens with a character where its lines start or in the next column (a line alone is full where it ends where the next part's lines end), and the parts join with no space. A hyphen at a line's end is kept or dropped by the document's own words first: the compound written whole elsewhere keeps it, the word written whole elsewhere drops it, and the first word of a line that opens lowercase counts as no word (it may finish a word cut on another line). Then by the page editor's English dictionary (`public/spelling`): a word it knows drops the hyphen ("be-" | "cause"), and two lowercase words it knows that make no word it knows keep it ("cross-" | "entropy"), unless the first is a prefix ("sub-", "micro-") or the second a suffix ("-ism", "-ly"); a name split in two ("Rout-" | "ledge") is no compound. `next.config.ts` traces `public/spelling` into the API functions. In prose, a line that stops mid-sentence wraps into a line that opens lowercase, unless its room to the paragraph's column edge (a paragraph set flush left) would have taken the next word with three ems to spare and it ends in no hyphen (a verse or a quotation pasted with its own line breaks keeps them); it wraps into a capital when its room would not have taken the next word with an em to spare. A URL at a line's end goes on into the next line's first word when its link's address holds the words on both sides, or, with no link, when it ends open (a slash, a hyphen) or the next word reads as the rest of a URL; never into an ordinary word or a new URL. A table's caption ("Table 2:", IEEE's "TABLE I" over a title, "表 3" and a title, the last line of the paragraph above, a link alone under the table) is its `<caption>`, with its marks, face, and size, on the side the page sets it: a caption under its table is `<caption style="caption-side: bottom">`. A caption between two tables goes to the side the document sets its captions: the side its captions take where a table stands on one side of them only. With no side, it goes to the nearer table. A sub-table's caption ("(a) …") joins its grid the same way, on a page where a table's caption joined. A "表 N" followed by a particle (に, の, で …) is a sentence, not a caption. Footnotes come back, and contents entries link to their headings.

### The parse benchmark

`scripts/parse-bench` measures every change to the parser (`npx tsx scripts/parse-bench/run.mts`; its header lists the flags).

- **The corpus** (`corpus.json`): synthetic documents written as LaTeX, HTML, and Word files, each with its exact reference (`synthetic/`); arXiv papers, their references built from their LaTeX source by LaTeXML (`arxiv/`); pages of public documents with hand-made references (`refs/`); whole public documents read without a reference, every page (the entries marked sweep, which `--sweep` runs); and the owner's own files, kept in `.bench/` and never committed. The public PDFs span producers and genres: IEEE, ACM, Springer, Elsevier, Nature, and PLOS papers; a Word thesis with an SF 298 form; a Word report with Cambria Math; an SEC 10-K; the Federal Register in three columns; an OpenStax chapter; the W-9; a LibreOffice guide; PowerPoint and Google Slides decks; a résumé; an invoice; a newsletter; and two Japanese documents. A reference records each block's words with their styles and colors, its alignment and indent, and the font of each role; `look.py` adds what the reference's page measures: a paragraph's indent in points, the space under a paragraph or a list and between a list's items (beyond the step its two lines take, each at the size most of its characters take), a list's justified items, and the side of each equation number.
- **The scores** (`metrics.ts`), for the parse and for the import: against the reference, the words (recall, precision, order), furniture leaks, headings (F1 at their level), lists (items, depths, and each marker as the list draws it, by depth: a marker left in the words is none, but an item with no marker whose words open with the reference's marker, a label no level draws such as "[Bil95]", has it), paragraphs and their joins, table cells (a formula in a table cell is placed as a word is: in place when its cell holds it read alike, or the same characters), math (both sides drawn by KaTeX to MathML, then compared; a formula alone in a table cell is read from the cell the table's rows and columns map it to), footnotes, styles (bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, small caps, sub, sup, and a text color and a highlight color by their color), roles (the alignment of paragraphs and list items, indentation and its size, the space under a block and between a list's items, captions, checkboxes, separators, quotations, and equation labels and their side), and fonts per role (the body, the title, each heading level, captions, footnotes: the face's shape, the size, bold, and color). Without a reference (`free.ts`, `glyphs.ts`, `layout.ts`, `drawn.ts`): the words against pdftotext's (unscored where pdftotext reads under half as many words as the candidate: a CID font it cannot map; a letter Word's text layer doubles counts once; diagonal text is none; a symbol font's character counts as the page draws it, Symbol's letters as Greek and the other symbol fonts' letters as no word; a line inside a region the candidate shows as a figure is the figure's unless the candidate's words hold it; words pdftotext cannot read and pdf.js can count as the page's, and a table header Word repeats at a page's top is no word to cover), furniture found by its place on the pages (text set sideways at the page's edge is furniture that leaks nothing; a heading's own number is no page number; a running head, a running foot, or a page number a figure's crop shows at the page's edge leaks too), on a TeX page every glyph against the text (a garbled character, a formula left as a picture; a display read without its printed number; a delimiter or an arrow KaTeX draws as a picture stands for the page's; a delimiter stacked from pieces is one symbol; three periods in a row, a column, or a step down are `\ldots`, `\vdots`, `\ddots`; a display's crop counts half a pass; a display's letters on no more rows than the page's), the page's lines (no line read across a column's gutter; no sentence run on across a table's rows; no prose in a display's crop; no figure in two pieces; no picture no figure shows; no caption apart from its figure or table, and a table's caption on the side of its grid the page sets it; no crop over another; no Title mark the page lacks; no space after a block of 48 pt or more but where the page leaves it, down to the candidate's next block or figure; no grid of numbers read as prose; a run-in head at the page's indent; an end-of-proof box at the column's right edge; no paragraph set in where the page sets it flush, measured from where the column's other lines of the paragraph's width start), the body's face (serif, sans, or mono as the page's own), the page labels (none running backwards; an unnamed page reads "" as the page editor draws it), and the import's look (inline formulas at their words' size, PDF figures at their printed width, a Word file's paragraph borders against its own, the space around a display, a table's row height, a list marker inside its column, a number whole in its table cell, and a checklist's wrapped lines at the page's indent, as the page sets them; the page editor's rules read as the browser's cascade does: the most specific wins, an import's own rules over the page editor's). A title the parse takes from a later page is scored on the pages that hold its words; a page whose text layer is a scan's OCR alone scores no face styles; a clause list's numbered titles score alike as items or as headings over their clauses. An import whose rich text has half of a surrogate pair fails. An inline formula a saved run held on a page and the candidate lost counts as lost (`.bench/formulas-baseline.json`, written with the baseline).
- **The rule.** A parser change runs the benchmark against the baseline (`baseline.json`; `.bench/baseline-private.json` for the owner's files) and no document drops without a reason. Times are measured one run at a time. `check.mts` checks the metrics and adapters on small hand-built cases; `pair.mts` draws a PDF's page beside the same page of its import in the page editor.
